Fourier transforms and Abel-Jacobi theory
Younghan Bae, Sam Molcho, Aaron Pixton

Abstract
We connect Fourier transforms between compactified Jacobians over the moduli space of stable curves and logarithmic Abel-Jacobi theory. As an application, we compute the pushforward of monomials of divisors on compactified Jacobians in terms of the twisted double ramification cycle formula.



1
Introduction

1.1
Overview

For an
L^{2}
-function
f:\mathbb{T}^{g}:=\mathbb{R}^{g}/\mathbb{Z}^{g}\to\mathbb{C}
, the integral of
f
can be evaluated as the
0
-th Fourier coefficient

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{g}}f(x)dx=\widehat{f}(0)\,

of the Fourier transform
\widehat{f}(\mathsf{m}):=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{g}}f(x)e^{-2\pi i\mathsf{m}\cdot x}dx
,
\mathsf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{g}
.

An analogous statement holds for a family of abelian varieties. Let
\pi:A\to B
be a family of principally polarized abelian varieties with unit section
e
. Let
\mathfrak{F}:\mathsf{CH}^{*}(A,\mathbb{Q})\to\mathsf{CH}^{*}(A,\mathbb{Q})
be the Fourier-Mukai transform, given by the Poincaré line bundle. For a class
\alpha\in\mathsf{CH}^{*}(A,\mathbb{Q})
, we have

\pi_{*}(\alpha)=e^{*}\mathfrak{F}(\alpha)\,,

(1)

replacing integration with the pushforward
\pi_{*}
and replacing evaluation at
0
with the pullback
e^{*}
. Therefore, (
1
) provides a useful tool for studying the pushforward, if one knows enough about the Fourier transform – for example, its compatibility with the weight decomposition of
\mathsf{CH}^{*}(A,\mathbb{Q})
.

We study here the intersection theory of certain families of degenerating abelian schemes – the fine compactified Jacobians
\pi:\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\,\epsilon}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
over the moduli space of stable curves – using the Fourier transform. Two challenges arise: (i) the Poincaré line bundle does not extend to a line bundle on the product of the compactifications, and (ii) the relative group structure is lost. To address these issues, firstly, we construct a ‘minimal’ logarithmic modification of the product of two compactified Jacobians where the Poincaré line bundle admits a unique line bundle extension. This construction yields a logarithmic desingularization of Arinkin’s kernel
[
5
,
6
]
and provides the Fourier transform with a recursive structure. Secondly, we link the Fourier transform with logarithmic Abel-Jacobi theory. The resolved Abel-Jacobi section of Holmes-Molcho-Pandharipande-Pixton-Schmitt
[
33
]
is related to the universal double ramification (uniDR) formula of Bae-Holmes-Pandharipande-Schmitt-Schwarz
[
8
]
. We study the Fourier transform of the class of the resolved Abel-Jacobi section and show that the
\mathsf{uniDR}
formula and Fourier transform share the same genus recursive structure, enabling us to compute the Fourier transform effectively.

We establish formula (
1
) for compactified Jacobians under an appropriate normalization of Fourier kernel. By combining this formula with the calculation of the Fourier transform, we show that the pushforward of certain monomials of divisors can be expressed in terms of the twisted double ramification (DR) cycle formula
\mathsf{DR}^{c}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
introduced by Janda-Pixton-Pandharipande-Zvonkine
[
35
]
.
Our formula involves all codimensions
c\leq g
1
1

1

If the codimension
c
is greater than
g
, then
\mathsf{DR}^{c}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
vanishes by
[
19
]
.
and considers individual coefficients of the twisted
\mathsf{DR}
formula as a polynomial in
b\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathsf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Thus, we offer a geometric interpretation of
\mathsf{DR}^{c}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
in both contexts in terms of intersection theory on compactified Jacobians.

1.2
Compactified Jacobians

Let
p:\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{g,n}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
be the universal curve with
n\geq 1
. A
stability condition

\epsilon
of degree
d
for
p
assigns a rational number to every irreducible component of every stable curve
(C,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
of genus
g
with
n
marked points satisfying the following two conditions:

(a)

the sum of the values of
\epsilon
over the irreducible components of
C
equals
d
, and

(b)

\epsilon
is additive under all partial smoothings of the curve
C
.

A prestable curve
C^{\prime}
is
quasi-stable
if the relative dualizing
sheaf
\omega_{C^{\prime}}
is nef and chains of unstable components have length at most
1
. A line bundle
L
on
C^{\prime}
is
admissible
if
L
has degree
1
on each unstable component of
C^{\prime}
.

With respect to a stability condition
\epsilon
, there exists a stability inequality defining when an admissible line bundles on a quasi-stable curve is
\epsilon
-stable (resp.
\epsilon
-semistable). A stability condition
\epsilon
is
non-degenerate
if there are no strictly
\epsilon
-semistable admissible line bundles. A stability condition
\epsilon_{0}
of degree
0
is
small
if line bundles of multidegree
0
on underlying curves are
\epsilon_{0}
stable.

For a non-degenerate stability condition
\epsilon
of degree
d
for
p
, there exists a moduli space
\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
of
\epsilon
-stable admissible line bundles on quasi-stable curves over
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
(
[
37
]
). It is a proper, smooth Deligne-Mumford stack of dimension
4g-3+n
. In particular, for small stability condition
\epsilon_{0}
,
\overline{J}^{\epsilon_{0}}_{g,n}
contains the Jacobian of multidegree zero line bundles
J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n}
as an open substack.

The compactified Jacobian
\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
carries a universal family which can be used to define tautological classes on
\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
. There exists a universal quasi-stable curve with
n
sections,

p:\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{g,n}^{\text{qs}}\to\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\,,\hskip 5.69054ptx_{i}:\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\to\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{g,n}^{\text{qs}},\,i=1,\ldots,n\,,

with a universal admissible line bundle
\mathcal{L}
on
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{g,n}^{\text{qs}}
. We choose the universal admissible line bundle to be trivialized along the first marking.
For
1\leq i\leq n
, the
\xi
-class at the
i
th marked point is defined by
\xi_{i}:=x_{i}^{*}c_{1}(\mathcal{L})
and the
\kappa_{0,1}
class is defined by
\kappa_{0,1}=p_{*}(c_{1}(\omega_{p,\log})c_{1}(\mathcal{L}))
.
The
\Theta
-divisor is defined by
\Theta:=-\frac{1}{2}p_{*}(c_{1}(\mathcal{L})^{2})
.
The divisor classes
\xi_{i},\Theta
and
\kappa_{0,1}
span the rational Picard group
\mathrm{Pic}(\overline{J}^{\epsilon}_{g,n})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
modulo divisor classes pulled back from
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
(
[
27
]
).

1.3
Twisted double ramification cycle formula

Let
b\in\mathbb{Z}
be an integer and let
\mathsf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be a vector of integers satisfying

a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n}=(2g-2+n)b\,.

(2)

Let
r
be a positive integer. We denote by
\mathsf{DR}^{c,r}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
2
2

2

In the literature, this formula is denoted by
\mathsf{P}^{c,r,b}_{g}(\mathsf{a})
, while
\mathsf{DR}_{g}^{b}(\mathsf{a})
refers to the twisted double ramification cycle, which corresponds to the codimension
g
part of the double ramification cycle formula.
the codimension
c
component of the tautological class

\exp\left(-\frac{b^{2}}{2}\kappa_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}^{2}}{2}\psi_{i}\right)\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Gamma\in\mathsf{G}_{g,n}\\
w\in\mathsf{W}_{\Gamma,r,b}\end{subarray}}\frac{r^{-h^{1}(\Gamma)}}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)|}\;(j_{\Gamma})_{*}\Bigg[\prod_{e=(h,h^{\prime})\in E(\Gamma)}\frac{1-\exp\left(-\frac{w(h)w(h^{\prime})}{2}(\psi_{h}+\psi_{h^{\prime}})\right)}{\psi_{h}+\psi_{h^{\prime}}}\Bigg]

in
\mathsf{CH}^{c}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n},\mathbb{Q})
. For a detailed explanation of the notation, we refer to Section
6.1
.
For sufficiently large
r
,
\mathsf{DR}^{c,r}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
becomes polynomial in
r

[
35
]
. We denote by
\mathsf{DR}^{c}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
the value at
r=0
of the polynomial associated with
\mathsf{DR}^{c,r}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
.

The formula
\mathsf{DR}^{c}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
is a polynomial of degree
2c
in the variables
b,a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
(see
[
60
,
59
]
), well-defined only modulo the relation (
2
). If we rewrite
a_{1}
in terms of the other variables using (
2
), then we can treat it as a polynomial in the other variables alone, with no relations.
It then makes sense to take a specific coefficient of the polynomial, and we write

[\mathsf{DR}^{c}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})]_{b^{m}a_{2}^{k_{2}}\cdots a_{n}^{k_{n}}}

for the coefficient of the monomial
b^{m}a_{2}^{k_{2}}\cdots a_{n}^{k_{n}}
in
\mathsf{DR}^{c}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
in
\mathsf{CH}^{c}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n},\mathbb{Q})
.

1.4
Intersection theory on compactified Jacobians

For a nondegenerate stability condition
\epsilon
for the universal curve
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{g,n}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
of degree
d
, let
\pi:\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
be the compactified Jacobian. For the definition of the tautological classes of
\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
, we choose the universal line bundle which is trivialized along the first marking – so in particular,
\xi_{1}=0
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\pi:\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
be the compactified Jacobian for a non-degenerate stability condition
\epsilon
. Let
\ell,m,k_{2},\ldots,k_{n}
be non-negative integers.

(a)

If
2\ell+m+\sum_{i}k_{i}<2g
, then
\pi_{*}(\Theta^{\ell}\kappa_{0,1}^{m}\xi_{2}^{k_{2}}\cdots\xi_{n}^{k_{n}})=0
in
\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n},\mathbb{Q})
.

(b)

If
2\ell+m+\sum k_{i}=2g
, then

\pi_{*}\Big(\frac{\Theta^{\ell}}{\ell!}\cdot\frac{(-\kappa_{0,1})^{m}}{m!}\cdot\prod_{i=2}^{n}\frac{\xi_{i}^{k_{i}}}{k_{i}!}\Big)=(-1)^{g-\ell}[\mathsf{DR}_{g}^{g-\ell}(b;\mathsf{a})]_{b^{m}a_{2}^{k_{2}}\cdots a_{n}^{k_{n}}}\,.

In particular, the pushforward is independent of the choice of stability condition.

(c)

For any
\ell,m,k_{2},\ldots,k_{n}
, the pushforward
\pi_{*}(\Theta^{\ell}\kappa_{0,1}^{m}\xi_{2}^{k_{2}}\cdots\xi_{n}^{k_{n}})
lies in the tautological ring
\mathsf{R}^{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n})
.

When
\epsilon
is small, the number
2\ell+m+\sum_{i}k_{i}
is the weight of the class
\Theta^{\ell}\kappa_{0,1}^{m}\xi_{2}^{k_{2}}\cdots\xi_{n}^{k_{n}}
over the locus
\mathcal{M}^{\,\mathrm{ct}}_{g,n}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
where
\pi
is an abelian scheme. Passing to rational cohomology, Theorem
1.1
(a) can be obtained by the perverse filtration (Section
8.3
). The closed formula and the independence of stability condition in Theorem
1.1
(b) is not at all clear from previous results.

Theorem
1.1
(b) consists of the leading terms of the
\mathsf{DR}
cycle formula, viewed as a polynomial in
b,\mathsf{a}
. By Theorem
1.3
below, the full
\mathsf{DR}
cycle formula can be recovered from these leading terms. Therefore Theorem
1.1
gives refined geometric meaning of the
\mathsf{DR}
cycle formula.

Although our methods do not give a simple formula for the pushforward when
2\ell+m+\sum_{i}k_{i}
is greater than
2g
, we can at least say that the pushforward is tautological. In this case, the pushforward depends on the choice of stability condition
\epsilon
, so explicit formulas will necessarily be more complicated.

Theorem
1.1
(c) only implies that classes in the subring of
\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon},\mathbb{Q})
generated by divisors push forward to tautological classes. The forthcoming paper
[
10
]
will show that arbitrary tautological classes on the logarithmic Picard group push forward to tautological classes.

1.5
Logarithmic Poincaré line bundle and Arinkin’s kernel

Our construction of the extended Poincaré line bundle is motivated by the duality of the logarithmic Picard group. For smooth curves and their relative Jacobians, the Poincaré line bundle can be described as the Deligne pairing of two universal line bundles. This construction extends to logarithmically smooth curves. For a log smooth curve
C\to B
and the logarithmic Picard group
\mathrm{LogPic}_{C}\to B
, introduced by Molcho-Wise
[
50
]
, the logarithmic Poincaré line bundle
3
3

3

The construction of
\mathcal{P}^{\log}
will appear in the forthcoming work
[
49
]
and it will not be used in this paper.

\mathcal{P}^{\log}\to\mathrm{LogPic}_{C}\times_{B}\mathrm{LogPic}_{C}

is defined via the Deligne pairing of the two universal logarithmic line bundles, which induces a “geometric” duality isomorphism
\mathrm{LogPic}^{0}(\mathrm{LogPic}^{0}_{C})\cong\mathrm{LogPic}^{0}_{C}
. Any log line bundle on a log scheme (or algebraic log space)
X
is representable by a line bundle on a logarithmic modification of
X
, and thus, we can represent
\mathcal{P}^{\textup{log}}
by a line bundle on some logarithmic modification of
\mathrm{LogPic}_{C}^{0}\times_{B}\mathrm{LogPic}_{C}^{0}
.

To connect the geometric duality obtained from the logarithmic Poincaré line bundle to derived equivalences among compactified Jacobians is however a more delicate issue: the logarithmic modification and line bundle representing the log Poincaré must be chosen carefully. For nondegenerate stability conditions
\epsilon_{1}
and
\epsilon_{2}
,
\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon_{1}}\times_{B}\overline{J}^{\epsilon_{2}}_{C}
is a natural logarithmic modification of
\mathrm{LogPic}^{0}_{C}\times_{B}\mathrm{LogPic}^{0}_{C}
, but the classical Poincaré line bundle on
J_{C}\times_{B}J_{C}
does not extend to a line bundle on it; it extends to a line bundle
\mathcal{P}
only up to the open locus
J_{C}^{\epsilon_{1}}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon_{2}}\cup\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon_{1}}\times_{B}J_{C}^{\epsilon_{2}}
, where
J^{\epsilon}_{C}\subset\overline{J}^{\epsilon}_{C}
denotes the locus of line bundles.

Theorem 1.2
.

For
B=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
, there exists a logarithmic modification
f:\overline{J}^{(2)}_{C}\to\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon_{1}}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon_{2}}
, where

(a)

\overline{J}^{(2)}_{C}
is smooth and log smooth. The map
f
is an isomorphism over
J_{C}^{\epsilon_{1}}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon_{2}}\cup\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon_{1}}\times_{B}J_{C}^{\epsilon_{2}}
and its complement has codimension
2
;

(b)

if
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
denotes the unique line bundle on
\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}
extending
\mathcal{P}
, then
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
represents
\mathcal{P}^{\log}
;

(c)

the pushforward
\overline{\mathcal{P}}:=Rf_{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
is a maximal Cohen-Macaulay sheaf extending
\mathcal{P}
;

(d)

\mathfrak{F}:=-\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}:\mathrm{D}^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}(\overline{J}^{\epsilon_{1}}_{C})\to\mathrm{D}^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}(\overline{J}^{\epsilon_{2}}_{C})
is an equivalence between derived categories.

This provides an independent proof that the line bundle
\mathcal{P}
admits a maximal Cohen-Macaulay extension, a result originally proven by Arinkin
[
5
,
6
]
(later generalized in
[
42
,
43
]
). Following Arinkin’s argument, the kernel
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
induces a derived equivalence between
\overline{J}^{\epsilon_{1}}_{C}
and
\overline{J}^{\epsilon_{2}}_{C}
. Consequently, we can use the explicit line bundle
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
as the kernel of our derived equivalence.

1.6
Structure of the universal double ramification cycle formula

The universal double ramification formula is a natural lift of the twisted
\mathsf{DR}
formula. Let
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0}
denote the universal Picard stack, which parametrizes prestable curves of genus
g
with
n
markings and total degree
0
line bundles. For
b\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathsf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
satisfying (
2
), the
\mathsf{uniDR}
formula is a tautological class
\mathsf{uniDR}^{c}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
in
\mathsf{CH}^{c}(\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0},\mathbb{Q})
that generalizes the
\mathsf{DR}
formula
[
8
]
. The
\mathsf{uniDR}
formula is also polynomial in
b,a_{i}
. For a detailed explanation, we refer to Section
6.5
.

The top degree part of the
\mathsf{uniDR}
formula is defined by the relative group structure of
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0}
over the moduli stack of prestable curves
\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}
. Under the pullback along the “multiplication by
N
” map
[N]:\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0}\to\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0}
, the
\mathsf{uniDR}
formula becomes polynomial in
N
. We define the
top degree part

\widetilde{\mathsf{uniDR}}^{c}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
as the part such that the sum of the weight (with respect to
[N]
) and the polynomial degree (with respect to
b,a_{i}
) is exactly
2c
.

We prove a correspondence between
\mathsf{uniDR}
and its top degree part
\widetilde{\mathsf{uniDR}}
. The correspondence is most naturally stated using the negative zeta value regularization convention:

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{d+1}:=\zeta(-d-1)=-\frac{B_{d+2}}{d+2}\quad\text{ for $d\geq 0$},

(3)

where
B_{n}
are the Bernoulli numbers.

Theorem 1.3

(Theorem
6.6
)
.

Let
g,c,n\geq 0
. For each
0\leq m\leq g
, let
j_{m}:\mathfrak{Pic}_{\Gamma_{m}}\to\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0}
be the stratum parameterizing curves with degenerations forced by gluing the last
m
pairs of markings and let
q_{m}:\mathfrak{Pic}_{\Gamma_{m}}\to\mathfrak{Pic}_{g-m,n+2m,0}
be the
(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{m}
-torsor associated to partial normalization. Then

\mathsf{uniDR}_{g}^{c}(b;\mathsf{a})=\sum_{m=0}^{\min(g,c)}\frac{1}{2^{m}m!}(j_{m})_{*}(q_{m})^{*}\Bigg[\sum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}>0}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{m}k_{i}\Big)\widetilde{\mathsf{uniDR}}^{c-m}_{g-m}(b;\mathsf{a},k_{1},-k_{1},\ldots,k_{m},-k_{m})\Bigg]\,,

where the infinite sum over
k_{i}
of a polynomial in
k_{i}
is evaluated via (
3
).

By pulling back Theorem
1.3
along the unit
e:\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}\to\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n}
, we obtain genus recursion for the
\mathsf{DR}
cycle formula (Theorem
6.1
).

We also prove an identity involving the pushforward of the regular (twisted)
\mathsf{DR}
cycle formula under the map
p:\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n+1}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
forgetting the last marking.

Theorem 1.4

(Theorem
7.1
)
.

Let
g,c,n\geq 0
. Let
F=p_{*}\mathsf{DR}_{g}^{c}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})\in\mathsf{CH}^{c-1}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n})
be viewed as a polynomial in
b,a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1}
modulo the relation (
1
).
Then
F
is a multiple of
(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}
. Moreover, we have the identity

\left[\frac{F}{(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}}\right]_{a_{n+1}:=b}=(g+1-c)\mathsf{DR}_{g}^{c-1}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\,.

A generalization of this theorem to the
\mathsf{uniDR}
formula is given in Section
7.6
(Theorem
7.7
).

1.7
Fourier transform and the resolved Abel-Jacobi sections

We sketch our proof of Theorem
1.1
here; full details are found in Section
8
. Our key approach to computing the Fourier transform is by linking it to logarithmic Abel-Jacobi theory.

Let
\epsilon_{0},\epsilon
be non-degenerate stability conditions. Following the appropriate normalization as in Maulik-Shen-Yin
[
41
]
, we consider the Chow-theoretic realization of the Fourier transform of Theorem
1.2
(d) given by the following relative algebraic correspondence:

\mathfrak{F}:=f_{*}\big(\mathrm{td}(T_{\overline{J}^{(2)}_{C}}-f^{*}T_{\overline{J}^{\epsilon_{0}}_{g,n}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}}\overline{J}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}})\cdot\mathrm{ch}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}})\big):\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\,\epsilon_{0}},\mathbb{Q})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\,\epsilon},\mathbb{Q})\,.

(4)

We denote its inverse by
\mathfrak{F}^{-1}
. Choose
\epsilon_{0}
to be small, so that the unit section
e
exists in
\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon_{0}}
.

We start from the Fourier image of the resolved Abel-Jacobi section. By
[
33
]
, the rational Abel-Jacobi section admits a logarithmic resolution
\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}:\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,b;\mathsf{a}}^{\,\epsilon_{0}}\to\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\,\epsilon_{0}}
. Using Theorem
1.2
, the image of
\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}
has the form:

\mathfrak{F}\big([\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}]\big)=\exp\left(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i}\right)\cdot(1+\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}})\,,

(5)

where
\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}}
is a class associated with an explicit piecewise polynomial (in the sense of
[
47
, Section 5.5]
) on
\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
which is supported away from the locus of integral curves (Proposition
5.8
).

Logarithmic Abel-Jacobi theory computes the locus of the resolved Abel-Jacobi section. Using
[
8
]
(Proposition
8.2
), the class of the image of
\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}
can be written as the codimension
g
part of the
\mathsf{uniDR}
formula:

[\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}]=\mathsf{uniDR}^{g}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})\Big|_{\overline{J}^{\epsilon_{0}}_{g,n}}\in\mathsf{CH}^{g}(\overline{J}^{\epsilon_{0}}_{g,n},\mathbb{Q})\,.

(6)

Consider the inverse Fourier transform from
\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
to
\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon_{0}}
. Combining (
5
) and (
6
), we obtain

\mathsf{uniDR}^{g}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})\Big|_{\overline{J}^{\epsilon_{0}}_{g,n}}=[\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}]=\mathfrak{F}^{-1}\left(\exp\left(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i}\right)\cdot(1+\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}})\right)\,.

(7)

We are then able to match the leading term of the inverse Fourier transform
\mathfrak{F}^{-1}
and the top degree term of the
\mathsf{uniDR}
formula by induction on genus (Theorem
8.3
). After restricting
\mathfrak{F}^{-1}
to
\overline{J}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}}J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n}
, the inverse transform
\mathfrak{F}^{-1}
reduces to

\mathfrak{F}^{-1}=(-1)^{g}\cdot\mathrm{ch}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee})\cdot\pi_{1}^{*}\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\pi})^{-1}:\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon},\mathbb{Q})\to\mathsf{CH}^{*}(J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n},\mathbb{Q})\,,

where
\mathcal{R}_{\pi}
is the sheaf of residues of logarithmic differentials on
\overline{J}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}
. Let
\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}:=\mathrm{ch}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee})
be, up to sign, the leading term of
\mathfrak{F}^{-1}
.
We show that the image of
\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}
of classes supported on boundary strata of
\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\,\epsilon}
can be computed from
\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}
for lower genera (Theorem
5.11
). By Theorem
1.3
, the
\mathsf{uniDR}
formula satisfies a genus recursion with respect to the top degree part. Combining the two recursive structures via (
7
), we show that the contribution of the inverse Fourier transform of the class
\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}}
does not contribute in codimension
g
and the contribution from
\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\pi})^{-1}
exactly matches with the recursion formula in Theorem
1.3
. Therefore, the codimension
c<g
part of the image of monomials of weight
1
divisors vanishes and we get

\left[\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}\left(\exp\left(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i}\right)\right)\right]_{\mathrm{codim}=g}=(-1)^{g}\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{uniDR}}_{g}^{g}(b;\mathsf{a})\Big|_{J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n}}\,.

Pulling back both sides along the zero section
e
and applying (
1
) yields Theorem
1.1
when
\ell=0
.

For monomials with a positive exponent on
\Theta
, we use Theorem
1.4
to reduce to
\ell=0
.

1.8
Other degenerate abelian fibrations

Let
\mathcal{A}_{g}
be the moduli space of principally polarized abelian varieties of dimension
g
and let
\mathcal{A}_{g}\subset\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\prime}
be the canonical partial compactification of rank
1
degenerations. Let
\pi:\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{g}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{g}
be the universal family and let
\mathcal{X}^{\circ}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{g}
be the universal semi-abelian scheme. Let
\mu:\mathcal{X}^{\circ}_{g}\times_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{g}}\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{g}\to\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{g}
be the multiplication map. By the work of Arinkin-Fedorov
[
7
]
, the Poincaré line bundle extends to a line bundle
\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{X}^{\circ}_{g}\times_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{g}}\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{g}\cup\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{g}\times_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{g}}\mathcal{X}^{\circ}_{g}
.

Theorem 1.5
.

There exists a unique maximal Cohen-Macaulay sheaf
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
on
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{g}\times_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{g}}\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{g}
extending
\mathcal{P}
. Moreover,
\mathfrak{F}:=-\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}:\mathrm{D}^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{g})\to\mathrm{D}^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{g})
induces a derived equivalence.

We prove Theorem
1.5
by resolving the indeterminacy of the multiplication map
\mu
, following the approach of Theorem
1.2
. It gives a new derived equivalence which does not follow from
[
6
]
.

We connect the partial Fourier transformation and the weight decomposition for semi-abelian group schemes and compute the class of the unit section
e
inside
\mathcal{X}^{\circ}_{g}
.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
e:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{g}\to\mathcal{X}^{\circ}_{g}
be the unit section. Then we have

[e]=\Big[\exp(\Theta)\cdot\Big(1+\frac{1}{2}i_{*}q^{*}\sum_{a=1}^{g}\frac{-B_{2a}}{a!}\cdot\frac{(\Theta_{1})^{a-1}}{2}\Big)\Big]_{\mathrm{codim}=g}\in\mathsf{CH}^{g}(\mathcal{X}^{\circ}_{g})\,.

For a detailed explanation of notations, we refer to Section
9.5
.
Along the way, we provide a new proof of the
\mathsf{uniDR}
formula on the relative Jacobian over the treelike locus
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\,\mathrm{tl}}_{g,n}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
.

Conventions

We work over a field
k
of characteristic zero. All Chow groups are taken with
\mathbb{Q}
-coefficients. All monoids and log structures will always be fine and saturated (f.s.) in the sense of
[
38
,
55
]
. For a scheme
X
, let
\mathrm{D}^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}(X)
denote the bounded derived category of coherent sheaves on
X
. Let
K_{0}(X)
(resp.
K^{0}(X)
) denote the Grothendieck group of coherent sheaves (resp. locally free sheaves). For a morphism
X\to Y
and
Y^{\prime}\to Y
, we denote
X|_{Y^{\prime}}:=X\times_{Y}Y^{\prime}
.

2
Compactified Jacobians

2.1
Compactified Jacobian and logarithmic Picard group

Relative Jacobians of a family of prestable curves (
[
61
, Definition 0E6T]
) can be compactified in two distinct ways: through the compactified Jacobian, and via logarithmic Picard groups.

Let
p:C\to B
be a prestable curve of genus
g
equipped with a section. For
b\in B
, let
\Gamma_{b}
denote the dual graph of the prestable curve
C_{b}
. A
stability condition

\epsilon
of degree
d
for
p
is a function

\epsilon:V(\Gamma_{b})\to\mathbb{Q}

for each geometric point
b\in B
which has total degree
d
and is compatible with degenerations (Section
1.2
). A prestable curve
C
is called quasi-stable if the dualizing sheaf
\omega_{C}
is nef and chains of unstable components have length at most
1
. A line bundle
L
on
C
is
admissible
if
L
has degree
1
on each unstable component of
C
. An admissible line bundle
L
on a quasi-stable curve
C
is
\epsilon
-stable (resp.
\epsilon
-semistable) if for every proper subcurve
\emptyset\subsetneq\underline{C}\subsetneq C
with neither
\underline{C}
nor its complement consisting entirely of unstable components, we have

\epsilon(\underline{C})-\frac{E(\underline{C},\underline{C}^{c})}{2}<(\leq)\deg(L|_{\underline{C}})<(\leq)\,\epsilon(\underline{C})+\frac{E(\underline{C},\underline{C}^{c})}{2}\,,

where
E(\underline{C},\underline{C}^{c})
is the number of intersection points of
\underline{C}
with the complement
\underline{C}^{c}
and
\epsilon(\underline{C})
is the sum of the values of
\epsilon
over all irreducible components of
\underline{C}
. A stability condition is called
nondegenerate
if
\epsilon
-semi-stability equals
\epsilon
-stability.

For a stability condition
\epsilon
, a compactified Jacobian
\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon}\to B
of
p
is a good moduli space parametrizing
\epsilon
-semistable rank
1
torsion free sheaves (
[
16
,
37
]
). When the stability
\epsilon
is clear from context, or the precise choice of
\epsilon
is irrelevant to the argument, we simply denote
\overline{J}_{C}:=\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon}
. Let
J^{\underline{0}}_{C}\to B
be a relative Jacobian parametrizing multidegree
0
line bundles. Tensor product induces a natural action

\mu:J^{\underline{0}}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}^{\epsilon}_{C}\to\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon}\,.

(8)

The compactified Jacobian has several desirable properties when the base
B
is
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
. For nondegenerate
\epsilon
,
\overline{J}^{\epsilon}_{C}
is a smooth Deligne-Mumford stack, the projection map
\pi:\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon}\to B
is representable, proper and flat (
[
37
, Corollary 4.4]
). When the family
p
has a section, rigidifying along that section produces a universal sheaf on the universal curve
C\to\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon}
.

To describe the second compactification of relative Jacobians, we begin by recalling definitions from logarithmic geometry. A
log scheme

X=(X,\mathsf{M}_{X})
consists of a scheme
X
together with a sheaf of commutative monoids
\mathsf{M}_{X}
on the étale site of
X
together with a morphism
\alpha:\mathsf{M}_{X}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}
with
\alpha^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*}\cong\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*}
. For a sheaf of monoids
\mathsf{M}_{X}
, let
\mathsf{M}_{X}^{\mathrm{gp}}
be the sheaf of abelian groups associated with the Grothendieck group of
\mathsf{M}_{X}
. For a log scheme
B
, we write
\textbf{LogSch}/B
the category of f.s. log schemes over
B
. We refer to
[
38
,
55
]
for the foundational concepts of logarithmic geometry.

The logarithmic Picard group, introduced by Molcho and Wise
[
50
]
, offers a canonical compactification of the relative Jacobian within the framework of algebraic logarithmic stacks. Let
B
be a logarithmic scheme which is log smooth, and let
p:C\to B
be a proper vertical log smooth curve.

Theorem 2.1

(
[
50
]
)
.

Let
\textbf{LogPic}_{C}:(\textbf{LogSch}/B)^{\text{op}}\to\text{(Grp)}
be the functor defined by

(B^{\prime}\to B)\mapsto\{\text{$\mathsf{M}^{\mathrm{gp}}_{C|_{B^{\prime}}}$-torsor of bounded monodromy}\}\,.

Then
\textbf{LogPic}_{C}
is an algebraic logarithmic stack. The relative rigidification
\mathrm{LogPic}_{C}
is log smooth with proper components, and forms a commutative group object over
B
.

For details on the notion of bounded monodromy, we refer to
[
50
, Section 3.5]
. In brief, it amounts to an infinitesimal smoothability condition.

Compactified Jacobians serve as a “scheme-theoretic” birational model of the logarithmic Picard group. In
[
50
, Proposition 4.4.8]
a natural morphism

\overline{J}_{C}\to\mathrm{LogPic}_{C}\,

(9)

is constructed. While the logarithmic Picard stack is not representable by an algebraic stack, the morphism (
9
) is a log modification by
[
1
]
, which implies that the compactified Jacobian
\overline{J}_{C}
can be interpreted as a representable, birational model of
\mathrm{LogPic}_{C}
.
4
4

4

A morphism
f:X\to Y
between algebraic logarithmic stacks is called a
log modification
if for any morphism
T\to Y
from a log scheme,
f_{T}:X_{T}\to Y_{T}
is a log modification (in particular, proper, representable and birational).

2.2
Universal Picard stack and tautological classes

Tautological classes on the relative compactified Jacobian are derived from tautological classes pulled back from the universal Picard stack. Let
\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}
denote the moduli stack of prestable (not necessarily stable) curves of genus
g
with
n
marked points, and let
p:\mathfrak{C}_{g,n}\to\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}
be the universal curve together with sections
x_{i}:\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}\to\mathfrak{C}_{g,n}
for
1\leq i\leq n
. The
universal Picard stack

\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n}\to\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}

is the relative Picard stack over
\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}
, as described in
[
8
, Section 2]
. It is a smooth algebraic stack of locally of finite type over
\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}
and decomposes into a disjoint union of connected components
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,d}
, indexed by
d
, the total degree of a line bundle. Under the natural boundary stratification,
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,d}
is an algebraic stack with a log structure.

The compactified Jacobian admits a morphism to the universal Picard stack. For
n\geq 1
, let
B=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
and let
\overline{J}^{\epsilon}_{C}\to B
denote the compactified Jacobian associated with a non-degenerate stability condition
\epsilon
. We choose a universal rank
1
torsion-free sheaf
F\to C
. According to
[
25
]
, there exists a quasi-stable model

\nu:C^{\mathrm{qs}}\to C

along with an admissible line bundle

L\to C^{\mathrm{qs}}

such that
R\nu_{*}L\cong F
. The pair
(C^{\mathrm{qs}},L)
is referred to as the
quasi-stable model
. This quasi-stable model, defined over
\overline{J}_{C}
induces a morphism

\varphi:\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon}\to\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n}\,,

(10)

which is dependent on the chosen universal sheaf. For
n\geq 1
, let
\mathfrak{Pic}^{\text{rel}}_{g,n}
be the relative rigidification of
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n}\to\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}
. Then
\varphi
factors through
\overline{\varphi}:\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon}\to\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n}^{\text{rel}}
.

Lemma 2.2
.

For a nondegenerate stability condition
\epsilon
, the morphism (
10
) is smooth. Moreover
\overline{\varphi}
is an open immersion.

Proof.

Clearly
\varphi
is locally of finite presentation. Fibers of
\varphi
have constant dimension. Since
\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon}
and
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n}
are both smooth, we conclude that
\varphi
is flat by the miracle flatness lemma. Moreover, fibers of
\varphi
are smooth, hence it is smooth. Since
\overline{\varphi}
is a momonorphism, it is an open immersion.
∎

We define a natural notion of tautological ring for a compactified Jacobian by pulling back the tautological ring of
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n}
via the morphism
\varphi
defined in (
10
):

\mathsf{R}^{*}(\overline{J}_{C})\subset\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{C},\mathbb{Q})\,.

(11)

In forthcoming work
[
10
]
, the tautological ring of compactified Jacobians—and more generally, the logarithmic Picard group—is studied in detail. In this paper, we focus on monomials of divisors. Among the tautological classes of codimension
1
, we highlight the following:

\Theta:=-\frac{1}{2}p_{*}(c_{1}(\mathcal{L})^{2}),\kappa_{0,1}=p_{*}(c_{1}(\omega)c_{1}(\mathcal{L})),\text{ and }\xi_{i}:=x_{i}^{*}(c_{1}(\mathcal{L}))\,.

We describe the boundary strata of the relative and compactified Jacobians when
B=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
. For each prestable graph
\Gamma
and multi-degree function
\delta\colon V(\Gamma)\to\mathbb{Z}
, let
\mathfrak{Pic}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}
denote the stack parametrizing prestable curves
C_{v}
of genus
g(v)
with
n(v)
markings for each vertex
v\in V(\Gamma)
, together with a line bundle
L
on a prestable curve
C
obtained via the gluing map
\nu:\bigsqcup_{v\in V(\Gamma)}C_{v}\to C
associated to
\Gamma
, such that the restriction
\nu^{*}L|_{C_{v}}
has degree
\delta(v)
.
Then
\mathfrak{Pic}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}
admits morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Pic}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\mathfrak{Pic}_{g(v),n(v),\delta(v)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\Gamma_{\delta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

(12)

where the morphism
j_{\Gamma_{\delta}}
is proper, representable and
q
is the morphism induced by the partial normalization induced by the edge contraction data. The morphism
q
is a
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{h^{1}(\Gamma)}
-torsor. For a quasi-stable graph
\Gamma
with
\epsilon
-stable multidegree
\delta
,
\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}
is given by the cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{g,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Pic}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n}\,,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\Gamma_{\delta}}}

(13)

where the right vertical morphism is (
10
).

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\pi:J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
be the relative Jacobian.
Let
j:\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g-\ell,n+2\ell}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
be the gluing map identifying
n+2i-1
-th marking and
n+2i
-th marking for
1\leq i\leq\ell
. Let
q:J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n}|_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g-\ell,n+2\ell}}\to J^{\underline{0}}_{g-\ell,n+2\ell}
be the morphism given by the pullback of line bundles to partial normalization. Then
q
can be identified with

J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n}|_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g-\ell,n+2\ell}}\cong(L_{n+1}\otimes L_{n+2}^{\vee})^{\times}\oplus\cdots\oplus(L_{n+2\ell-1}\otimes L^{\vee}_{n+2\ell})^{\times}\to J^{\underline{0}}_{g-\ell,n+2\ell}\,.

Proof.

By partial normalization, we get a morphism
J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n}|_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g-\ell,n+2\ell}}\to J^{\underline{0}}_{g-\ell,n+2\ell}
. Fibers of the morphism record the identification of the line bundle at
n+2i-1
and
n+2i^{\prime}
,
L|_{n+2i-1}\cong L|_{n+2i}
.
∎

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\Gamma_{h}
be the stable graph consisting of a single vertex of genus
g-h
with
h
loops and let
\Gamma_{h}^{\prime}
be the quasistable graph obtained by subdividing each edge of
\Gamma_{h}
. Then there exists a nondegenerate stability condition
\epsilon_{h}
of degree
d-h
together with a map

\overline{J}^{\epsilon_{h}}_{g-h,n+2h}\to\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}

which identifies
\overline{J}^{\epsilon_{h}}_{g-h,n+2h}\to\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
with
\overline{J}_{\Gamma^{\prime}_{h}}\to\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
.

Proof.

We define a nondegenerate stability condition
\epsilon_{h}
for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g-h,n+2h}
of degree
d-h
as follows. Suppose
\Gamma
is a stable graph of genus
g-h
with
n+2h
legs. Then gluing the last
h
pairs of legs gives a stable graph of genus
g
with
n
legs, so the stability condition
\epsilon
can be applied to give numbers
\epsilon(v)
for
v\in V(\Gamma)
(with sum
d
).
We then define
\epsilon_{h}:V(\Gamma)\to\mathbb{Q}
by

\epsilon_{h}(v)=\epsilon(v)-\frac{1}{2}\text{(number of $i$-th legs $n+1\leq i\leq n+2h$ attached to $v$)}\,.

(14)

Since
\epsilon
is a stability condition, (
14
) is a stability condition for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g-h,n+2h}
of degree
d-h
. When
\epsilon
is nondegenerate, then
\epsilon_{h}
is also nondegenerate by
[
37
, Definition 5.1]
.

Over
\overline{J}^{\epsilon_{h}}_{g-h,n+2h}
, there exists a morphism of curves
\nu:\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{qs}}_{g-h,n+2h}\to\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma^{\prime}_{h}}
where
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{qs}}_{g-h,n+2h}
is the universal quasi-stable curve and
\nu
is the morphism gluing the
(n+2i-1)
-th to the
(n+2i)
-th marking for
1\leq i\leq h
. For the universal line bundle
\mathcal{L}
on
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{qs}}_{g-h,n+2h}
, the pushforward
\nu_{*}\mathcal{L}
is a rank
1
torsion free sheaf. The universal quasi-stable model of
(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{qs}}_{g-h,n+2h},\nu_{*}\mathcal{L})
defines the desired morphism
\overline{J}^{\epsilon_{h}}_{g-h,n+2h}\to\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
.
∎

We end with the connection between the universal Picard stack and the logarithmic Picard group. For the universal Picard stack
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n}
over
\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}
and the logarithmic Picard group
\mathrm{LogPic}_{g,n}
over
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
, in
[
50
, Proposition 4.4.8]
a natural morphism

\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n}\to\mathrm{LogPic}_{g,n}

(15)

is constructed. When
B=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
, the morphism (
9
) is in fact defined by the composition

\overline{J}_{g,n}\to\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n}\to\mathrm{LogPic}_{g,n}
.

3
Extending the Poincaré line bundle

3.1
Poincaré line bundle

Let
p:C\to B
be a proper log smooth curve with a section. Let
\epsilon_{1}
and
\epsilon_{2}
be two nondegenerate stability conditions for
p
. For the compactified Jacobian
\overline{J}_{C}^{\,\epsilon_{2}}\to B
, let

J^{\,\epsilon_{2}}_{C}\subset\overline{J}^{\,\epsilon_{2}}_{C}

(16)

be the open substack given by the locus of line bundles. For the compactified Jacobian
\overline{J}_{C}^{\,\epsilon_{1}}
, choose the universal sheaf
\mathcal{F}
on the universal curve
C
which is trivialized along the section. Let
C^{\text{qs}}\to\overline{J}_{C}^{\,\epsilon_{1}}
be the universal quasi-stable curve and
\mathcal{L}_{1}
be the universal line bundle on
C^{\text{qs}}
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
p:C^{\mathrm{qs}}\to\overline{J}_{C}^{\,\epsilon_{1}}\times_{B}J_{C}^{\,\epsilon_{2}}
be the pullback of the universal quasi-stable curve, and
\mathcal{L}_{1}
(resp.
\mathcal{L}_{2}
) be the universal line bundle of
\overline{J}_{C}^{\,\epsilon_{1}}
(resp.
J_{C}^{\,\epsilon_{2}}
). The
Poincare line bundle

\mathcal{P}
is defined by

\mathcal{P}=\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}\rangle\in\mathrm{Pic}(\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}J_{C})\,,

where
\langle-,-\rangle
is the Deligne pairing
[
21
]
. If we wish to emphasize the universal curve, we may write
\langle-,-\rangle_{C}
.

Definition
3.1
coincides with the definition from
[
5
,
42
]
because we only consider families of prestable curves.
The Poincaré line bundle in general depends on the choices of universal line bundles, but if both stability conditions
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}
are of degree zero, it is independent of the choices.
We often omit stability conditions in our notation and write
\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}J_{C}:=\overline{J}_{C}^{\,\epsilon_{1}}\times_{B}J_{C}^{\,\epsilon_{2}}
.

Similarly, the Poincaré line bundle exists on
J_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}
. Since the two line bundles coincide on the intersection, the line bundle
\mathcal{P}
exists on
J_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}\cup\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}J_{C}
.
Let

\iota:\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}J_{C}\cup J_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}\hookrightarrow\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}

(17)

be the open embedding.

By Grothendieck-Riemann-Roch (
[
21
, (6.6.1)]
), the first Chern class of the Deligne pairing of two line bundles can be written as

c_{1}(\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}\rangle)=p_{*}(c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{1})c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{2}))\,.

(18)

3.2
Birational model of
\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}

In this section, we set
B=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
. Let
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}
be two nondegenerate stability conditions for
B
. Throughout this section, we abbreviate notation and write
\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}:=\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon_{1}}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon_{2}}
. On
\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}
the Deligne pairing does not extend. Using logarithmic geometry, we construct a birational model of
\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}
to resolve the indeterminacy of the Deligne pairing (
18
).

A map of log schemes (or algebraic stacks) is
strict
if it induces an isomorphism on log structures. We assume our log schemes come with a tropicalization map

t:B\to\mathcal{A}_{B}

(19)

to some Artin fan
\mathcal{A}_{B}
, and write
\Sigma_{B}
for the corresponding cone stack, see
[
17
]
. The tropicalization map is strict.
Moreover, for any logarithmic morphism
f:X\to S
between log schemes, there exists an induced morphism
A_{X}\to\mathcal{A}_{S}
between the corresponding Artin fans.

A
log alteration
of a log scheme
B
is a map
\mathcal{A}_{B}^{\prime}\times_{\mathcal{A}_{B}}B\to B
given by the base change of a proper, Deligne-Mumford type and birational logarithmic morphism
\mathcal{A}_{B}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{A}_{B}
. A log alteration is called a
log modification
if it’s representable, and a root if it is bijective on geometric points.
Under the correspondence
\mathcal{A}_{B}\leftrightarrow\Sigma_{B}
, log modifications correspond to subdivisions of
\Sigma_{B}
, and roots to the choice of a finite index integral substructure of the integral structure of
\Sigma_{B}
.

Definition 3.2
.

A log alteration
B^{\prime}\to B
is called
small
if the map
\mathcal{A}_{B^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{A}_{B}
induces an isomorphism between the groups of Weil divisors of
\mathcal{A}_{B}
and
\mathcal{A}_{B^{\prime}}
.

Let
\mathfrak{Q}
denote the stack of quasi-stable curves. The stack
\mathfrak{Q}
is algebraic because it is an open substack of
\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}
. By restriction,
\mathfrak{Q}
carries a natural logarithmic structure.

Definition 3.3
.

We define a log stack
\mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}:(\textbf{LogSch}/B)^{\mathrm{op}}\to(\mathrm{Grp})
by

\mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}(S\to B):=\{(C_{1}\to S,C_{2}\to S,\widetilde{C}\to C_{i})\}

where

•

C_{i}\to S
are two quasi-stable models of
C_{S}/S
,

•

\widetilde{C}\to C_{i}
is a quasi-stable model such that
\widetilde{C}\to S
remains a log smooth curve, and

•

\widetilde{C}
is the minimal common log modification of the
C_{i}
, i.e.

\widetilde{C}=C_{1}\times^{\textup{fs}}_{C}C_{2}.

There is a natural projection

\mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}\to\mathfrak{Q}\times_{B}\mathfrak{Q}

by sending

(C_{1}\to S,C_{2}\to S,\widetilde{C}\to C_{i})\to(C_{1}/S,C_{2}/S)\,.

We show that
\mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}
is given by a modification of
\mathfrak{Q}\times_{B}\mathfrak{Q}
.

Proposition 3.4
.

The projection
\mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}\to\mathfrak{Q}\times_{B}\mathfrak{Q}
is a log modification.

Proof.

We argue tropically. Let
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{Q}}
denote the stack over rational polyhedral cones that parametrizes, over a cone
\sigma
, a quasi-stable graph
\widetilde{\Gamma}
with stabilization
\Gamma
, where edge lengths are metrized by
M_{\sigma}:=\mathrm{Hom}(\sigma,\mathbb{N})
. It is straightforward to see that
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{Q}}
is a tropicalization of
\mathfrak{Q}
. We define
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}}
as the stack that parametrizes two quasi-stable models
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
of
\Gamma
over
\sigma
together with a minimal common quasi-stable model
\Gamma^{\prime}
, all metrized over
\sigma
. Then we have an isomorphism:

\mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}\cong\Sigma_{\mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}}\times_{(\Sigma_{\mathfrak{Q}}\times_{\Sigma_{B}}\Sigma_{\mathfrak{Q}})}(\mathfrak{Q}\times_{B}\mathfrak{Q})\,,

which indicates that it suffices to verify that
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}}\to\Sigma_{\mathfrak{Q}}\times_{\Sigma_{B}}\Sigma_{\mathfrak{Q}}
is a subdivision.

Let
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
be two quasistable models of
\Gamma
metrized by
M_{\sigma}
. Suppose
e
is an edge of
\Gamma
that is subdivided in both
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
. The length of the edge
e
is an element
\ell_{e}\in M_{\sigma}
. Since the edge is subdivided in both
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
, there exist elements
\ell_{e_{i}}^{\prime},\ell_{e_{i}}^{\prime\prime}
,
i=1,2
such that

\ell_{e}=\ell_{e_{i}}^{\prime}+\ell_{e_{i}}^{\prime\prime}\,.

We orient
\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime},\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime\prime}
so that the terminal point of
\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime}
and the initial point of
\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime\prime}
is the quasistable vertex. Similarly,
\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime},\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime\prime}
are oriented “the same way”, meaning the initial point of
\ell_{e_{2}^{\prime}}
coincides with the initial point of
\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime}
, and its terminal point is the quasistable vertex.

Combinatorially, the only possible minimal quasistable model of
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
is given by the common subdivision of the
\Gamma_{i}
, i.e. the fiber product:

\widetilde{\Gamma}=\Gamma_{1}\times_{\Gamma}\Gamma_{2}\,.

This model, however, is not metrized over
M_{\sigma}
: put otherwise, the cone complex associated to the fiber product fails to be flat over
\sigma
on the locus where the functions
\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime}
and
\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime}
become equal. Nevertheless, there is a minimal subdivision of
\sigma
– the subdivision along the hyperplane

\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime}=\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime}

for every edge
e
subdivided in both
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
– over which
\widetilde{\Gamma}
is defined. Consequently, in the universal case, it follows that the map
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}}\to\Sigma_{\mathfrak{Q}}\times_{\Sigma_{B}}\Sigma_{\mathfrak{Q}}
is the subdivision along the hyperplanes
\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime}-\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime}
where
e
ranges through all the edges of
\Gamma
that are subdivided in both
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

Let
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
be two universal curves on
\mathfrak{Q}\times_{B}\mathfrak{Q}
with the same stabilization
C
. The f.s. fiber product
C_{1}\times^{\textup{fs}}_{C}C_{2}\to\mathfrak{Q}\times_{B}\mathfrak{Q}
is not flat, so it is not a curve. By the semistable reduction theorem (
[
45
]
),
there exists a canonical log alteration
\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{Q}\times_{B}\mathfrak{Q}
and
C^{\prime}
of
C_{1}\times^{\textup{fs}}_{C}C_{2}
such that
C^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}
becomes a curve. Proposition
3.4
provides an alternative perspective:
\mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}
is exactly
\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}
, and further alteration of
C_{1}\times^{\textup{fs}}_{C}C_{2}
is unnecessary as it is already flat over
\mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}
.

Corollary 3.6
.

The stack
\mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}
is smooth and log smooth. Furthermore, the exceptional locus of
\mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}
does not contain any divisors.

Proof.

Cones of
\Sigma_{B}
correspond to stable graphs
\Gamma
; for such a graph, with set of edges
E(\Gamma)
, the associated cone is

\sigma_{\Gamma}=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{E(\Gamma)}

with its natural integral structure. Similarily, cones of
\Sigma_{\mathcal{Q}}
correspond to quasistable graphs
\Gamma^{\prime}
, with corresponding cone

\sigma_{\Gamma^{\prime}}=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{E(\Gamma^{\prime})}.

Let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be a quasistable graph with stabilization
\Gamma
. For each edge
e
of
\Gamma
, there is either exactly one or exactly two edges of
\Gamma^{\prime}
that map to
e
. We write
E^{u}(\Gamma)
for the edges of the first kind, and
E^{s}(\Gamma)
for the edges of the second kind. If we denote the edges of
\Gamma^{\prime}
that map to
e\in E^{s}(\Gamma)
by
e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}
, then we have

E(\Gamma^{\prime})=E^{u}(\Gamma)\bigcup_{e\in E^{s}(\Gamma)}\left\{e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\right\}.

The map
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{Q}}\to\Sigma_{B}
is defined as follows: on the cone corresponding to
\Gamma^{\prime}
, with stabilization
\Gamma
, the map

\sigma_{\Gamma^{\prime}}=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{E^{u}(\Gamma)}\times_{e\in E^{s}(\Gamma)}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}e^{\prime}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}e^{\prime\prime}\to\sigma_{\Gamma}=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{E^{u}(\Gamma)}\times_{e\in E^{s}(\Gamma)}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}e

is the product of the identity on the
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{E^{u}(\Gamma)}
factor with the addition maps

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}e^{\prime}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}e^{\prime\prime}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}e\,,(\ell_{e}^{\prime},\ell_{e}^{\prime\prime})\mapsto\ell_{e}^{\prime}+\ell_{e}^{\prime\prime}\,.

Similarily, cones of the fiber product
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{Q}}\times_{\Sigma_{B}}\Sigma_{\mathfrak{Q}}
are indexed by pairs of quasistable graphs
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
with mutual stabilization
\Gamma
. Keeping the notation above, we further split the edges of
\Gamma
as

E^{u}(\Gamma)\cup E^{s_{1}\vee s_{2}}(\Gamma)\cup E^{s_{1}\cap s_{2}}(\Gamma)

where

•

E^{s_{1}\vee s_{2}}(\Gamma)
are the edges of
\Gamma
subdivided in either
\Gamma_{1}
or
\Gamma_{2}
but not both, and

•

E^{s_{1}\cap s_{2}}(\Gamma)
are the edges subdivided in both
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
.

From the description of the maps
\sigma_{\Gamma_{i}}\to\sigma_{\Gamma}
, it follows that the cone corresponding to
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
, which is the fiber product
\sigma_{\Gamma_{1}}\times_{\sigma_{\Gamma}}\sigma_{\Gamma_{2}}
, is explicitly

\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{e\in E^{u}(\Gamma)}\times\prod_{e\in E^{s_{1}\vee s_{2}}(\Gamma)}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}\times\prod_{e\in E^{s_{1}\cap s_{2}}(\Gamma)}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}\times_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}

If we label the two edges in
\Gamma_{i}
subdividing
e\in E^{s_{1}\cap s_{2}}(\Gamma)
by
e_{i}^{\prime},e_{i}^{\prime\prime}
, as we’ve done above, the corresponding cone
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}\times_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}
is explicitly

\left\{(\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime},\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime\prime},\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime},\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime\prime}):\ell_{e_{i}}^{\prime}+\ell_{e_{i}}^{\prime\prime}=\ell_{e}\right\}

This cone is responsible for an “
xy=zw
” singularity in
\mathfrak{Q}\times_{B}\mathfrak{Q}
, and is subdivided in
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}}
into two unimodular simplices, along the hyperplane

\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime}=\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime}\,.

We refer to (
21
) for the associated tropical picture.
Hence, each cone in
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}}
is smooth. Furthermore, the subdivision is along hyperplanes, which means no additional divisors are introduced. This proves the corollary.
∎

We return to the compactified Jacobian
\overline{J}_{C}
and the morphism
\varphi:\overline{J}_{C}\to\mathfrak{Q}
induced by (
10
).

Definition 3.7
.

The stack
\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}
is defined by the fiber diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Q}\times_{B}\mathfrak{Q}\,.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\times\varphi}

(20)

The log modification
f:\overline{J}^{(2)}_{C}\to\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}
is the one appears in Theorem
1.2
.
The below horizontal arrow is strict by Lemma
2.2
. Therefore the diagram (
20
) is an f.s. fiber diagram. By Proposition
3.4
, the morphism
f:\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}\to\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}
is a log modification.

Recall that a log stack
X
is called
log smooth
if the morphism
X\to\Sigma_{X}
to a tropicalization is smooth.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
(a).

The morphism
\varphi\times\varphi:\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}\to\mathfrak{Q}\times_{B}\mathfrak{Q}
is smooth, strict and log smooth by Lemma
2.2
. Since the diagram (
20
) is Cartesian, the claim follows from Corollary
3.6
.
∎

A morphism
f:X\to Y
between fine log schemes is called
integral
if for any geometric point
x\in X
, the induced morphism between commutative monoids
\overline{\mathsf{M}}_{Y,f(x)}\to\overline{\mathsf{M}}_{X,x}
is integral.

Definition 3.8
.

A
log family
is a log smooth, integral, saturated morphism
f:X\to S
between log schemes. A log family is
semistable
if in addition
X,S
are smooth and log smooth.

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
f:\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}\to\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}
be the log modification in Defintion
3.7
.

(a)

The two projections
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}:\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}\to\overline{J}_{C}
are semistable. Moreover, the projection
\widetilde{\pi}:\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}\to B
is also semistable.

(b)

For any geometric point
x
of
\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}
,
f^{-1}(x)
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Proof.

(a) Since
\overline{J}_{C}
and
\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}
are smooth, it suffices to show that the map is integral with reduced fibers. This conclusion follows directly from Corollary
3.6
and its proof. Specifically, under either projection
\pi_{i}
, the two cones arising from the subdivision of each square

\{\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime},\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime\prime},\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime},\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime\prime}:\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime}+\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime\prime}=\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime}+\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime\prime}\}

along
\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime}=\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime}
map surjectively onto their images in
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{Q}}
, with their integral structures likewise surjecting onto the corresponding images. The second assertion follows from the first because
\overline{J}_{C}\to B
is semistable.

(b) This result again follows from the proof of Corollary
3.6
, as the map
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}}\to\Sigma_{\mathfrak{Q}}\times_{\Sigma_{B}}\Sigma_{\mathfrak{Q}}
is a product of the subdivisions along
\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime}=\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime}
.
The exceptional fibers arising from these subdivisions are product of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s, ensuring the desired structure.
∎

A
compactified abelian fibration

\overline{A}\to B
is a log alteration of a log abelian scheme
\mathsf{LogAb}\to B
which is of Deligne-Mumford type over
B
. For the definition of log abelian schemes, we refer to
[
36
]
.

Proposition 3.10
.

\widetilde{\pi}:\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}\to B
is a compactified abelian fibration.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{LogPic}_{C}
denote the logarithmic Picard group as introduced in
2.1
. The map
\widetilde{\pi}
factors as

\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}\to\mathsf{LogPic}_{C}\times_{B}\mathsf{LogPic}_{C}\to B\,,

where the first map is a log modification, and the second map is a log abelian scheme by
[
50
, Theorem 4.15.7]
. Therefore
\widetilde{\pi}
is a compactified abelian fibration.
∎

3.3
Direct images of line bundles under hyperplane subdivisions

We first consider the local situation.
Let
X
be an affine toric variety. We write
N,M
for the character and cocharacter lattice of its torus respectively, and we write
\Sigma_{X}
for its fan, which is a rational polyhedral cone in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
. To simplify the presentation, we assume in the rest of this subsection that
\Sigma_{X}
is full dimensional in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
.
5
5

5

The assumption can be safely omitted, as otherwise
X
splits as a product
X^{\prime}\times T
of a toric variety
X^{\prime}
that satisfies this hypothesis with a torus
T
, and any construction
C(X)
we perform on
X
in this subsection will also split as
C(X^{\prime})\times T
.

Definition 3.11
.

A toric blowup
g:\widetilde{X}\to X
is called a
subdivision by a hyperplane
if it is the blowup corresponding to the subdivision of
\Sigma_{X}
by a hyperplane: there is a
u\in M
and
\Sigma_{\widetilde{X}}
is the union of the two cones
\sigma_{+}:=\Sigma_{X}\cap\{v\in N_{\mathbb{R}}:u(v)\geq 0\}
and
\sigma_{:}=\Sigma_{X}\cap\{v\in N_{\mathbb{R}}:u(v)\leq 0\}
.
Moreover, a subdivision by a hyperplane is called
saturated
if
g:\widetilde{X}\to X
has reduced fibers.

A subdivision by a hyperplane is, equivalently, the domains of linearity of some convex (down) piecewise linear function
\phi
whose bend locus is the hyperplane
u=0
. In terms of
\phi
, the subdivision
g:\widetilde{X}\to X
has reduced fibers if and only if the value of
\phi(v)
is
0
or
\pm 1
on the primitive vector of every ray of
\Sigma_{X}
– see the discussion in
[
50
, Section 4.4]
.

Example 3.12
.

Our primary example of interest is the Atiyah flop for
X=\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}[x,y,z,w]/(xy-zw)
. The fan
\Sigma_{\widetilde{X}}:=\widetilde{\Sigma}
of
\widetilde{X}
is the cone over a square
\Pi
subdivided into two unimodular triangles:

w_{00}=(0,0,1)
w_{10}=(1,0,1)
w_{01}=(0,1,1)
w_{11}=(1,1,1)
e_{2}
e_{1}

(21)

These triangles are the domains of linearity of the piecewise linear function
\phi:=\min{(e_{1},e_{2})}
.

The fibers of a saturated subdivision by a hyperplane are points, except for the fiber over the fixed point of
X
, which is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. We refer to this
\mathbb{P}^{1}
as the
exceptional
fiber. If we translate
\phi
by a linear function so that its values are
0
on the separating hyperplane,
\phi
is naturally an element of
u^{\perp}\cap M
, which is the cocharacter lattice of the exceptional
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. If
\phi
is saturated, it becomes a generator of
\mathsf{Pic}(\mathbb{P}^{1})
.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
g:\widetilde{X}\to X
be a saturated subdivision by a hyperplane with the exceptional fiber
\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\widetilde{X}
. Then the restriction map
\mathsf{Pic}(\widetilde{X})\to\mathsf{Pic}(\mathbb{P}^{1})
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

The Picard group of
\widetilde{X}
is isomorphic to the quotient of the group of piecewise linear functions on the fan
\widetilde{\Sigma}:=\Sigma_{\widetilde{X}}
by the group of linear functions,

\mathsf{Pic}(\widetilde{X})=\mathsf{PL}(\widetilde{\Sigma})/M

A piecewise linear function on
\widetilde{\Sigma}
is given by a pair of vectors
u_{1},u_{2}\in M
, such that
u_{1}=u_{2}
along the separating hyperplane
\{u=0\}
. Translating by
u_{2}
, the piecewise linear function can be uniquely represented by the function
0,u_{1}-u_{2}
. But
u_{1}-u_{2}
is an element of
u^{\perp}\cap M
which is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
and naturally identified with
\mathsf{Pic}(\mathbb{P}^{1}
).
∎

In fact, when
g
is saturated, the convex piecewise linear function
\phi
when normalized to be
0
along one of the cones
\sigma_{+}
or
\sigma_{-}
must have value
-1
along the primitive vectors of the rays of the other cone that do not belong to the separating hyperplane, i.e. it corresponds to the generator
\mathcal{O}(-1)
of
\mathsf{Pic}(\mathbb{P}^{1}
), i.e.
\mathsf{Pic}(\mathbb{P}^{1})=\mathbb{Z}\phi
.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
X
be an affine toric variety and let
g:\widetilde{X}\to X
be a separated subdivision by a hyperplane. If
L
is a line bundle on
\widetilde{X}
whose restriction to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
is
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(m)
for some
m\geq-1
, then we have
H^{i}(\widetilde{X},L)=0
for
i\geq 1
. Moreover, we have
R^{i}g_{*}L=0
for all
i>0
.

Proof.

Let us write
\Sigma:=\Sigma_{X},\widetilde{\Sigma}=\Sigma_{\widetilde{X}}
, and recall that there is a vector
u\in M
such that
\widetilde{\Sigma}
is the union of the two cones

\sigma_{+}=\{v\in\Sigma:\left\langle u,v\right\rangle\geq 0\},\sigma_{-}=\{v\in\Sigma:\left\langle u,v\right\rangle\leq 0\}

meeting along the separating hyperplane
\{u=0\}
. They are the domains of linearity of the convex down function
\phi
, which we normalize to be
0
on
\sigma_{-}
. Furthermore, the supports of
\Sigma
and
\widetilde{\Sigma}
are the same, and we may harmlessly identify them with
\Sigma
itself.

By Lemma
3.13
, the line bundle
L
corresponds to the piecewise function
\phi_{m}:=m\cdot\phi
for
m\leq 1
. For
w\in M
, set

Z_{w}=\{v\in\Sigma:\left\langle w,v\right\rangle\leq\phi_{m}(v)\}

where
\Sigma
is the support of
\widetilde{\Sigma}
, or, equivalently, the fan of
X
. As in
6
6

6

We use the opposite convention regarding the sign of the Weil divisor associated to a piecewise linear function, hence the reverse inequalities.

[
28
, Section 3.5]
, we have

H^{i}(\widetilde{X},L)\cong\bigoplus_{w\in M}H^{i}(\Sigma,\Sigma-Z_{w})\,.

We describe the topology of
\Sigma-Z_{w}
. Since the origin
v=0
is always contained in
Z_{w}
, the complement of
Z_{w}
deformation retracts onto its intersection with the polytopal complex obtained by slicing
\widetilde{\Sigma}
at “height 1”, i.e. with the intersection of
\widetilde{\Sigma}
with the level set of a generic linear function
L:\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
that takes each ray of
\Sigma
to a positive number. We denote this polytopal complex by
\Pi=\widetilde{\Sigma}\cap L^{-1}(1)
. The possible topological types of the sets
\Pi-Z_{w}
are highly constrained. For example, when
g
is the Atiyah flop,
\Pi
is a square, and the possible topological types depend on whether
w
dominates
\phi_{m}
or not on the vertices of
\Pi
. Degenerate cases are allowed, where a two-dimensional cell collapses to a lower-dimensional boundary cell. We illustrate the possible topological types below, with the regions
Z_{w}\cap\Pi
highlighted in red:

\leq
>
>
\leq
\leq
>
>
>
\leq
>
\leq
>
>
\leq
\leq
>

In general, for
m\leq 0
, the function
m\phi
is convex up, and hence the sets

\Sigma-Z_{w}=\{v\in\Sigma:\left\langle w,v\right\rangle>\phi_{m}(v)\}

are convex. In the case
m=1
,
\Sigma-Z_{w}
is not necessarily convex, but its intersection with either piece
\Pi_{\pm}=\Pi\cap\sigma_{\pm}
is convex, as it is the intersection of a polyhedron with a half-space. Furthermore, the intersection of

(\Sigma-Z_{w})\cap\Pi_{+}\cap\Pi_{-}=(\Sigma-Z_{w})\cap\{u=0\}\cap\Pi

is the set

\{v\in\Pi\cap\{u=0\}:\left\langle w,v\right\rangle>0\}

which is convex and connected (albeit perhaps empty). Therefore, in any case, the complement of
Z_{w}
is contractible. Furthermore, it is connected unless
Z_{w}
contains the separating hyperplane
\{u=0\}
, in which case it has exactly two components.

From the long exact sequence of relative cohomology

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\Sigma,\Sigma-Z_{w})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\Sigma)=\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\Sigma-Z_{w})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\Sigma,\Sigma-Z_{w})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\,,}

we find that
H^{i}(\Sigma,\Sigma-Z_{w})
is
0
for all
i\geq 2
, and
0
for
i=1
unless the complement of
Z_{w}
has two components, in which case

H^{i}(\Sigma,\Sigma-Z_{w})\cong\mathbb{C}\,.

Now, let
w
be a character for which
Z_{w}
contains the separating hyperplane
\{u=0\}
(which can only happen for
m=1
, as otherwise we’ve seen that
\Sigma-Z_{w}
is convex). By our normalization hypothesis on
\phi
we have
\phi=0
on
\sigma_{-}
and
\phi\leq 0
on
\sigma_{+}
. This means that
w
is

•

>0
on some ray of
\sigma_{-}

•

\leq 0
on the separating hyperplane
\{u=0\}
.

•

>\phi
on
\sigma_{+}
.

Let
v
be the primitive vector along a ray of
\sigma_{+}
on which
\phi(v)\neq 0
. By the hypothesis that the subdivision is saturated,
\phi(v)=-1
. Now, the linear function
w
has negative slope in the direction connecting the separating hyperplane with
v
, as it has to rise away from the separating hyperplane towards
\sigma_{-}
. But
w
also takes integral values on integral points, so if its value is
\leq 0
along the separating hyperplane, it must be
\leq-1
along
v
. Therefore, for
m=1
, it is impossible to find such a
w
.

Therefore, if
m\leq 1
, for any
w
,
\Sigma-Z_{w}
is contractible and connected.
From the long exact sequence, it follows that
H^{i}(\widetilde{X},L)=0
for all
i>0
.

For the second claim, since
X
is affine, Grothendieck’s spectral sequence gives
H^{0}(X,R^{i}g_{*}L)\cong H^{i}(\widetilde{X},L)
. We have just seen that this group vanishes for
i>0
. Since
R^{i}g_{*}L
is a coherent sheaf on the affine variety
X
, hence determined by its global sections, it also vanishes for
i>0
.
∎

Now, we discuss the global situation. Let
f:Y\to X
be a log modification. Generalizing Definition
3.11
, we say that
f
is a
saturated subdivision by hyperplanes
if it is étale locally pulled back from a saturated subdivision by hyperplanes of toric varieties.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
f:Y\to X
be a a saturated subdivision by hyperplanes between log smooth varieties, and let
L
be a line bundle on
Y
. Assume that the restriction of
L
to any fiber of
f
has vanishing higher cohomology. Then we have
Rf_{*}L\cong f_{*}L
.

Proof.

We prove the higher direct images
R^{i}f_{*}L
vanish for all
i>0
. By Krull’s intersection theorem, the statement can be checked formally locally on
X
, so we can assume
X
is the spectrum of a complete Noetherian local ring. Since
X
is log smooth, and since
Y
is a log blowup of
X
, we have a Cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

with
Z
a toric variety,
g
a toric blowup – which by hypothesis must be a saturated subdivision along a hyperplane –, and the map
p
étale. By
[
50
, Lemma 4.4.12.2]
, the Picard group of
Y
and the Picard group of
\widetilde{Z}
are isomorphic, and both coincide with the Picard group of an exceptional
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. We may thus identify
L
with a line bundle on
\widetilde{Z}
. By Proposition
3.14
, since the cohomology of
L
vanishes on the fibers of
f
, it vanishes on the exceptional fibers of
g
as well. Thus we have
R^{i}g_{*}L=0
for
i>0
. Since
X
and
Z
are formally locally isomorphic, we have

R^{i}f_{*}L\cong(R^{i}g_{*}L)_{\widehat{p(X)}}=0\,,i>0\,,

which gives the result.
∎

For our purposes, we need the following strengthening of the previous result.

Proposition 3.16
.

Let
f:Y\to X
be a log modification between log smooth varieties which is locally the pullback of a product of saturated subdivisions by hyperplanes. Let
L
be a line bundle on
Y
such that for each closed point
x\in X
,
H^{i}(f^{-1}(x),L|_{f^{-1}(x)})=0
for all
i>0
. Then
Rf_{*}L\cong f_{*}L
.

Proof.

By the same argument in Proposition
3.15
, we reduce to the toric case. Then the result follows by combining Proposition
3.15
with the Künneth formula.
∎

3.4
Proof of Theorem
1.2

We show that the canonical extension of the Poincaré line bundle on the birational model constructed in Section
3.2
provides a desingularization of Arinkin’s kernel (
[
6
]
). In contrast to Arinkin’s approach, our construction offers a direct description of the kernel, which will be a crucial input in Section
5
.

The notion of Cohen-Macaulay sheaves can be expressed in terms of the dualizing complex. Denote by
\mathrm{D}^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}(X)
the bounded derived category of coherent sheaves on
X
, and let
\omega^{\bullet}_{X}\in\mathrm{D}^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}(X)
be a dualizing complex for
X
. We normalize
\omega^{\bullet}_{X}
so that its stalk at a generic point of
X
has nontrivial cohomology only in degree
0
. When
X
is Gorenstein, the dualizing complex is an invertible sheaf.
Consider the duality functor

\mathbb{D}:\mathrm{D}^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{D}^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}(X),\,\mathcal{E}\mapsto R\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{E},\omega^{\bullet}_{X})\,.

A coherent sheaf
\mathcal{E}
on
X
is called
Cohen-Macaulay
of codimension
d
if and only if

h^{i}(\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{E}))=0,\,i\neq d\,.

(22)

A Cohen-Macaulay sheaf of codimension zero is called
maximal
. The following extension property will be used frequently (
[
31
, Theorem 5.10.5]
).

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a maximal Cohen-Macaulay sheaf on
X
and let
Z\subset X
be a closed subscheme with codimension
\geq 2
. Let
j:X\setminus Z\to X
be an open embedding. Then the canonical morphism
\mathcal{F}\to j_{*}j^{*}\mathcal{F}
is an isomorphism.

We go back to the construction in Section
3.2
. By Theorem
1.2
(a), the codimension of the open embedding (
17
) is two and the total space
\overline{J}^{(2)}_{C}
is smooth. Consequently, the Poincare line bundle
\mathcal{P}
on
J_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}\cup\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}J_{C}
extends to the unique line bundle:

\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathrm{Pic}(\overline{J}^{(2)}_{C})\,.

(23)

We call
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
the
extended Poincaré line bundle
.

Before presenting the proof of Theorem
1.2
, we explain the connection to Arinkin’s work. By Arinkin
[
6
]
(see also
[
43
]
), the Poincaré line bundle
\mathcal{P}
admits a unique maximal Cohen-Macaulay extension:

\overline{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathrm{Coh}(\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C})\,.

(24)

This extension is obtained in loc.cit. from flat descent of a maximal Cohen-Macaulay sheaf on the isotropic Hilbert scheme of points. Theorem
1.2
can be thought of as a desingularization of Arinkin’s kernel.

Corollary 3.18
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
be the unique extension of the Poincaré line bundle (
23
) and let
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
be the maximal Cohen-Macaulay sheaf (
24
). Then we have

Rf_{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\cong\overline{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathrm{D}^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}(\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C})\,.

Proof.

By Theorem
1.2
,
f_{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\cong Rf_{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
is a maximal Cohen-Macaulay sheaf which restricts to
\mathcal{P}
on
J_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}\cup\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}J_{C}
. By Theorem
1.2
and Lemma
3.17
, we have
f_{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\cong\overline{\mathcal{P}}
.
∎

In the remainder of this section, we prove Theorem
1.2
(c). We begin by providing a modular description of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
. By pulling back the universal curve over
\mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}
along the morphism
\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}\to\mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}
in (
20
), we obtain a semi-stable curve:

\widehat{C}\to\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}\,.

(25)

Proposition 3.19
.

Let
C_{1},C_{2}
be the two universal quasi-stable curves over
\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}
and
\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}
be the two universal line bundles on
C_{1},C_{2}
. Let
\widehat{C}\to\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}
be the semi-stable curve (
25
). Then the unique extension (
23
) has the form
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\cong\langle\mathcal{L}_{1}|_{\widehat{C}},\mathcal{L}_{2}|_{\widehat{C}}\rangle
.

Proof.

By Theorem
1.2
(a) and Lemma
3.17
, it is enough to show that
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
restricts to the Poincare line bundle
\mathcal{P}
on
J_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}\cup\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}J_{C}
which is immediate from Definition
3.1
.
∎

Theorem
1.2
(b) is the consequence of Proposition
3.19
.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
(c).

A geometric log point
x\in\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}
corresponds to a quadruple
(C_{1},L_{1},C_{2},L_{2})
of two quasistable log curves with stabilization
C
, and stable line bundles
L_{i}
on
C_{i}
. By Proposition
3.9
(b), the fiber of
\overline{J}^{(2)}_{C}
over
x
is a product of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
s, one for each edge
e
of the dual graph of
C
that is subdivided simultaneously in the dual graphs of both
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
. It will be clear from our argument that we may work one such edge at a time, so we assume that one edge
e
has been subdivided, and
f^{-1}(x)=\mathbb{P}^{1}
. We note that the curves
C_{1},C_{2}
are not isomorphic as log curves over
x
, but their underlying schemes are isomorphic, obtained by replacing the node of
C
corresponding to
e
by a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. We may thus write

C_{1}=C_{2}=C^{\prime}\cup_{\{n_{1},n_{2}\}}\mathbb{P}^{1}

with
n_{1},n_{2}
two nodes, and
\mathbb{P}^{1}
the unique unstable component. Since
C_{1},C_{2}
are curves over a geometric point, we can represent each line bundle
L_{i}
as a divisor

D^{\prime}_{i}+p_{i}

where
D^{\prime}_{i}
is supported on the smooth locus of
C^{\prime}
and
p_{i}
is a smooth point of the unstable
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
By construction, the curve
\widehat{C}
restricted over
f^{-1}(x)=\mathbb{P}^{1}
is the blowup of
C_{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
at
(n_{1},0),(n_{2},\infty)
, where
n_{1},n_{2}
are the two nodes of the exceptional
\mathbb{P}^{1}
in
C_{1}
. Now, the pullbacks of
D_{i}^{\prime}
and of
p_{i}
remain in the smooth locus
C^{\prime}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
and its complement respectively. Therefore,
D_{i}^{\prime}
stay disjoint from the
p_{j}
and we have

\left\langle D_{i}^{\prime},p_{j}\right\rangle=0

for
i,j=1,2
. Similarily,
\left\langle D_{1}^{\prime},D_{2}^{\prime}\right\rangle
can be calculated inside any proper subvariety of
\widehat{C}
that contains the smooth locus of
C^{\prime}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, for example inside
C^{\prime}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, while
\left\langle p_{1},p_{2}\right\rangle
can be calculated inside the complement of the smooth locus of
C^{\prime}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, which is the blowup
Bl(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})
of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
at
(n_{1},0)
and
(n_{2},\infty)
. Since
C^{\prime}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
D_{1}^{\prime},D_{2}^{\prime}
are pulled back from a point,
\left\langle D_{1}^{\prime},D_{2}^{\prime}\right\rangle=0
.

V_{0}
E_{0}
E_{\infty}
V_{\infty}
H_{1}
H_{2}

Figure 1:
The blowup of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
at
(n_{1},0),(n_{2},\infty)
and its torus invariant divisors.

On the other hand,

\mathsf{Pic}(Bl(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}))=\mathbb{Z}^{4}

is generated by the classes of two horizontal divisors
H_{1},H_{2}
, and four vertical divisors
V_{0},V_{\infty},E_{0},E_{\infty}
over
0
and
\infty
with relations

H_{1}+E_{0}=H_{2}+E_{\infty},V_{0}+E_{0}=V_{\infty}+E_{\infty}\,.

Up to renaming, we may arrange so that

H_{1}\cdot E_{\infty}=H_{2}\cdot V_{\infty}=0\,.

In our naming convention, we are thinking of
H_{1}
as the strict transform of
n_{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
H_{2}
as the strict transform of
n_{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{2}
, and of
E_{0},E_{\infty}
as the exceptional divisors of the blowup
\widehat{C}
of
C_{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. By construction,
p_{1}
has relative degree
1
over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
and intersects
V_{0},V_{\infty}
transversely, while
p_{2}
has relative degree
1
and intersects
E_{0},E_{\infty}
transversely. A simple calculation in
\mathsf{Pic}(Bl(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}))
shows that these properties uniquely characterize
p_{1}
as the line bundle equivalent to
H_{2}+E_{\infty}
while
p_{2}
as
H_{1}+V_{\infty}
. Then, we have

\left\langle H_{2}+E_{\infty},H_{1}+V_{\infty}\right\rangle=f_{*}(E_{\infty}\cdot V_{\infty})=[\infty]

which has degree
1
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

In general, we conclude that

\left\langle\mathcal{L}_{1}|_{\widehat{C}},\mathcal{L}_{2}|_{\widehat{C}}\right\rangle|_{f^{-1}(x)}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(1)\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(1)\,,

(26)

where we identified
f^{-1}(x)\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
using Proposition
3.9
.

We may now prove
R^{i}f_{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}=0
for
i>0
. By Proposition
3.16
, it is enough to show that for any
x\in\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}
, we have
H^{i}(f^{-1}(x),\widetilde{\mathcal{P}})=0
for all
i>0
. But this follows from (
26
).

We show that
f_{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
is a Cohen-Macaulay sheaf using the cohomological criteria (
22
). By the Grothendieck duality along
f
(see, e.g.,
[
54
]
), we have the equivalence
Rf_{*}\circ\mathbb{D}_{\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}}\cong\mathbb{D}_{\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}}\circ Rf_{*}
.
Since
f
is birational, there is no degree shift in this equivalence.
Applying this and (a), we have

\mathbb{D}(f_{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}})\cong\mathbb{D}(Rf_{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}})\cong Rf_{*}\mathbb{D}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}})\,.

The total space
\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}
is smooth by Theorem
1.2
(a), and hence we can express
\mathbb{D}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}})\cong\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}\otimes\omega_{\overline{J}^{(2)}_{C}}
. The dualizing line bundle
\omega_{\overline{J}^{(2)}_{C}}
restricts trivially to each contracted
\mathbb{P}^{1}
by the adjunction formula. Using the Künneth formula, for any
x\in\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}
, we have

H^{i}(f^{-1}(x),\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}\otimes\omega_{\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}})=H^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{P}^{1},\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee})=0,\,i>0\,,

where the vanishing follows because
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}
has degree
-1
on each
\mathbb{P}^{1}
by (
26
). Therefore, by Proposition
3.16
, the complex
\mathbb{D}(f_{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}})
is a coherent sheaf. By (
22
), the pushforward
f_{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
is a maximal Cohen-Macaulay sheaf on
\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}
.
∎

4
Todd class of a residue sheaf

4.1
Residue sheaf of a semistable morphism

We recall the local structure of semistable morphisms (Definition
3.8
). Let
f:X\to S
be a semistable morphism with
\dim X=n,\dim S=l
. Étale locally on the domain, there exists non-negative integers
n
and
l
, a partition
A
of
n
into
l
parts
n_{i}
, and a chart for
f
of the form

\prod_{i=1}^{l}\mathbb{A}^{n_{i}}\to\prod_{i=1}^{l}\mathbb{A}^{1}\,,\text{ given by }t_{i}=\prod_{\alpha\in n_{i}}x_{\alpha}

(27)

by
[
15
, Theorem 16]
and the proof therein.

For a semistable morphism
f:X\to S
, the sequence

0\to f^{*}\Omega_{Y}^{1}\to\Omega_{X}^{1}\to\Omega_{f}^{1}\to 0

(28)

is exact. Consequently, the Todd class of the virtual tangent bundle associated with
f
is the dual of the Todd class of
\Omega_{f}^{1}
. For a morphism
f:X\to S
between log schemes, one can associate the logarithmic cotangent sheaf
\Omega_{f,\log}^{1}
(
[
38
, (1.7)]
).
When
f
is log smooth, the logarithmic cotangent sheaf
\Omega_{f,\log}^{1}
is locally free.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
f:X\to S
be a semistable morphism. Then the sequence

0\to\Omega_{f}^{1}\to\Omega^{1}_{f,\log}\to\Omega^{1}_{f,\log}/\Omega^{1}_{f}\to 0

(29)

is exact. In this case, we call
\mathcal{R}_{f}:=\Omega_{f,\log}^{1}/\Omega^{1}_{f}
the
residue sheaf
of
f
.

Proof.

By the definition of
\Omega_{f,\log}^{1}
, there exists a canonical morphism
\Omega_{f}^{1}\to\Omega_{f,\log}^{1}
. The injectivity of the above morphism can be checked étale locally. On each étale local chart, it follows from (
27
).
∎

We use the tropicalization map (
19
) for log schemes.
For a morphism
f:X\to S
between log schemes, consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{X}[S]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

(30)
