where the middle square is the fiber diagram. We call
\mathcal{A}_{X}[S]
the
relative Artin fan
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
f:X\to S
be a semistable morphism. In the diagram (
30
), the natural morphism
\phi_{f}^{*}\mathcal{R}_{g}\to\mathcal{R}_{f}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Since
f
is flat (
[
38
, Corollary 4.5]
) and log smooth, the morphism
\phi_{f}
is strict and smooth. Thus, the natural morphism
\Omega_{\phi_{f}}^{1}\to\Omega^{1}_{\phi_{f},\log}
is an isomorphism and the result follows from Lemma
4.1
.∎

We work with piecewise linear and piecewise polynomial functions on smooth and log smooth schemes; see
[
33
, Section 2.5]
. For an algebraic log stack
X
, a
(strict) piecewise linear function
on
X
is a global section
H^{0}(X,\overline{\mathsf{M}}_{X}^{\mathrm{gp}})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
and a
(strict) piecewise polynomial
on
X
is a global section
H^{0}(X,\,\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{Q}}\overline{\mathsf{M}}^{\mathrm{gp}}_{X})
.
For
\alpha\in H^{0}(X,\mathsf{M}_{X}^{\mathrm{gp}})
, the
\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{X}
-torsor
\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\times}(\alpha)
is defined as the preimage of
\alpha
under the natural map
\mathsf{M}_{X}^{\mathrm{gp}}\to\overline{\mathsf{M}}_{X}^{\mathrm{gp}}
. The associated line bundle, denoted by
\mathcal{O}_{X}(\alpha)
, is obtained by gluing along the infinity section.

We now proceed to calculate the Todd class of the residue sheaf of a semistable morphism. From Lemma
4.2
, it follows that to compute
\mathcal{R}_{f}
, it is sufficient to perform the calculation for the map
\mathcal{A}_{X}\to\mathcal{A}_{S}
. By
[
48
, Theorem 3.3.1]
, we have a graded ring isomorphism

\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\mathcal{A}_{X})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathsf{PP}(\Sigma_{X})\,,

which reflects the fact that
\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\mathcal{A}_{X})
satisfies étale descent. As a consequence, the calculation of the Todd class can be carried out stratum by stratum.

For a Chern root
x
, we denote by
\mathrm{td}(x)
the associated Todd class and the dual of the Todd class by

\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(x):=\frac{x}{e^{x}-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{B_{k}}{k!}x^{k}

where
B_{k}
is the
k
-th
Bernoulli number
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
n,l
be positive integers, and
A
be a partition of
n
into
l
parts
n_{i}
. Consider the map
f:\mathcal{A}^{n}\to\mathcal{A}^{l}
induced by the semistable map
\mathbb{A}^{n}\to\mathbb{A}^{l}
defined according to the partition
A
, as described in (
27
). The Todd class of the residue sheaf of
f
is expressed as

(\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{f}))^{-1}=\prod_{i=1}^{l}\frac{\prod_{\alpha\in n_{i}}\mathrm{td}(\delta_{\alpha})}{\mathrm{td}(\sum_{\alpha\in n_{i}}\delta_{\alpha})}\in\mathsf{PP}(\Sigma_{\mathbb{A}^{n}})

where
\delta_{\alpha}
is the piecewise linear function corresponding to the normal bundle of the
\alpha
-th coordinate hyperplane.

Proof.

We begin by analyzing the case
l=1
. Let
\mathcal{D}
denote the origin in
\mathcal{A}^{1}
, and
\mathcal{D}_{i}
denote the coordinate axes in
\mathcal{A}^{n}
. Denote by
\delta
,
\delta_{i}
the Chern classes of their respective normal bundles. These classes correspond to piecewise linear functions, where
\delta_{i}
has slope
1
along the ray associated with
\mathcal{D}_{i}
and slope
0
elsewhere. The pullback relationship for
f
gives:

f^{*}\delta=\sum_{i}\delta_{i}^{\prime},.

By (
28
) and Lemma
4.1
, we get a short exact sequence

0\to f^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{D}}\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{D}_{i}}\to\mathcal{R}_{f}\to 0\,.

Therefore, we deduce:

\mathrm{td}(\mathcal{R}_{f})=\frac{\mathrm{td}(\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{D}_{i}})}{\mathrm{td}(f^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{D}})}=\frac{\prod\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\delta_{i})^{-1}}{\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\sum\delta_{i})^{-1}}\,.

(31)

where the last equality follows from the usual exact sequence
0\to\mathcal{O}(-D)\to\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{O}_{D}\to 0
for Cartier divisors
D
.
For the general case, the map
f
decomposes as a product of
l
maps,
f_{i}:\mathcal{A}^{n_{i}}\to\mathcal{A}^{1}
. Thus, the Todd class of the relative cotangent bundle
\mathrm{td}(\mathcal{R}_{f})
is given as the product of the formula for the special case (
31
) applied to each factor.
∎

Now we state the general formula for the Todd class of the residue sheaf. To do so, we first introduce some notation (see
[
57
, Section 4]
). For a cone
\sigma\in\Sigma_{S}
, let
j_{\sigma}:S_{\sigma}\to S
denote the corresponding monodromy torsor. Similarly, for a cone in
\Sigma_{X}
, corresponding to a cell
c
in the generic fiber of
\Sigma_{X}\to\Sigma_{S}
, let
i_{c}:X_{c}\to X
denote the corresponding monodromy torsor, and let

f_{c}:X_{c}\to S_{\sigma}

the induced map.
For a ray
\rho
in
\Sigma_{S}
or
\Sigma_{X}
, we write
\delta_{\rho}
for the piecewise linear function with slope
1
along
\rho
and
0
along the other rays; we identify
\delta_{\rho}
implicitly with its image in the Chow ring of
S
or
X
, which is the first Chern class of the normal bundle of
D_{\rho}
in
S
or
X
. For a cone
\sigma
, we write
\sigma(1)
for its set of rays.

To express the Todd class in terms of the strata algebra, we proceed as follows. Given a polynomial
P_{c}
on a cone
c
of
\Sigma_{X}
, we define
\mathsf{prin}_{c}[P_{c}]
to be the sum of terms in
P_{c}
which are formally divisible by

\delta_{c}:=\prod_{r\in c(1)}\delta_{r}\,.

where
c(1)
is the set of rays in the cone
c
. Then, we define:

\left\lfloor{P}\right\rfloor_{c}=\frac{\mathsf{prin}_{c}[P_{c}]}{\delta_{c}}\,.

Additionally, we write
G_{c}
for the monodromy group of
c
. Finally, write
\Sigma_{f}:\Sigma_{X}\to\Sigma_{S}
for the induced map of cone stacks.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
f:X\to S
be a semistable morphism. The Todd class of the residue sheaf of
f
is given by

\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{f})^{-1}=\sum_{c\in\Sigma_{X}}\frac{(i_{c})_{*}}{|G_{c}|}\prod_{\rho\in\Sigma_{f}(c)(1)}\left\lfloor{\frac{\prod_{r\in c(1),\Sigma_{f}(r)=\rho}\mathrm{td}(\delta_{r})}{\mathrm{td}(\delta_{\rho})}}\right\rfloor_{c}\,.

Proof.

We prove the formula in the ring of piecewise polynomials:

\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{f})^{-1}=\left(\prod_{\rho\in\Sigma_{f}(c)(1)}\frac{\prod_{r\in c(1),\Sigma_{f}(r)=\rho}\mathrm{td}(\delta_{r})}{\mathrm{td}(\delta_{\rho})}\right)_{c}\in\mathsf{PP}(\Sigma_{X})\,.

(32)

Here,
(-)_{c}
indicates that the piecewise polynomial function is determined on the cone
c
of
\Sigma_{X}
by truncating the power series

\prod_{\rho\in\Sigma_{f}(c)(1)}\frac{\prod_{r\in c(1),\Sigma_{f}(r)=\rho}\mathrm{td}(\delta_{r})}{\mathrm{td}(\delta_{\rho})}.

at codimension greater than
\dim X
. It is implicitly understood that these functions must agree on intersections of cones
c\cap c^{\prime}
.
The formula agrees with Lemma
4.3
on each cone because

\delta_{\rho}=\sum_{r\mapsto\rho}\delta_{r}\,.

This ensures that the Todd class satisfies the statement of the proposition ètale locally on
X
. But the formula (
32
) is invariant under automorphisms of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n}
, and, by direct computation, the formula defined on each cone agrees with its restriction to any face,
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n}
. Since piecewise polynomials satisfy étale local descent
[
48
]
, the formula descends to
\mathsf{PP}(\Sigma_{X})
.

Once the formula (
32
) is established in the ring of piecewise polynomials, translating it into a statement about normally decorated strata classes becomes straightforward, see
[
33
, Lemma 40]
.
∎

Example 4.5
.

For the morphism
\mathcal{A}^{2}\to\mathcal{A}^{1}
corresponding to
(x,y)\to xy
, the formula above reduces to Mumford’s formula for the Todd class of a node (
[
53
, Section 5]
).

4.2
Residue sheaf for compactified Jacobians

The Todd class of
\mathcal{R}_{\pi}
for compactified Jacobians can be expressed in terms of tautological classes. Let
B:=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
and
\pi:\overline{J}_{g,n}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
be a relative compactified Jacobian for some non-degenerate stability condition. For
1\leq h\leq g
, denote by
\Gamma_{h}
the stable graph consisting of a single vertex of genus
g-h
with
h
loops. Define

i_{h}:\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g-h,n+2h}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}

as the morphism induced by gluing the
n+i
-th marking with the
n+h+i
-th marking for
1\leq i\leq h
. Let
\Gamma_{h}^{\prime}
be the quasi-stable graph obtained by subdividing each edge of
\Gamma_{h}
. Consider the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{g-h,n+2h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{h}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{g,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g-h,n+2h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}\,.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{h}}

(33)

Here, the right square is a Cartesian diagram, and the morphism
\overline{J}_{g-h,n+2h}\to\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{h}}
is induced by Lemma
2.4
.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
\pi:\overline{J}_{C}\to B=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
be the projection. Let
\jmath_{h}:\overline{J}_{g-h,n+2h}\to\overline{J}_{g,n}
be the composition in (
33
). Then we have

\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\pi})^{-1}-1=\sum_{h=1}^{g}\frac{(-1)^{h}}{h!2^{h}}\jmath_{h*}\Bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{h}\left(\sum_{k_{i}=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k_{i}}B_{2k_{i}}}{(2k_{i})!}\frac{\alpha_{i}^{2k_{i}-1}+\beta_{i}^{2k_{i}-1}}{\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i}}\right)\Bigg]

with
\alpha_{i}=\psi_{n+i}-\xi_{n+i}+\xi_{n+h+i}
and
\beta_{i}=\psi_{n+h+i}+\xi_{n+i}-\xi_{n+h+i}
.

Proof.

The morphism
\pi:\overline{J}_{g,n}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
satisfies hypothesis in Lemma
4.2
. By Lemma
4.2
we compute the sheaf
\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{\overline{J}[\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}]/\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}},\log}/\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{\overline{J}[\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}]/\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}}}
on each chart and show that they glue.

We consider the morphism (
10
). Let
j_{\Gamma_{\ell}^{\prime}}:\mathfrak{Pic}_{\Gamma^{\prime}_{\ell}}\to\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n}
be the finite morphism. Then the following diagram is cartesian

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{g-\ell,2\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{g,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Pic}_{\Gamma_{\ell}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath_{g-\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\Gamma_{\ell}^{\prime}}}

For each
i
, there exists a quasi-stable vertex
v_{i}
and two edges
e=(h_{i},\overline{h}_{i})
and
e=(h_{i}^{\prime},\overline{h}^{\prime}_{i})
connecting
v_{i}
and the unique stable vertex
v_{0}
where
h_{i},h_{i}^{\prime}
are half-edges at
v_{0}
.
By Lemma
2.2
,
\varphi
is smooth, so the normal bundle of
j_{g-\ell}
is isomorphic to the pullback of the normal bundle of
j_{\Gamma_{\ell}^{\prime}}
. Therefore, the Chern roots of the normal bundle of
j_{\Gamma_{\ell}^{\prime}}
are given by
\psi_{h_{i}}+\psi_{\overline{h}_{i}}
,
\psi_{h_{i}^{\prime}}+\psi_{\overline{h}_{i}^{\prime}}
for
1\leq i\leq\ell
. By the genus
0
relation
[
8
]
, we have

\psi_{\overline{h}_{i}}=-\xi_{h_{i}}+\xi_{h_{i}^{\prime}}\text{ and }\psi_{\overline{h}_{i}^{\prime}}=\xi_{h_{i}}-\xi_{h_{i}^{\prime}}\,.

(34)

The result now follows from Example
4.5
.
∎

Let
T_{\pi}^{\mathrm{vir}}
be the virtual tangent bundle of
\pi:\overline{J}_{C}\to B
. To relate the Todd class of
T_{\pi}^{\mathrm{vir}}
and the Todd class of
\mathcal{R}_{\pi}
, we use the following adaptation of Faltings-Chai
[
26
]
.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
\pi:\overline{J}_{C}\to B
be a compactified Jacobian. Then we have
\Omega^{1}_{\pi,\log}\cong\pi^{*}\mathbb{E}
where
\mathbb{E}=\pi_{*}\Omega^{1}_{\pi,\log}
is the Hodge bundle.

Proof.

By
[
26
, Chapter VI, Theorem 1.1]
(also
[
56
, Lemma 4.3.10]
), the corresponding identity was obtained for certain compactified abelian schemes. After pulling back to
B
,
\overline{J}_{C}
and the compactified abelian scheme have a common refinement. Since log modifications are log étale,
\Omega^{1}_{\pi,\log}
does not change under log modification. Hence we get the result.
∎

By combining (
28
), (
29
) and Proposition
4.7
we have

\mathrm{td}(T_{\pi}^{\mathrm{vir}})=\pi^{*}\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathbb{E})\cdot\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\pi})^{-1}\,.

(35)

4.3
Todd class for the birational model

We consider the projection

\widetilde{\pi}:\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}\to B

where
\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}\to\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}
is a log modification constructed in Definition
3.7
. By Proposition
3.9
(a),
\widetilde{\pi}
is semistable and by Proposition
3.10
, it is a compactified abelian fibration.
We generalize the result in Section
4.2
. By the same argument in Proposition
4.7
we have

\Omega_{\widetilde{\pi},\log}^{1}\cong\widetilde{\pi}^{*}(\mathbb{E}\oplus\mathbb{E})\,.

(36)

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
\pi:\overline{J}_{C}\to B
denote the projection, and let
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}:\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}\to\overline{J}_{C}
represent the two projections. Then the following holds:

\mathrm{td}(T_{\overline{J}^{(2)}_{C}/B})\cdot f^{*}\mathrm{td}(-T_{\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}/B})=\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\widetilde{\pi}})\cdot\pi_{1}^{*}\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\pi})^{-1}\cdot\pi_{2}^{*}\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\pi})^{-1}\,.

(37)

Proof.

Consider the fiber diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{C}\times\overline{J}_{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times B\,,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\times\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{B}}

where
\Delta_{B}:B\to B\times B
is the diagonal.
Since
\overline{J}_{C},B
are smooth and
\pi
is flat, we have

T_{\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}/B}=\pi_{1}^{*}T_{\overline{J}_{C}}+\pi_{2}^{*}T_{\overline{J}_{C}}-2\,\pi^{*}T_{B}=\pi_{1}^{*}T_{\pi}^{\mathrm{vir}}+\pi_{2}^{*}T_{\pi}^{\mathrm{vir}}

in
K^{0}(\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C})
.
Using (
35
), we obtain

\mathrm{td}(T_{\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}/B})=\mathrm{td}(\mathbb{E}^{\oplus 2})^{\vee}\cdot\pi_{1}^{*}\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\pi})^{-1}\cdot\pi_{2}^{*}\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\pi})^{-1}\,.

Finally, by applying Lemma
4.2
and (
36
), we derive the desired result.
∎

Let now
\kappa
be a cone of
\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}
parametrizing two quasistable graphs
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
with stabilization
\Gamma
. Keeping the notations of Corollary
3.6
, a cone
c
of
\widetilde{J}^{(2)}_{C}
lying over
\kappa
has the form

c=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{e\in E^{u}(\Gamma)}\times\prod_{e\in E^{s_{1}\vee s_{2}}(\Gamma)}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}\times\prod_{e\in E^{s_{1}\cap s_{2}(\Gamma)}}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{3}

(38)

where, for
e\in E^{s_{1}\cap s_{2}(\Gamma)}
, the component
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{3}
is either the
\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime}\leq\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime}
or the
\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime}\leq\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime}
piece of the resolution of

\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}\times_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}=\{(\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime},\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime\prime},\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime},\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime\prime}):\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime}+\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime\prime}=\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime}+\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime\prime}\}.

Proposition 4.9
.

As a piecewise polynomial function on
\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}
, the restriction of (
37
) to the cone (
38
) is given by

\prod_{e\in E^{s_{1}\cap s_{2}}(\Gamma)}\frac{\mathrm{td}(\max{(\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime},\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime})}-\min{(\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime},\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime})})\mathrm{td}(\ell_{e})}{\mathrm{td}(\max{(\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime},\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime}))}\mathrm{td}(\ell_{e}-\min{(\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime},\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime})})}

where
\ell_{e}=\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime}+\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime}
is the total length of the edge.

Proof.

The cone
c
is the product of cones
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}
and
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{3}
. Cones of the first two kinds correspond to strata of
\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}
on which the map to
\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}
is an isomorphism, so the relative Todd class is trivial on them. To ease the notation, we assume
\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime}\geq\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime}
. Then, in the third case, the cone and its two projections look as follows:

\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime}=0,x=1
\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime}=\ell_{e}
\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime}=\ell_{e},z=1
\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime}=0
\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime}=\ell_{e},y=1
\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime}=0
\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime}=\ell_{e}
\pi_{2}
\pi_{1}

Let
x,y,z
be the piecewise linear functions on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{3}
with slope
1
along the rays indicated in the diagram, and slope
0
on the other rays. On the cone
c
, formula (
32
) reads

\displaystyle\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\widetilde{\pi}})\cdot\pi_{1}^{*}\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\pi})^{-1}\cdot\pi_{2}^{*}\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\pi})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{td}(x)\mathrm{td}(y)\mathrm{td}(z)}{\mathrm{td}(x+y+z)}\frac{\mathrm{td}(x+y+z)}{\mathrm{td}(x)\mathrm{td}(y+z)}\frac{\mathrm{td}(x+y+z)}{\mathrm{td}(x+y)\mathrm{td}(z)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{td}(y)\mathrm{td}(x+y+z)}{\mathrm{td}(x+y)\mathrm{td}(y+z)}.

Given that
x=\ell_{e}-\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime},\,y=\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime}-\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime}
and
z=\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime}
,
the result follows. It is easy to treat the analogous case where
\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime}\geq\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime}
, which simply interchanges the roles of
\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime}
and
\ell_{e_{1}^{\prime}}
, leading to the claimed formula.
∎

5
Fourier transform

5.1
Derived equivalence of compactified Jacobians

In Section
3
, we construct an extension of Poincaré line bundle. We show that it gives a derived equivalence of compactified Jacobians, following Arinkin’s argument in
[
6
]
(see also
[
43
]
).

Theorem 5.1

(
[
6
]
)
.

Let
B=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
and let
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}
be two non-degenerate stability conditions. Let
\overline{\mathcal{P}}:=f_{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathrm{Coh}(\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon_{1}}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon_{2}})
be pushforward of the extended Poincaré line bundle. Then
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
induces a derived equivalence

\mathfrak{F}:=-\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}:\mathrm{D}^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}(\overline{J}^{\epsilon_{1}}_{C})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{D}^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}(\overline{J}^{\epsilon_{2}}_{C})

which is linear over
\mathrm{D}^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}(B)
with the inverse kernel given by
\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}:=\mathcal{H}om(\overline{\mathcal{P}},\mathcal{O})\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}\omega_{\pi}[g]
.

Proof.

Over
B=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
, the relative Jacobian
J^{\underline{0}}_{C}\to B
is a
\delta
-regular family of semi-abelian schemes (
[
5
, Proposition 3.3]
). By Theorem
1.2
, the kernel
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=f_{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\cong Rf_{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
is a maximal Cohen-Macaulay extension of Poincaré line bundle. Therefore, the argument in
[
6
, Section 7.3]
applies to the family
\overline{J}_{C}\to B
and shows that that
\mathfrak{F}
is a derived equivalence.
∎

For a separated scheme
X
, let
K_{0}(X)
denote the Grothendieck group of coherent sheaves on
X
. We consider the Baum-Fulton-MacPherson homomorphism (
[
29
, Chapter 18]
):

\tau:K_{0}(X)\xrightarrow{}\mathsf{CH}_{*}(X,\mathbb{Q})\,.

(39)

When
X
is smooth, we have
\tau(F)=\mathrm{ch}(F)\cup\mathrm{td}(T_{X})
. This homomorphism is functorial with respect to proper pushforward and l.c.i. pullback
[
29
, Theorem 18.2]
.

In our case, the base
B=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
is Deligne-Mumford stack and so (
39
) has to be modified. One can use the Baum-Fulton-MacPherson homomorphism for quotient stacks
[
30
]
. For our purposes, the following simple modification suffices. We may take a finite étale morphism
B^{\prime}\to B
where
B^{\prime}
is a scheme (for example, we may take
B^{\prime}\to B
to be the moduli space of curves with a level structure). After base change to
B^{\prime}
, the calculations involving
\tau_{X}
hold in the Chow group with
\mathbb{Q}
-coefficients.

We next fix our notation for relative correspondences (
[
20
]
). Let
\pi_{1}:M_{1}\to B,\pi_{2}:M_{2}\to B
be two proper morphisms between smooth Deligne-Mumford stacks. Then any relative correspondence

Z\in\mathsf{CH}_{*}(M_{1}\times_{B}M_{2})

defines a morphism

Z:\mathsf{CH}^{*}(M_{1})\to\mathsf{CH}^{*}(M_{2}),\,\alpha\mapsto\pi_{2*}(\pi_{1}^{!}\alpha\cap[Z])\,,

where
\pi_{1}^{!}
is the l.c.i. pullback. We interchangeably use the relative correspondence and the induced morphism.

Now we consider the Chow-theoretic Fourier transform induced by Theorem
5.1
, following
[
41
, Section 2.3]
. Since
\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}
is lci, the Todd class of the virtual tangent bundle
T_{\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}}
is defined as an operational Chow class:

\mathrm{td}(T_{\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}})\in\mathsf{CH}_{\mathsf{op}}^{*}(\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C})_{\mathbb{Q}}\,.

The (Chow-theoretic)
Fourier transform
is defined by the relative correspondence

\mathfrak{F}:=\mathrm{td}(-T_{\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}})\cap\tau(\overline{\mathcal{P}})\in\mathsf{CH}_{*}(\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C})_{\mathbb{Q}}\,.

(40)

The inverse Fourier transform is given by

\mathfrak{F}^{-1}:=\mathrm{td}(-(p_{1}\times_{B}p_{2})^{*}T_{B})\cap\tau(\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{-1})\in\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C})_{\mathbb{Q}}\,.

(41)

The choice of the asymmetric Fourier transform will be useful for understanding the Fourier transform of tautological classes. By Theorem
5.1
, we have

\mathfrak{F}\circ\mathfrak{F}^{-1}\cong\mathrm{id}\,,\,\mathfrak{F}^{-1}\circ\mathfrak{F}\cong\mathrm{id}\,.

(42)

The Fourier transform of the class of a section which factors through the locus of line bundles
J_{C}\subset\overline{J}_{C}
(
16
) can be easily computed:

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}
be two non-degenerate stability conditions for
p:C\to B
.
Let
s:B\to\overline{J}_{C}^{\,\epsilon_{1}}
be a section which factors through
J_{C}^{\,\epsilon_{1}}
. Let
i_{s}:=(s,\mathrm{id}):\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon_{2}}\to\overline{J}_{C}^{\,\epsilon_{1}}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon_{2}}
be a closed embedding. Then we have

\mathfrak{F}^{-1}\big(\mathrm{ch}\,(i_{s}^{*}\overline{\mathcal{P}})\big)=[s]\,.

Proof.

On the open locus
J_{C}^{\,\epsilon_{1}}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\,\epsilon_{2}}\subset\overline{J}^{\,\epsilon_{1}}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon_{2}}
, the sheaf
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is the usual Poincaré line bundle. Since
i_{s}
factors through the open locus, the pullback
i_{s}^{*}\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is a line bundle. For
j=1,2
, let
\pi_{j}:\overline{J}^{\,\epsilon_{1}}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon_{2}}\to\overline{J}_{C}^{\,\epsilon_{j}}
be the
j
-th projection.
By the functoriality of (
39
), we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}([s])

\displaystyle=\pi_{2*}\big(i_{s*}1\cdot\mathrm{td}(-T_{\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}})\cdot\tau(\overline{\mathcal{P}})\big)

\displaystyle=(\pi_{2}\circ i_{s})_{*}\big(\mathrm{td}(T_{i_{s}})\cdot\mathrm{td}(-i_{s}^{*}T_{\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}})i_{s}^{*}\tau(\overline{P})\mathrm{td}(-T_{\overline{J}_{C}^{\,\epsilon_{2}}})\big)

\displaystyle=\tau(i_{s}^{*}\overline{\mathcal{P}})\mathrm{td}(-T_{\overline{J}_{C}^{\,\epsilon_{2}}})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ch}\,(i_{s}^{*}\overline{\mathcal{P}})\,.

By (
42
), we get the result by applying
\mathfrak{F}^{-1}
.
∎

5.2
Logarithmic Abel-Jacobi theory

We compute the Fourier transform of the closure of rational Abel-Jacobi sections. The main result is Proposition
5.8
, which extends Proposition
5.2
. A key ingredient in our approach is the desingularization of Arinkin’s kernel constructed in Theorem
1.2
.

Let
\overline{J}_{C}
be a compactified Jacobian with respect to a non-degenerate stability condition
\epsilon
of degree
0
.
For
b\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathsf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with
\sum_{i}a_{i}=(2g-2+n)b
, the rational Abel-Jacobi section
\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}:B\dashrightarrow\overline{J}_{C}
is given by
t\in B\mapsto\mathcal{O}_{C_{t}}(\sum a_{i}x_{i})\otimes(\omega_{C_{t},\log}^{\otimes-b})
. The section is well-defined on the locus where the underlying curve is smooth.

We resolve the indeterminacy of the rational Abel-Jacobi section by taking the following f.s. fiber product in the category of algebraic log stacks (see Section
2.1
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{b;\mathsf{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{LogPic}_{C}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}}

Since
\overline{J}_{C}\to\mathrm{LogPic}_{C}
is a log modification,
B_{b;\mathsf{a}}\to B
is also a log modification and
B_{b;\mathsf{a}}\to\overline{J}_{C}
extends the rational Abel-Jacobi section.

The stack
B_{b;\mathsf{a}}
is not smooth. For our purposes, we use a smooth modular compactification constructed by the second author. As we only need the properties of the construction, we do not review the terminology and rather refer the interested reader to loc.cit.

Theorem 5.3

(
[
46
]
)
.

An algebraic logarithmic stack
B_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}:(\textbf{LogSch}/B)^{\mathrm{op}}\to(\mathrm{Grp})
is defined by
(S\to B)\mapsto(C^{\prime}\to C_{S},\alpha)
,
where
C^{\prime}\to C_{S}
is a quasi-stable model and
\alpha
is an equidimensional piecewise linear function on
C^{\prime}
where
\mathcal{O}_{C^{\prime}}(\sum_{i}a_{i}x_{i})\otimes(\omega_{C_{t},\log}^{\otimes-b})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C^{\prime}}(\alpha)
is
\epsilon
-stable. Then
B_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}
is a Deligne-Mumford stack which is connected and smooth.

The
resolved Abel-Jacobi section
is defined by

\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}:B_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}\to\overline{J}_{C},(C^{\prime}\to C,\alpha)\mapsto\mathcal{O}_{C^{\prime}}(\sum a_{i}x_{i})\otimes(\omega_{C^{\prime},\log}^{\otimes-b})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C^{\prime}}(\alpha)\,.

(43)

We aim to compute the Fourier transform of (
43
). By pulling back the diagram (
20
) along the resolved Abel-Jacobi-section (
43
), we obtain the following fiber diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{J_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{C}\,.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathsf{aj}}_{b;\mathsf{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}}

(44)

By Theorem
5.3
, the resolved Abel-Jacobi section
\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}
is a morphism between smooth stacks and hence
\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}
is l.c.i. Since
\pi_{1}
is flat,
\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\prime}
is also l.c.i.

Lemma 5.4
.

In the diagram (
44
), we have
(\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\prime})^{!}[\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}]=[\widetilde{J_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}}]
in
\mathsf{CH}_{*}(\widetilde{J_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}})
.

Proof.

We first show that
\widetilde{J_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}}
is reduced and irreducible. By Proposition
3.9
(a), the composition
\pi_{1}\circ f
is a saturated morphism. Therefore, the outer square is the f.s. fiber product. Similarly, the bottom square is an f.s. fiber product, and thus, the top square is also an f.s. fiber product. The morphism
g
is a log modification because the top square is an f.s. fiber product and
f
is a log modification. This shows that
\widetilde{J_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}}
is reduced and irreducible.

By dimension considerations, the class
(\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\prime})^{!}[\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}]
is some multiple of the fundamental class of
\widetilde{J_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}}
since it is irreducible. The multiplicity can be verified on the open locus where the underlying curve is smooth. Over this locus, the map
B_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}\to B
is an isomorphism and
g
is also an isomorphism. Therefore the multiplicity is one.
∎

We describe the pullback of the extended Poincaré line bundle
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
along the resolved Abel-Jacobi section. From Theorem
5.3
,
k
-points of
\widetilde{J_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}}
are tuples

(C_{1},C_{2},C^{\prime}\to C_{i},\alpha,L_{2})\,,

(45)

where
C^{\prime}\to C_{1},C_{2}
is a common semistable model,
\alpha
is an equidimensional piecewise linear function on
C_{1}
where
\mathcal{O}_{C_{1}}(\sum_{i}a_{i}x_{i})\otimes(\omega_{C_{1},\log}^{\otimes-b})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C_{1}}(\alpha)
is
\epsilon
-stable,
L_{2}
is a
\epsilon
-stable line bundle on
C_{2}
.

For a log curve
C\to B
, we have the formula of the Deligne pairing of an arbitrary line bundle and a line bundle associated to a piecewise linear function.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
p:C\to B
be a log smooth curve over a log smooth base
B
. For a piecewise linear function
\alpha
on
C
and a line bundle
L
on
C
, we have

\langle\mathcal{O}_{C}(\alpha),L\rangle_{C}=\sum_{w\in V(\Gamma_{C_{b}})}\deg(L|_{w})\cdot\alpha(w)\in\mathsf{PL}(B)\,.

(46)

Proof.

We explain the right hand side of (
46
). For each generic point
b\in B
of a maximal cone, there exists a map
\mathsf{PL}(C_{b})\to\mathbb{Z}^{V(\Gamma_{C_{b}})}
induced by taking the slope and composing with the divisor map as in
[
33
, Section 3.3]
. The right-hand side of (
46
) gives the value at each maximal cone.

Both sides of (
46
) is stable under log modification of the base
B
and taking semistable modification of the log curve
C\to B
, as the degree of
L
is
0
on newly introduced exceptional components. Therefore it is enough to show (
46
) when
B
and
C
are smooth and logarithmically smooth. The log curve
p
is smooth over the nonempty open locus of
B
, where the left hand side of (
46
) is trivial, it is enough to check the multiplicity of left hand side on each boundary of
B
.

We represent the line bundle
L
as the difference of two very ample divisors
L\cong\mathcal{O}(D_{1}-D_{2})
. From the above reduction step, it is enough to prove (
46
) when
\alpha
is
\mathcal{O}_{C}(E)
where
E
is an irreducible component of
p^{-1}D
and
L
is an effective divisor intersecting
E
transversely. When
p^{-1}D
is irreducible
L
can be written as a linear combination of a divisor flat over
B
and
p^{-1}D
, Since
p^{-1}D
is the divisor pulled back from the base, it does not contribute. When
p^{-1}D
has several irreducible components, we first consider the case when
L
is a linear combination of a divisor flat over
B
and a vertical divisor not equal to
E
. Then
\langle\mathcal{O}_{C}(\alpha),L\rangle_{C}\cong\mathcal{O}(mD)
where
m
is the multiplicity of
E
and
E
which is the same as the right hand side. When
L
contains contribution of
E
,
E
can be written as
p^{-1}D
minus other vertical divisors transverse to
E
, hence we get the result.
∎

Remark 5.6
.

In fact, Lemma
5.5
holds without assuming that
B
is log smooth. However, the argument in this more general setting is more involved, and since we do not need it here, we omit it.

From (
45
), we consider the universal curve
C^{\prime}\to\widetilde{J^{\epsilon}_{b;\mathsf{a}}}
, the equidimensional piecewise linear function
\alpha
on
C^{\prime}
pulled back from
C_{1}
, and the universal line bundle
L_{2}
on
C^{\prime}
pulled back from
C_{2}
.

Corollary 5.7
.

For the piecewise linear function
\beta
on
\widetilde{J^{\epsilon}_{b;\mathsf{a}}}
given by
\langle\mathcal{O}_{C^{\prime}}(\alpha),L_{2}\rangle_{C^{\prime}}
, we have

\widetilde{\mathsf{aj}}_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{*}(c_{1}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}))=g^{*}\pi_{2}^{*}\big(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i}\big)+\beta\,.

Proof.

By the proof of Lemma
5.4
, the upper square of (
44
) is an f.s. fiber diagram. Since
f
is log modification,
\widetilde{J_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}}\to\overline{J}_{C}
is a log modification. By Proposition
3.19
,
we obtain:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{aj}}_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{*}(c_{1}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}))

\displaystyle\cong\left\langle L_{2}|_{C^{\prime}},\mathcal{O}_{C^{\prime}}(\sum a_{i}x_{i})\otimes(\omega_{C_{1},\log}^{\otimes-b})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C^{\prime}}(\alpha)\right\rangle

\displaystyle\cong g^{*}\pi_{2}^{*}\big(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i}\big)+\beta\,.

The last equality follows from the linearity of Deligne pairing, together with Lemma
5.5
and the property that
\left\langle L,\mathcal{O}_{C}(s)\right\rangle=s^{*}L
for any smooth section
s
of the universal curve.
∎

We say a class
\gamma
in
\mathsf{PP}(\overline{J}_{C})
is
supported away from the integral locus
if it is a linear combination of normally decorated strata such that the stabilization of no stratum
\Gamma
corresponds to an irreducible curve.

Proposition 5.8
.

For
b\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathsf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with
\sum_{i}a_{i}=(2g-2+n)b
, there exists a class
\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}}
in
\mathsf{PP}(\overline{J}_{C})
supported away from the integral locus such that

\mathfrak{F}(\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}})=\exp(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i})(1+\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}})\,.

Proof.

We consider the diagram (
44
). Let
\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\prime}:\overline{J}_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}\to\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}
be the pullback of the resolved Abel-Jacobi section. Then we have

\displaystyle(\mathsf{aj}_{v}^{\prime})^{!}(\mathrm{td}(-T_{\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}})\cap\tau(\overline{\mathcal{P}}))

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\prime})^{!}(\mathrm{td}(-T_{\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}})\cap\tau(Rf_{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}))

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\prime})^{!}(\mathrm{td}(-T_{\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}})\cap f_{*}\tau(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}))

\displaystyle=g_{*}(\widetilde{\mathsf{aj}}_{b;\mathsf{a}})^{!}\big(\mathrm{ch}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}})\mathrm{td}(T_{\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}})f^{*}\mathrm{td}(-T_{\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}})\cap[\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}]\big)

\displaystyle=g_{*}\big(\widetilde{\mathsf{aj}}_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{*}(\mathrm{ch}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}})\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\widetilde{\pi}})\cdot\pi_{1}^{*}\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\pi})^{-1}\cdot\pi_{2}^{*}\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\pi})^{-1})\cap[\widetilde{J_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}}]\big)\,.

(47)

The first equality follows from Theorem
1.2
, the second from the functoriality of
\tau
under proper pushforward, the third from the base change formula for l.c.i. pullback, and the fourth from (
36
) and Lemma
5.4
.

We now compute the Fourier transform of
\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}
. From the definition (
40
), we have

\mathfrak{F}(\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}})=\pi_{2*}(\mathsf{aj}^{\prime}_{b;\mathsf{a}})^{!}\big(\mathrm{td}(-T_{\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}})\cap\tau(\overline{\mathcal{P}})\big)\,.

Therefore, applying (
47
), we get

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}(\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}})=\pi_{2*}g_{*}\big(\widetilde{\mathsf{aj}}_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{*}(\mathrm{ch}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}})\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\widetilde{\pi}})\cdot\pi_{1}^{*}\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\pi})^{-1}\cdot\pi_{2}^{*}\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\pi})^{-1})\cap[\widetilde{J_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}}]\big)\,.

The pullback of the extended Poincaré line bundle along
\widetilde{\mathsf{aj}}_{b;\mathsf{a}}
is calculated in Corollary
5.7
:

\widetilde{\mathsf{aj}}_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{*}\mathrm{ch}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}})=\exp(g^{*}\pi_{2}^{*}(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i}))\exp(\beta)\,.

Since the pullback along log maps preserves piecewise polynomials, by applying Proposition
4.4
and Corollary
5.7
the class

\widetilde{\mathsf{aj}}_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{*}\big(\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\widetilde{\pi}})\cdot\pi_{1}^{*}\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\pi})^{-1}\cdot\pi_{2}^{*}\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\pi})^{-1}\big)

(48)

lies in
\mathsf{PP}(\widetilde{J_{\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}})
.
The pullback of a piecewise polynomial along a log map is also piecewise polynomial. Moreover, the composition
\pi_{2}\circ g
is a sequence of log modifications. Since the pushforward of a piecewise polynomial is a piecewise polynomial
[
57
, Proposition 67]
, we have

\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}}:=\pi_{2*}g_{*}\big(\exp(\beta)\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{aj}}_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{*}\big(\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\widetilde{\pi}})\cdot\pi_{1}^{*}\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\pi})^{-1}\cdot\pi_{2}^{*}\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\pi})^{-1}\big)\big)-1\in\mathsf{PP}(\overline{J}_{C})\,.

Lastly, we show that
\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}}
is supported away from the integral locus. Over the locus where the underlying curve is integral, the Abel-Jacobi section is well-defined without log modification. Therefore, the indeterminacy locus of
\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}
only contains reducible stable curves. Thus, over the integral locus,

(i)

the function
\beta
is
0
, and

(ii)

the section
\widetilde{\mathsf{aj}}_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{*}
lands in the locus of
\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}
which is isomorphic to
\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}
.

From (i), we find that
\exp(\beta)=1
. Combining (ii) with the Todd class formula of Proposition
4.9
we see that the smaller of the coordinates
\ell_{e_{1}}^{\prime},\ell_{e_{2}}^{\prime}
is zero, reducing (
48
) to 1 over the integral locus. By combining these two observations, we conclude that the class
\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}}
vanishes over the integral locus.
∎

5.3
Partial Fourier transform

Let
\pi:\overline{J}_{C}\to B
be the compactified Jacobian associated with a nondegenerate stability condition. Let
J_{C}\subset\overline{J}_{C}
be the open substack corresponding to line bundles. We consider the Fourier transform restricted to the product
\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}J_{C}
.

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{F}^{-1}\in\mathsf{CH}_{*}(\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C})
be the inverse Fourier kernel (
41
). Then we have

\mathfrak{F}^{-1}|_{\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}J_{C}}=(-1)^{g}\mathrm{ch}(\mathcal{P}^{\vee})\cdot\pi_{1}^{*}\,\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\pi})^{-1}\,,

where
\mathcal{R}_{\pi}
is the residue sheaf for
\pi
defined in Lemma
4.2
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{E}:=\pi_{*}\Omega_{p}^{1}\to B
be the Hodge bundle on
B
. Consider the projection
\pi_{1}\times_{B}\pi_{2}:\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}\to B
. On
\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}J_{C}
, the inverse Fourier transform is expressed as

\mathfrak{F}^{-1}|_{\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}J_{C}}=(-1)^{g}\cdot\mathrm{ch}(\mathcal{P}^{\vee})\cdot(\pi_{1}\times_{B}\pi_{2})^{*}\mathrm{ch}(\det\mathbb{E})\cdot\mathrm{td}(T_{\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}J_{C}})\cdot\mathrm{td}(-(\pi_{1}\times_{B}\pi_{2})^{*}T_{B})\,,

where the sign comes from the shift
[-g]
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}
.
Applying Lemma
4.2
, this simplifies to

\mathrm{td}(T_{\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}J_{C}})\cdot\mathrm{td}(-(\pi_{1}\times_{B}\pi_{2})^{*}T_{B})=\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathcal{R}_{\pi})^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{td}^{\vee}((\pi_{1}\times_{B}\pi_{2})^{*}\mathbb{E}^{\oplus 2})\,.

From
[
53
, Corollary 5.3]
, it follows that
c(\mathbb{E})c(\mathbb{E}^{\vee})=1
, which implies that all Pontryagin classes of
\mathbb{E}
vanish. Consequently, the identity

\mathrm{td}^{\vee}(\mathbb{E})\mathrm{ch}\left(\frac{1}{2}\det(\mathbb{E})\right)=1\,.

(49)

holds. With this, the desired result follows.
∎

Now we consider a partial Fourier transform

\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}:=\mathrm{ch}(\mathcal{P}^{\vee})\in\mathsf{CH}_{*}(\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}J_{C})\,.

(50)

When the genus is important, we denote
\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{g}
.

We prove an elementary property of
\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}
. Let
J^{\underline{0}}_{C}\to B
be the relative Jacobian of multidegree zero line bundles. When the stability condition for the second factor is small, then we can further restrict (
50
) to
\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}J^{\underline{0}}_{C}
. The morphism
\mu
is the action defined in (
8
).

Lemma 5.10
.

On
\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}J^{\underline{0}}_{C}
, we have
c_{1}(\mathcal{P})=-\mu^{*}\Theta+\pi_{1}^{*}\Theta+\pi_{2}^{*}\Theta
.

Proof.

Let
\hat{p}:\widehat{C}\to\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}J_{C}
be the universal quasi-stable curve. By the Deligne pairing formula (
18
), we have
c_{1}(\mathcal{P})=\hat{p}_{*}(c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{1})c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{2}))
.
Therefore we get the desired formula.
∎

5.4
Recursive structure of Fourier transform

We prove that the image of the Fourier transform, when restricted to
J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n}
, has a recursive structure. For a nondegenerate stability condition
\epsilon
, we consider the partial Fourier transform (
50
), given by
\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{g}:\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}^{\epsilon}_{g,n})\to\mathsf{CH}^{*}(J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n})
.

We state the main result of this section.

Theorem 5.11
.

Let
\gamma
be a piecewise polynomial class on
\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
and let
\Xi_{g}
be a monomial in the
\xi
-classes and the
\kappa_{0,1}
-class on
\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
. There exists an effective algorithm to compute
\mathfrak{F}_{g}^{\circ}(\gamma\cdot\Xi_{g})
in terms of
\mathfrak{F}_{h}^{\circ}(\Xi_{h})
over
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h,m}
for all pairs
(h,m)
, where either
h=g,m\leq n
or
h<g
with any
m\geq 1
.

The precise algorithm in Theorem
5.11
will be clear from the proof below. The basic idea is straightforward - a piecewise polynomial class on
\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
is supported on the boundary. Each boundary stratum (of positive codimension) corresponds to some stable graph
\Gamma
along with a quasi-stable subdivision
\widetilde{\Gamma}
and an
\epsilon
-stable multidegree
\delta
on
\widetilde{\Gamma}
. We will show that the Fourier transform can be computed in terms of Fourier transforms on “smaller”
\overline{J}_{h,m}
corresponding to the vertices of
\Gamma
.

Before proving Theorem
5.11
, we study two extreme cases: a class supported on a maximally subdivided stable graph (
33
) and a class supported on a stable graph. We begin with the first case. Consider the following diagram where the right square is a fiber product

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J^{\underline{0},\mathbb{G}_{m}^{h}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J^{\underline{0}}_{g-h,n+2h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g-h,n+2h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}\,.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

(51)

Proposition 5.12
.

For
1\leq h\leq g
, let
\jmath_{h}:\overline{J}_{g-h,n+2h}\to\overline{J}_{g,n}
be the morphism defined in (
33
). For a class
\alpha
in
\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{g-h,n+2h})
, we have

\mathfrak{F}_{g}^{\circ}((\jmath_{h})_{*}\alpha)=(r_{h})_{*}q^{*}\mathfrak{F}_{h}^{\circ}(\alpha)\,.

Proof.

To simplify notation, we denote by
C=\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{g,n}\to B=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
and
C^{\prime}=\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{g-h,n+2h}\to\partial B=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g-h,n+2h}
the two universal curves. Consider the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{\partial C}\times_{\partial B}J^{\underline{0},\mathbb{G}_{m}^{h}}_{\partial C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}J^{\underline{0}}_{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J^{\underline{0}}_{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{\partial C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath_{h}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath_{h}}

(52)

where
\jmath_{h}
is the map defined in Lemma
2.4
and the square is Cartesian. Let
q:J^{\underline{0},\mathbb{G}_{m}^{h}}_{C^{\prime}}\to J^{\underline{0}}_{C^{\prime}}
be the projection as described in (
51
). Let
\nu:C^{\prime}\to\overline{C^{\prime}}
be the morphism that results from gluing the
(n+2i-1)
-th and
(n+2i)
-th markings for all
1\leq i\leq h
. Let
\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{G}_{m}}
denote the restriction of the universal line bundle on
C\to J^{\underline{0}}_{C}
to
J^{\underline{0},\mathbb{G}_{m}^{h}}_{C^{\prime}}
, and let
\mathcal{L}
be the universal line bundle on
C^{\prime}\to J^{\underline{0}}_{C^{\prime}}
.

We first compare two Poincaré line bundles. Let
\mathcal{P}_{g}
be the Poincaré line bundle on
\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}J^{\underline{0}}_{C}
and let
\mathcal{P}_{g-h}
be the Poincaré line bundle on
\overline{J}_{C^{\prime}}\times_{\partial B}J^{\underline{0}}_{C^{\prime}}
. It is easier to work with the universal sheaf described by a rank
1
torsion free sheaf. Let
\mathcal{F}_{g}
be the universal sheaf on
C\to\overline{J}_{C}
and
\mathcal{F}_{g-h}
be the universal sheaf on
C^{\prime}\to\overline{J}_{C^{\prime}}
. For the gluing morphism
\nu:C^{\prime}\to\overline{C^{\prime}}
over
\overline{J}_{C^{\prime}}
, Lemma
2.4
implies that

\jmath_{h}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{g}\cong\nu_{*}\mathcal{F}_{g-h}\,.

The Deligne pairing naturally extends when one of the factors is a rank
1
torsion-free sheaf via an appropriate determinant line bundle (
[
5
, eq. (3)]
).
For
\mathrm{id}\times q:\overline{J}_{\partial C}\times_{\partial B}J^{\underline{0},\mathbb{G}_{m}^{h}}_{\partial C}\to\overline{J}_{\partial C}\times_{\partial B}J^{\underline{0}}_{\partial C}
, we have

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}\times q)^{*}\mathcal{P}_{g-h}

\displaystyle\cong(\mathrm{id}\times q)^{*}\langle\mathcal{F}_{g-h},\mathcal{L}\rangle_{C^{\prime}}\cong\langle\mathcal{F}_{g-h},\nu^{*}\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\rangle_{C^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\cong\langle\nu_{*}\mathcal{F}_{g-h},\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\rangle_{\overline{C^{\prime}}}\cong(\jmath_{h}^{\prime})^{*}\mathcal{P}_{g}\,,

(53)

where the third isomorphism follows from
\nu_{*}
being exact.

We apply the above computation to (
52
). For any
\alpha\in\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{g-h,n+2h})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{g}^{\circ}((\jmath_{h})_{*}\alpha)

\displaystyle=\pi_{2*}(\pi_{1}^{*}(\jmath_{h})_{*}\alpha\cup\mathrm{ch}(\mathcal{P}_{g}))

\displaystyle=\pi_{2*}(\jmath^{\prime}_{h})_{*}((\pi_{1}^{\prime})^{*}\alpha\cup(j_{h}^{\prime})^{*}\mathrm{ch}(\mathcal{P}_{g}))

\displaystyle=\pi_{2*}(\jmath^{\prime}_{h})_{*}((\mathrm{id}\times q)^{*}\pi_{1}^{*}\alpha\cup(\mathrm{id}\times q)^{*}\mathrm{ch}(\mathcal{P}_{g-h}))

\displaystyle=\pi_{2*}(\jmath^{\prime}_{h})_{*}(\mathrm{id}\times q)^{*}(\pi_{1}^{*}\alpha\cup\mathrm{ch}(\mathcal{P}_{g-h}))

\displaystyle=(r_{h})_{*}\pi_{2*}(\mathrm{id}\times q)^{*}(\pi_{1}^{*}\alpha\cup\mathrm{ch}(\mathcal{P}_{g-h}))

\displaystyle=(r_{h})_{*}q^{*}\pi_{2*}(\pi_{1}^{*}\alpha\cup\mathrm{ch}(\mathcal{P}_{g-h}))=(r_{h})_{*}q^{*}\mathfrak{F}_{g-h}^{\circ}(\alpha)\,,

where the third equality follows from (
5.4
) and the other equalities are from the projection formula. This completes the proof.
∎

Now we describe the strata of
\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
corresponding to stable graphs. For a stable graph
\Gamma
of genus
g
with
n
markings, let

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}:=\prod_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g(v),n(v)}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}

denote the gluing map. Let
\delta:V(\Gamma)\to\mathbb{Z}
be an
\epsilon
-stable multidegree. Let
\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}
be the compactified Jacobian defined as in (
13
). Then
\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}
is smooth.

Let
\Gamma
be a stable graph with
h^{1}(\Gamma)=0
, and let
\delta:V(\Gamma)\to\mathbb{Z}
be the unique
\epsilon
-stable multidegree. Then there exists a nondegenerate stability condition
\epsilon_{v}
for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g(v),n(v)}
of degree
\delta(v)
such that the stratum
\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}
is isomorphic to
\prod_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\overline{J}_{g(v),n(v)}^{\epsilon_{v}}
.

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
\Gamma
be a stable graph with
h^{1}(\Gamma)=0
. For each vertex
v\in V(\Gamma)
, choose a class
\alpha_{v}\in\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}^{\epsilon_{v}}_{g(v),n(v)})
. Then, the Fourier transform satisfies the factorization property:

\mathfrak{F}_{g}^{\circ}\big(\prod_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\alpha_{v}\big)=\prod_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\mathfrak{F}_{g(v)}^{\circ}(\alpha_{v})\,.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}
be the restriction of the Poincaré line bundle on
\overline{J}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}}J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n}
to
\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}}J^{\underline{0}}_{\Gamma}
. By
[
42
, Lemma 5.5]
we have

\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}\cong\boxtimes_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\mathcal{P}_{v}\,,

where
\mathcal{P}_{v}
are Poincaré line bundles on
\overline{J}_{g(v),n(v)}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g(v),n(v)}}J^{\underline{0}}_{g(v),n(v)}
. This gives the result.
∎

Let
\Gamma
be a stable graph with
h^{1}(\Gamma)>0
, and let
\delta:V(\Gamma)\to\mathbb{Z}
be an
\epsilon
-stable multidegree. We describe the boundary strata
\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}
up to birational equivalence.
We consider a composition of morphisms

\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}\to\mathfrak{Pic}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}\xrightarrow{q}\prod_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\mathfrak{Pic}_{g(v),n(v),\delta(v)}\to\prod_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\mathrm{LogPic}_{g(v),n(v)}

(54)

where the first morphism is from (
13
), the second morphism is from (
12
) and the third morphism is from (
15
). Choose any nondegenerate stability condition
\epsilon_{v}
for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g(v),n(v)}
of degree
\delta(v)
. We denote

\overline{J}^{\Gamma_{\delta}}:=\prod_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\overline{J}_{g(v),n(v)}^{\epsilon_{v}}\,.

By combining (
54
) and (
9
), we obtain an f.s. fiber diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}^{\Gamma_{\delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\mathrm{LogPic}_{g(v),n(v)}\,.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}

(55)

Since the right vertical map is a log modification,
r:\widetilde{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}\to\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}
is also a log modification.

We compare Poincaré line bundles up to birational equivalence. For the relative Jacobian
J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
, we denote

J_{\Gamma}:=\prod_{v\in V(\Gamma)}J^{\underline{0}}_{g(v),n(v)}

and

J^{\mathbb{G}_{m}}_{\Gamma}:=J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n}|_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}}\,.

By Lemma
2.3
, the morphism
q:J^{\mathbb{G}_{m}}_{\Gamma}\to J_{\Gamma}
induced by the partial normalization of the underlying curve is a
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{h^{1}(\Gamma)}
-torsor. For each
v\in V(\Gamma)
, let
\mathcal{P}_{g(v),n(v)}
be the Poincaré line bundle on
\overline{J}_{g(v),n(v)}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g(v),n(v)}}J^{\underline{0}}_{g(v),n(v)}
and denote

\mathcal{P}^{\Gamma}:=\boxtimes_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\mathcal{P}_{g(v),n(v)}\in\mathrm{Pic}(\overline{J}^{\Gamma}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}}J_{\Gamma})\,.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}
be the restriction of the Poincaré line bundle on
\overline{J}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}}J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n}
to
\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}}J^{\mathbb{G}_{m}}_{\Gamma}
.
From (
55
), we obtain a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}}J^{\mathbb{G}_{m}}_{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}^{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}}J^{\mathbb{G}_{m}}_{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}^{\Gamma}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}}J^{\mathbb{G}_{m}}_{\Gamma}\,.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

(56)

Here, we abbreviate
r=r\times\mathrm{id},s=s\times\mathrm{id}
.

Proposition 5.14
.

In (
56
), we have
r^{*}\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}\cong s^{*}\mathcal{P}^{\Gamma}
in
\mathrm{Pic}(\widetilde{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}}J^{\mathbb{G}_{m}}_{\Gamma})
.

Proof.

We first write
r^{*}\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}
and
s^{*}\mathcal{P}^{\Gamma}
as Deligne pairings. Let
C_{\Gamma}\to\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}
be the universal quasi-stable curve induced by the morphism
\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}
. The partial normalization
\nu:C^{\nu}_{\Gamma}\to C_{\Gamma}
arises from the morphism
q
in (
12
). We pullback
\nu
along
\widetilde{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}\times_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}}J_{\Gamma}^{\mathbb{G}_{m}}
. Denoting by
L_{1}\to C_{\Gamma}
the restriction of the admissible model of the universal sheaf from
\overline{J}_{C}
and by
M
the pullback of the universal multidegree zero line bundle from
J^{\underline{0}}_{C}
, the projection formula along
\nu
gives:

r^{*}\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}=\langle L_{1},M\rangle_{C_{\Gamma}}\cong\langle\nu^{*}L_{1},\nu^{*}M\rangle_{C^{\nu}_{\Gamma}}\,.

(57)

To compute
s^{*}\mathcal{P}^{\Gamma}
, introduce the contraction morphism
\mu:C^{\nu}_{\Gamma}\to C^{s}_{\Gamma}
which contracts rational
\mathbb{P}^{1}
components with a single special point. Let
L_{2}\to C^{s}_{\Gamma}
be the pullback of the admissible line bundle from
\overline{J}^{\Gamma_{\delta}}
. By the construction of
\widetilde{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}
(
55
), there exists a piecewise linear function
\alpha
on
C^{\nu}_{\Gamma}
which induces an isomorphism

\mu^{*}L_{2}\cong\nu^{*}L_{1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C^{\nu}_{\Gamma}}(\alpha)\,.

(58)

We now compare the two line bundles using the Deligne pairing. From the established relationships, we can write:

r^{*}\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}\cong\langle\nu^{*}L_{1},\nu^{*}M\rangle\cong\langle\nu^{*}L_{1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C^{\nu}_{\Gamma}}(\alpha),\nu^{*}M\rangle\cong\langle\mu^{*}L_{2},\mu^{*}M\rangle_{C^{\mu}_{\Gamma}}\cong s^{*}\mathcal{P}^{\Gamma}\,.

Here, the first isomorphism follows from (
57
), and the second from (
58
). The third isomorphism uses the fact that
M
is a line bundle of multidegree zero, allowing us to apply Lemma
5.5
, which ensures that the correction factor
\mathcal{O}_{C^{\nu}_{\Gamma}}(\alpha)
does not contribute. Thus, we obtain the desired isomorphism
r^{*}\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}\cong s^{*}\mathcal{P}^{\Gamma}
.
∎

Corollary 5.15
.

Let
\Gamma
be a stable graph of genus
g
with
n
markings, and let
\delta:V(\Gamma)\to\mathbb{Z}
be an
\epsilon
-stable multidegree. Suppose
h^{1}(\Gamma)>0
. For any monomial in
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n}
and the
\kappa_{0,1}
-class
\Xi
in
\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}})
, we have the vanishing of the Fourier transform

\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{g}((j_{\Gamma_{\delta}})_{*}(\Xi))=0\,.

Proof.

We consider the diagram (
56
). Given a monomial of
\xi
-classes
\Xi\in\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}})
, there exists a monomial of
\xi
-classes
\Xi^{\prime}
on
\overline{J}^{\Gamma_{\delta}}
such that

r^{*}\pi_{1}^{*}(\Xi)=s^{*}\pi_{1}^{*}(\Xi^{\prime})\,.

(59)

By the projection formula, we get

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{g}^{\circ}(j_{\Gamma_{\delta}*}(\Xi))

\displaystyle=\pi_{2*}(\pi_{1}^{*}(\Xi)\cup\mathrm{ch}(\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}))

\displaystyle=\pi_{2*}s_{*}(r^{*}\pi_{1}^{*}\Xi\cup s^{*}\mathrm{ch}(\mathcal{P}^{\Gamma}))

\displaystyle=\pi_{2*}s_{*}(s^{*}\pi_{1}^{*}(\Xi^{\prime})\cup s^{*}\mathrm{ch}(\mathcal{P}^{\Gamma}))

\displaystyle=0\,,

where the second equality follows from Proposition
5.14
, and the third equality follows from (
59
). The last equality holds because
h^{1}(\Gamma)>0
and hence
s
has positive relative dimension.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.11
.

Let
\gamma
be a piecewise polynomial class on
\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
. Such a class can be expressed as a linear combination of terms of the form
[\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\widetilde{\delta}},\gamma_{0}]
, where
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\widetilde{\delta}}
is an
\epsilon
-stable quasi-stable graph and
\gamma_{0}
is a monomial of balanced
\psi
-classes. We may assume that the graph is nontrivial.

To describe the boundary stratum
\overline{J}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\widetilde{\delta}}}
more concretely, consider that the graph
\widetilde{\Gamma}
arises from subdividing a stable graph
\Gamma
at
h
edges for some
h\geq 0
. By Lemma
2.4
, we can find a stability condition
\epsilon_{h}
on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g-h,n+2h}
with a map

\overline{J}_{g-h,n+2h}^{\epsilon_{h}}\to\overline{J}_{g,n}

whose image is the closure of the locus of curves with
h
self nodes. We can then lift the data of the
\epsilon
-stable multidegree
\widetilde{\delta}:V(\widetilde{\Gamma})\to\mathbb{Z}
to an
\epsilon_{h}
-stable multidegree
\delta
on a graph
\Gamma_{\delta}
obtained from
\Gamma
by cutting the
h
edges that are subdivided in
\widetilde{\Gamma}
, creating
2h
additional markings, and choosing some labeling for them. Using this multidegree, we find a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{g-h,n+2h}^{\epsilon_{h}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g-h,n+2h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}\,.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath_{h}}

(60)

with
\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}
being finite over the normalization of
\overline{J}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\widetilde{\delta}}}
.

For the monomial
\gamma_{0}
, we apply the genus
0
relation (
34
) on each unstable vertex. This allows us to rewrite
\gamma_{0}
as a polynomial involving
\psi
-classes pulled back from
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}
and
\xi
-classes associated with the last
2h
markings on
\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}
. In particular, no
\xi
-classes are introduced at the edges of
\Gamma
.

We consider the class
\pi^{*}\Psi\cdot\Xi
, where
\Psi
is a monomial in
\psi
-classes pulled back from
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}
in (
60
) and
\Xi
is a monomial in
\xi
-classes and
\kappa_{0,1}
on
\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}
. By Proposition
5.12
, we have the equality

\mathfrak{F}_{g}^{\circ}(\jmath_{h*}j_{\Gamma*}(\pi^{*}\Psi\cdot\Xi))=r_{h*}q^{*}\mathfrak{F}_{g-h}^{\circ}(j_{\Gamma*}(\pi^{*}(\Psi)\cdot\Xi))=\pi^{*}(\Psi)\cdot r_{h*}q^{*}\mathfrak{F}_{g-h}^{\circ}(j_{\Gamma*}(\Xi))\,,

(61)

where the second equality holds due to the linearity of
\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{g-h}
. If
h^{1}(\Gamma)>0
, the Fourier transform of (
61
) vanishes by Corollary
5.15
. If
h^{1}(\Gamma)=0
, the Fourier transform of (
61
) reduces to a computation involving Fourier transforms
\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{g_{v}}
corresponding to the vertices of
\Gamma
by Proposition
5.13
. Since
\Gamma
was a nontrivial stable graph, these vertices either have smaller genus
g_{v}<g
or have
g_{v}=g
and
n_{v}<n
. This establishes the recursive structure of the Fourier transform.
∎

6
The top degree part of the
\mathsf{DR}
formula

6.1
The double ramification cycle formula

Suppose we have a genus
g\geq 0
, codimension
c\geq 0
, integer
b
, and vector
\mathsf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
of integers with sum
(2g-2+n)b
. Then the double ramification cycle (
\mathsf{DR}
) formula
[
35
]
gives a cycle

\mathsf{DR}_{g}^{c}(b;\mathsf{a})\in\mathsf{CH}^{c}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}).

In the special case
c=g
, this formula gives the (
b
-twisted)
\mathsf{DR}
cycle, which encodes the divisorial condition
\mathcal{O}_{C}(a_{1}p_{1}+\cdots+a_{n}p_{n})\cong(\omega_{C,\log})^{\otimes b}
. But the formula gives a well-defined cycle for all values of
c
, and the value
c=g
will not be special for anything we do in this section.

The formula given in
[
35
, Section 1.1]
for
\mathsf{DR}_{g}^{c}(b;\mathsf{a})
is

\left[\exp\left(-\frac{b^{2}}{2}\kappa_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}^{2}}{2}\psi_{i}\right)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Gamma\in\mathsf{G}_{g,n}\\
w\in\mathsf{W}_{\Gamma,r,b}\end{subarray}}\frac{r^{-h^{1}(\Gamma)}}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)|}\;(j_{\Gamma})_{*}\Bigg[\prod_{e=(h,h^{\prime})\in E(\Gamma)}\frac{1-\exp\left(-\frac{w(h)w(h^{\prime})}{2}(\psi_{h}+\psi_{h^{\prime}})\right)}{\psi_{h}+\psi_{h^{\prime}}}\Bigg]\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{codim $c$,}\\
r=0\end{subarray}}.

Here
\mathsf{G}_{g,n}
is the set of stable graphs for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
and
\mathsf{W}_{\Gamma,r,b}
is the set of functions
w
assigning to each half-edge
h\in H(\Gamma)
an integer
w(h)\in\{0,1,\ldots,r-1\}
satisfying the congruence conditions:

•

If
h_{i}
is the leg with marking
i
, then
w(h_{i})\equiv a_{i}
(mod
r
).

•

If
e=(h,h^{\prime})\in E(\Gamma)
is an edge, then
w(h)+w(h^{\prime})\equiv 0
(mod
r
).

•

If
v\in V(\Gamma)
is a vertex, then

\sum_{h\text{ at }v}w(h)\equiv(2g_{v}-2+n_{v})b\pmod{r}.

The expression inside the brackets then has the property that its codimension
c
part is a polynomial in the integer parameter
r
for
r\gg 0
, and we take the constant term of that polynomial.

It will be convenient for us to work with “total
\mathsf{DR}
”, i.e.

\mathsf{DR}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})=\sum_{c\geq 0}\mathsf{DR}_{g}^{c}(b;\mathsf{a}).

One reason is that we can then factor out the exponential factor at the beginning of the formula and write

\mathsf{DR}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})=\exp(\mathsf{DRD}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a}))\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a}),

(62)

where

\mathsf{DRD}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})=-\frac{b^{2}}{2}\kappa_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}^{2}}{2}\psi_{i}

is a divisor class and

\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})=\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Gamma\in\mathsf{G}_{g,n}\\
w\in\mathsf{W}_{\Gamma,r,b}\end{subarray}}\frac{r^{-h^{1}(\Gamma)}}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)|}\;(j_{\Gamma})_{*}\Bigg[\prod_{e=(h,h^{\prime})\in E(\Gamma)}\frac{1-\exp\left(-\frac{w(h)w(h^{\prime})}{2}(\psi_{h}+\psi_{h^{\prime}})\right)}{\psi_{h}+\psi_{h^{\prime}}}\Bigg]\right]_{r=0}

(63)

is the “piecewise polynomial part” of the
\mathsf{DR}
formula. Thus the complexity of the
\mathsf{DR}
formula is contained in the
\mathsf{DRP}
factor.

It will be convenient for us to isolate a single coefficient in
\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
and describe it in a more raw combinatorial form. Let
G
be a connected finite graph (if it is a stable graph with genus assignments and legs, we ignore those features) and let
a_{v}
be integers indexed by the vertices
v\in V(G)
, subject to the relation
\sum_{v}a_{v}=0
. Then define the
double ramification cycle graph invariant

C(G)
by

C(G)(\{a_{v}\}):=\left[r^{-h^{1}(G)}\sum_{w\in\mathsf{W}_{G,r}}\prod_{e=(h,h^{\prime})\in E(G)}\frac{1}{2}w(h)w(h^{\prime})\right]_{r=0}.

(64)

Here
\mathsf{W}_{G,r}
is analogous to
\mathsf{W}_{\Gamma,r,b}
, but
G
has no legs and the conditions on
w:H(G)\to\{0,1,\ldots,r-1\}
are simply:

•

If
e=(h,h^{\prime})\in E(\Gamma)
is an edge, then
w(h)+w(h^{\prime})\equiv 0
(mod
r
).

•

If
v\in V(\Gamma)
is a vertex, then

\sum_{h\text{ at }v}w(h)\equiv-a_{v}\pmod{r}.

Then for a stable graph
\Gamma\in\mathsf{G}_{g,n}
, the coefficient of the pure boundary stratum class

\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)|}(j_{\Gamma})_{*}1

in
\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
(or in
\mathsf{DR}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
) is precisely given by
C(\Gamma)(\{a_{v}\})
after taking

a_{v}:=\left(\sum_{\text{leg $i$ at vertex $v$}}a_{i}\right)-(2g_{v}-2+n_{v})b.

(65)

It turns out that
C(G)(\{a_{v}\})
is a polynomial in the integers
a_{v}
of degree at most
2|E(G)|

[
60
,
59
]
. We think of
C(G)
as an element

C(G)\in\mathbb{Q}[\{a_{v}\}]\Big/\left(\sum_{v}a_{v}\right)

where the
a_{v}
are now formal variables.

The full
\mathsf{DRP}
formula includes tautological classes other than pure boundary strata classes - there can be insertions along edges of polynomials in
\psi+\psi^{\prime}
, the sum of the two
\psi
classes corresponding to the two halves of the edge. However, the coefficients of these more general tautological classes in the
\mathsf{DR}
formula are still easily expressed in terms of the graph invariants
C(G)
. This is done by modifying the graph slightly - if edge
e
has an insertion of
(-\psi-\psi^{\prime})^{d}/(d+1)!
, then modify the stable graph
\Gamma
by subdividing the edge
e
into
d+1
edges (adding
d
new semistable vertices of genus
0
). If the resulting semistable graph is
\widehat{\Gamma}
, then the coefficient in the
\mathsf{DR}
formula is now given by
C(\widehat{\Gamma})
(after the same specialization of variables (
65
), which gives
a_{v}:=0
for all semistable vertices). This perspective is explained further in
[
58
]
and will be used in Section
6.4.1
.

6.2
The top degree part

The total
\mathsf{DR}
cycle

\mathsf{DR}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})=\exp(\mathsf{DRD}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a}))\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})

depends polynomially on the inputs
b,a_{i}

[
60
,
59
]
. More precisely, the codimension
c
part is a polynomial of degree at most
2c
:

\mathsf{DR}_{g}^{c}(b;\mathsf{a})\in\mathsf{CH}^{c}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[\mathbb{Q}[b,a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}]/(a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n}-(2g-2+n)b)\right]_{\text{deg $\leq 2c$}}.

We can take the top degree part of this polynomial to define

\widetilde{\mathsf{DR}}_{g}^{c}(b;\mathsf{a})\in\mathsf{CH}^{c}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[\mathbb{Q}[b,a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}]/(a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n}-(2g-2+n)b)\right]_{\text{deg $2c$}}.

We also let
\widetilde{\mathsf{DR}}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
be the part of the total
\mathsf{DR}
formula
\mathsf{DR}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
in which the polynomial degree is exactly twice the codimension. Clearly we can factor out the exponential factor:

\widetilde{\mathsf{DR}}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})=\exp(\mathsf{DRD}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a}))\widetilde{\mathsf{DRP}}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a}).

The main result of this section is a correspondence between the total
\mathsf{DR}
formula and its top degree part. The correspondence is most naturally stated using the negative zeta value regularization convention

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{d+1}:=\zeta(-d-1)=-\frac{B_{d+2}}{d+2}\quad\text{ for $d\geq 0$},

(66)

where
B_{n}
are the Bernoulli numbers.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
g,n\geq 0
. For each
0\leq m\leq g
, let
j_{m}:\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g-m,n+2m}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
be the gluing map gluing the last
m
pairs of markings. Then

\mathsf{DR}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})=\sum_{m=0}^{g}\frac{1}{2^{m}m!}(j_{m})_{*}\left[\sum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}>0}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m}k_{i}\right)\widetilde{\mathsf{DR}}_{g-m}(b;\mathsf{a},k_{1},-k_{1},k_{2},-k_{2},\ldots,k_{m},-k_{m})\right],

where the infinite sums over
k_{i}
of polynomials in
k_{i}
are evaluated via (
66
).

Note that we can take the codimension
c
part of both sides to express
\mathsf{DR}_{g}^{c}
in terms of
\widetilde{\mathsf{DR}}_{g-m}^{c-m}
. The correspondence can also be formally inverted by adding a sign factor:

Corollary 6.2
.

Let
g,n\geq 0
. Then

\widetilde{\mathsf{DR}}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})=\sum_{m=0}^{g}\frac{(-1)^{m}}{2^{m}m!}(j_{m})_{*}\left[\sum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}>0}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m}k_{i}\right)\mathsf{DR}_{g-m}(b;\mathsf{a},k_{1},-k_{1},k_{2},-k_{2},\ldots,k_{m},-k_{m})\right].

We will prove Theorem
6.1
in Section
6.4
after first reviewing a formula of Zagier in Section
6.3
. We will then generalize to the universal Picard stack in Theorem
6.6
in Section
6.5
.

6.3
Zagier’s formula for a
\mathsf{DR}
coefficient

Zagier gave an alternative expression for the graph invariant
C(G)
appearing in the
\mathsf{DR}
formula - a proof can be found in the notes
[
59
]
. We briefly recall Zagier’s formula here. It has the advantage of being visibly polynomial in the
a_{v}
variables.

To describe the formula, it is convenient to fix an orientation of every edge of the graph
G
(it will be easy to see that this choice does not affect the following formula for
C(G)
). Also, for each edge
e\in E(G)
, let
z_{e}
be a formal variable. The shape of Zagier’s formula is a sum over spanning trees
T
of
G
. Given such a tree
T
, we need two auxiliary definitions before we can state the formula.

First, suppose
e\in E(G)
is an edge that does not belong to the spanning tree
T
. Then there is a unique cycle in the subgraph
T\cup\{e\}
; let
z_{e,T}
be the signed sum of
z_{f}
over edges
f
in that cycle, with signs given by comparing the orientation of
f
with the orientation of
e
as you go around the cycle (which must contain
e
). In other words,
z_{e}
always has positive sign in
z_{e,T}
.

Second, suppose
e\in E(G)
is an edge that does belong to the spanning tree
T
. Then the subgraph
T\setminus\{e\}
(given by cutting the edge
e
) has two connected components. Take the connected component containing the head of
e
, and let
a_{e,T}
be the sum of
a_{v}
for all vertices
v
in that connected component.

Then Zagier’s formula states that

C(G)=(-1)^{|E(G)|}\left[\sum_{\text{$T$ spanning tree}}\prod_{e\in T}\exp(a_{e,T}z_{e})\prod_{e\notin T}\frac{z_{e}}{\exp(z_{e,T})-1}\right]_{\text{coeff of }\prod_{e\in E(G)}z_{e}^{2}}.

(67)

Here the final subscript indicates to take the coefficient of
\prod_{e\in E(G)}z_{e}^{2}
when the bracketed expression is expanded as a power series in the
z_{e}
variables. Although the individual terms in the sum are not power series in the
z_{e}
variables due to dividing by
z_{e,T}
, it can be checked that these poles cancel in the overall sum, yielding an analytic function near the origin
z_{e}=0
. If a type of multivariate Laurent series expansion is chosen that is compatible with regular power series expansion (e.g. sequentially expanding as Laurent series in the individual variables
z_{e}
in some fixed order), then taking this coefficient can be moved inside the sum over
T
without changing the answer.

To write down a formula for the top degree part
\widetilde{\mathsf{DR}}
, we need to take the top degree part of the
\mathsf{DR}
coefficient graph invariant
C(G)
. Let

\widetilde{C}(G)\in\mathbb{Q}[\{a_{v}\}]\Big/\left(\sum_{v\in V(G)}a_{v}\right)

be the degree
2|E(G)|
part of
C(G)
. It is easy to extract this from Zagier’s formula. The final factor is the only one in which the degrees might be different in the
z_{e}
and
a_{v}
variables, so we just have that

\widetilde{C}(G)=(-1)^{|E(G)|}\left[\sum_{\text{$T$ spanning tree}}\prod_{e\in T}\exp(a_{e,T}z_{e})\prod_{e\notin T}\frac{z_{e}}{z_{e,T}}\right]_{\text{coeff of }\prod_{e\in E(G)}z_{e}^{2}}.

(68)

6.4
Proof of Theorem
6.1

We begin by canceling some of the exponential factors
\exp(\mathsf{DRD})
appearing on both sides of Theorem
6.1
. Since
j_{m}^{*}\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{1}
and
j_{m}^{*}\psi_{i}=\psi_{i}
, the projection formula lets us cancel all the factors of
\kappa_{1}
and
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{n}
on both sides, so it suffices to prove that

\displaystyle\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})=\sum_{m=0}^{g}\frac{1}{2^{m}m!}(j_{m})_{*}\Bigg[\sum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}>0}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m}k_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{k_{1}^{2}}{2}(\psi_{n+1}+\psi_{n+2})+\cdots+\frac{k_{m}^{2}}{2}(\psi_{n+2m-1}+\psi_{n+2m})\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{DRP}}_{g-m}(b;\mathsf{a},k_{1},-k_{1},k_{2},-k_{2},\ldots,k_{m},-k_{m})\Bigg].

We can see that both sides have the property that they only have insertions of powers of
\psi+\psi^{\prime}
(along edges), which is a good sign. But we will begin by checking that for any stable graph
\Gamma
with no automorphisms, the coefficient of
(j_{\Gamma})_{*}1
is equal on both sides. We will then discuss in Section
6.4.1
how to modify things to check that things still work when
\Gamma
has automorphisms or there are
\psi
insertions.

On the left side, the coefficient (which we will call
L(\Gamma)
) is simply
C(\Gamma)
with the usual specialization of variables (
65
) applied. On the right side, we have many terms that all produce a multiple of
(j_{\Gamma})_{*}1
- we get one term for each stable graph of genus
g-m
with
n+2m
legs such that gluing the last
m
pairs of legs recovers
\Gamma
. This is the same as taking a subset of the edges
E(\Gamma)
of size
m
such that the complement
\Gamma^{\prime}
is still connected, and then attaching legs to vertices of
\Gamma^{\prime}
where deleted edges were attached. There are
2^{m}m!
different ways to label these legs with
n+1,n+2,\ldots,n+2m
such that gluing them in pairs recovers
\Gamma
, but they all give the same contribution. So we can think of the coefficient on the right side (which we will call
R(\Gamma)
) as summing over all subsets of
E(\Gamma)
such that the complement
\Gamma^{\prime}
is still connected, then taking the
\mathsf{DR}
coefficients
\widetilde{C}(\Gamma^{\prime})
with appropriate specializations of variables, and finally evaluating the negative zeta value regularization.

We evaluate the
C(\Gamma)
and
\widetilde{C}(\Gamma^{\prime})
that appear using Zagier’s formula (
67
) and its top degree variant (
68
). As explained in Section
6.3
, this requires choosing an orientation for every edge in
\Gamma
(and we use the same orientations on the edges of each
\Gamma^{\prime}
). We now write out what this gives for the coefficients
L(\Gamma),R(\Gamma)
on the two sides. We begin with the simpler left side to introduce some shorthand:

L(\Gamma)=(-1)^{|E(\Gamma)|}\left[\sum_{T\subseteq\Gamma}\prod_{e\in T}\exp(a_{e,T}z_{e})\prod_{e\notin T}\frac{z_{e}}{\exp(z_{e,T})-1}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{coeff of }\prod z_{e}^{2},\\
\{a_{v}\}\mapsto\{b,a_{i}\}\end{subarray}}

(69)

Here the subscripts at the end mean that first we take the coefficient of
\prod_{e\in E(\Gamma)}z_{e}^{2}
and then we do the specialization (
65
) replacing the
a_{v}
with the
b
and
a_{i}
variables (using the data of the genus and leg assignments to vertices in
\Gamma
).

Then the right side can be written as

R(\Gamma)=\sum_{m\geq 0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq E(\Gamma)\\
|S|=m\\
\Gamma^{\prime}:=\Gamma\setminus S\\
\Gamma^{\prime}\text{ is connected}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|E(\Gamma^{\prime})|}\left[\sum_{T\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}}\prod_{e\in T}\exp(a_{e,T}z_{e})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\notin T\\
e\notin S\end{subarray}}\frac{z_{e}}{z_{e,T}}\prod_{e\in S}z_{e}^{2}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{coeff of }\prod z_{e}^{2},\\
\{a_{v}\}\mapsto\{b,a_{i},\pm k_{i}\},\\
k_{i}^{d}\mapsto\zeta(-d-1)\end{subarray}}.

(70)

We make a few notes to explain various things about the above formula:

•

The factor
1/(2^{m}m!)
in the statement of Theorem
6.1
has been cancelled out by a factor coming from the choice of labels for the
2m
legs that would correspond to the
m
edges in
S
, as discussed above.

•

We include the extra factor
\prod_{e\in S}z_{e}^{2}
because those are the variables for edges that are in
\Gamma
but not
\Gamma^{\prime}
, and at the end we want to take the coefficient of
z_{e}^{2}
with respect to all of those variables too.

•

The specialization of variables
\{a_{v}\}\mapsto\{b,a_{i},\pm k_{i}\}
indicates that we need to adjust the endpoints of the edges in
S
by
\pm k_{i}
. Although the signs and indices of the
k_{i}
here depend on the choice of labels described in the first note, it is easy to see from the following note that this choice does not affect the final expression.

•

The final subscript at the end,
k_{i}^{d}\mapsto\zeta(-d-1)
, combines multiplication by
\prod k_{i}
and summing over
k_{i}>0
using negative zeta regularization. Note that
\zeta(-d-1)=-B_{d+2}/(d+2)
is zero unless
d
is even.

We simplify this expression by pulling out the sum over spanning trees of
\Gamma^{\prime}
- the spanning trees of
\Gamma^{\prime}
are precisely the spanning trees of
\Gamma
with edges disjoint from
S
, and conveniently if
S
is disjoint from the edges of some spanning tree of
\Gamma
then
\Gamma^{\prime}
is automatically connected. We also multiply by the sign factor
(-1)^{|E(\Gamma)|}
that also appeared on the left side. This leaves a remaining sign of
(-1)^{m}
, which we incorporate into the product over edges in
S
. The result is

(-1)^{|E(\Gamma)|}R(\Gamma)=\sum_{T\subseteq\Gamma}\sum_{m\geq 0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq E(\Gamma)\setminus E(T)\\
|S|=m\end{subarray}}\left[\prod_{e\in T}\exp(a_{e,T}z_{e})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\notin T\\
e\notin S\end{subarray}}\frac{z_{e}}{z_{e,T}}\prod_{e\in S}\left(-z_{e}^{2}\right)\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{coeff of }\prod z_{e}^{2},\\
\{a_{v}\}\mapsto\{b,a_{i},\pm k_{i}\},\\
k_{i}^{d}\mapsto\zeta(-d-1)\end{subarray}}.

(71)

Note that taking the coefficient of
\prod z_{e}^{2}
of an individual term like this requires choosing a multivariate Laurent series expansion - we can do this for instance by choosing an ordering on the variables and expanding as a Laurent series in each of them in turn. We do this in a consistent way, e.g. by fixing an ordering of
E(\Gamma)
.

To continue simplifying this expression, we need to process the steps involving the auxiliary variables
k_{i}
. We can do this before taking the coefficient of
\prod z_{e}^{2}
by interpreting the bracketed expression as a formal Laurent series in the
z_{e}
variables with coefficients that are polynomials in the
a_{v}
, and applying these operations coefficient by coefficient. We first consider the specialization of variables
\{a_{v}\}\mapsto\{b,a_{i},\pm k_{i}\}
. Again, performing this specialization requires choosing an ordering
S=\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{m}\}
and choosing one endpoint of each edge to add
k_{i}
and one to subtract
k_{i}
- we use the fixed orientation on edges of
\Gamma
for this. We can then check (recalling the definitions of
a_{e,T}
and
z_{e,T}
) that

\left[\sum_{e\in T}a_{e,T}z_{e}\right]_{\{a_{v}\}\mapsto\{b,a_{i},\pm k_{i}\}}=\left[\sum_{e\in T}a_{e,T}z_{e}\right]_{\{a_{v}\}\mapsto\{b,a_{i}\}}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}k_{i}(z_{e_{i},T}-z_{e_{i}}).

Using this, our expression for
(-1)^{|E(\Gamma)|}R(\Gamma)
becomes

\sum_{T\subseteq\Gamma}\sum_{m\geq 0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq E(\Gamma)\setminus E(T)\\
|S|=m\end{subarray}}\left[\prod_{e\in T}\exp(a_{e,T}z_{e})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\notin T\\
e\notin S\end{subarray}}\frac{z_{e}}{z_{e,T}}\prod_{e\in S}\left(-z_{e}^{2}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{m}\exp\left(k_{i}(z_{e_{i},T}-z_{e_{i}})\right)\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{coeff of }\prod z_{e}^{2},\\
\{a_{v}\}\mapsto\{b,a_{i}\},\\
k_{i}^{d}\mapsto\zeta(-d-1)\end{subarray}},

where again
S=\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{m}\}
.

We can now directly perform the negative zeta regularization. Note that

\displaystyle\left[\exp(kU)\right]_{k^{d}\mapsto\zeta(-d-1)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{d=0}^{\infty}\frac{-B_{d+2}}{(d+2)d!}U^{d}

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dU}\left(\frac{1}{U}\left(\frac{-U}{e^{U}-1}+1\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{e^{U}}{(e^{U}-1)^{2}}-\frac{1}{U^{2}}\right),

using the standard exponential generating function for the Bernoulli numbers.

Taking
U=z_{e_{i},T}-z_{e_{i}}
and substituting this in, our expression for
(-1)^{|E(\Gamma)|}R(\Gamma)
is now

\sum_{T\subseteq\Gamma}\sum_{m\geq 0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq E(\Gamma)\setminus E(T)\\
|S|=m\end{subarray}}\left[\prod_{e\in T}\exp(a_{e,T}z_{e})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\notin T\\
e\notin S\end{subarray}}\frac{z_{e}}{z_{e,T}}\prod_{e\in S}\left(-z_{e}^{2}\left(\frac{\exp(z_{e,T}-z_{e})}{(\exp(z_{e,T}-z_{e})-1)^{2}}-\frac{1}{(z_{e,T}-z_{e})^{2}}\right)\right)\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{coeff of }\prod z_{e}^{2},\\
\{a_{v}\}\mapsto\{b,a_{i}\}\end{subarray}},

where we’ve combined our two products over edges in
S
now that the
e_{1},\ldots,e_{m}
labels are unnecessary.

But now we can freely move the sums over
m
and
S
inside the bracket and evaluate them as the usual product of binomials. The result is

\sum_{T\subseteq\Gamma}\left[\prod_{e\in T}\exp(a_{e,T}z_{e})\prod_{e\notin T}\left(\frac{z_{e}}{z_{e,T}}-z_{e}^{2}\left(\frac{\exp(z_{e,T}-z_{e})}{(\exp(z_{e,T}-z_{e})-1)^{2}}-\frac{1}{(z_{e,T}-z_{e})^{2}}\right)\right)\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{coeff of }\prod z_{e}^{2},\\
\{a_{v}\}\mapsto\{b,a_{i}\}\end{subarray}}

This now looks a lot like (
69
