) (recall we’ve multiplied through by the sign factor
(-1)^{|E(\Gamma)|}
), but we have a different meromorphic function of
z_{e}
and
z_{e,T}
appearing inside the product over edges
e\notin T
. Note that for a fixed edge
f\notin T
, the variable
z_{f}
only appears in this one factor in the expression. We claim that (when expanded as a Laurent series in the
z_{e}
in the fixed order previously chosen) the two functions

\frac{z_{f}}{\exp(z_{f,T})-1}\quad\text{ and }\quad\left(\frac{z_{f}}{z_{f,T}}-z_{f}^{2}\left(\frac{\exp(z_{f,T}-z_{f})}{(\exp(z_{f,T}-z_{f})-1)^{2}}-\frac{1}{(z_{f,T}-z_{f})^{2}}\right)\right)

have the same coefficient of any monomial
\prod_{e}z_{e}^{d_{e}}
with exponent
d_{f}=2
. This will complete the proof of the desired identity of coefficients
L(\Gamma)=R(\Gamma)
.

To see this claim, note that the difference between these two functions is analytic near the origin
z_{e}=0
, so it suffices to expand that as a power series and check that the desired coefficients vanish. Let
X=z_{f}
and
Y=z_{f,T}-z_{f}
(which does not use the variable
z_{f}
); then it suffices to check that the coefficient of
X^{2}Y^{i}
in

\frac{X}{e^{X+Y}-1}-\frac{X}{X+Y}+X^{2}\left(\frac{e^{Y}}{\left(e^{Y}-1\right)^{2}}-\frac{1}{Y^{2}}\right)

is
0
for all
i\geq 0
. Equivalently,

\left[\frac{X}{e^{X+Y}-1}-\frac{X}{X+Y}\right]_{\text{coeff of $X^{2}$}}=-\left(\frac{e^{Y}}{\left(e^{Y}-1\right)^{2}}-\frac{1}{Y^{2}}\right),

which is easily verified by dividing by
X
, differentiating with respect to
X
, and setting
X=0
.

6.4.1
General tautological classes

We are not yet finished with the proof of Theorem
6.1
- so far, we have only verified that for pure boundary strata corresponding to stable graphs
\Gamma
with no automorphisms, the coefficients match up on both sides.

First we discuss automorphisms. Let
\Gamma
,
S\subseteq E(\Gamma)
with
|S|=m
, and
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma\setminus S
be as above. Let
M
be the set of
2^{g}\cdot g!
different ways to add and label
2m
legs to
\Gamma^{\prime}
at the endpoints of the edges in
S
. Previously the resulting
2^{g}\cdot g!
stable graphs were all nonisomorphic (and all had trivial automorphism group), but this is no longer true if
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)
is nontrivial. There is a natural action of
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)
on
M
, and elements of
M
in the same orbit will correspond to isomorphic stable graphs. Moreover, if
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
is one such stable graph then its automorphism group is isomorphic to the subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)
stabilizing the corresponding element of
M
. We can also see that
|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma^{\prime\prime})|
does not depend on the choice of
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
. By the orbit-stabilizer theorem, the number of isomorphism classes of stable graphs corresponding to elements of
M
is

\frac{|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma^{\prime\prime})|}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)|}2^{g}\cdot g!.

This modified number precisely cancels the automorphism factors on the two sides of the desired identity.

Finally, we need to discuss how to handle insertions of powers of
\psi+\psi^{\prime}
along edges. To check that coefficients match up for tautological classes with these internal
\psi
classes, we replace
\Gamma
with semistable
\widehat{\Gamma}
as described at the end of Section
6.1
. The proof that
L(\widehat{\Gamma})=R(\widehat{\Gamma})
(expressions defined using (
69
) and (
70
)) goes through unchanged, so it remains to check that this identity is indeed the equality of coefficients we want to prove to obtain Theorem
6.1
. We have that
L(\widehat{\Gamma})
is the coefficient of

\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)|}(j_{\Gamma})_{*}\prod_{e=(h,h^{\prime})\in E(\Gamma)}\frac{(-\psi_{h}-\psi_{h^{\prime}})^{d_{e}}}{(d_{e}+1)!}

for some exponents
d_{e}
. The other side requires more discussion - what happens when the set of edges
S\subseteq E(\widehat{\Gamma})
contains edges along the semistable portions of
\widehat{\Gamma}
?

Suppose a given edge
e\in E(\Gamma)
was subdivided into
m+1
edges
e_{1},\ldots,e_{m+1}\in E(\widehat{\Gamma})
, i.e.
m=d_{e}
. Then the condition that the complement of
S
is connected implies that
S
contains at most one of the
e_{i}
. Also, it is easily checked that altering
S
by replacing one of the
e_{i}
by a different one does not change the value of a term in (
70
). If we combine terms with
S
that are related in this way, we get a factor of
m+1
and can reduce to a sum over
S\subseteq E(\Gamma)
, which are the actual edges appearing in the gluing. We then get to choose powers of
\psi
on the two sides of the glued edge. The factor of
m+1
then corresponds to the identity

\sum_{i=0}^{m}\frac{(-\psi)^{i}}{i!}\cdot\frac{(-\psi^{\prime})^{m-i}}{(m-i)!}=(m+1)\cdot\frac{(-\psi-\psi^{\prime})^{m}}{m!}.

6.4.2
A related lemma

We state and prove an identity that is closely related to Theorem
6.1
here. It will be used later in Section
7
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
g,n\geq 0
. Let
j:\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g-1,n+2}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
be the gluing map gluing the last two markings. Then

(2[\text{codim}]-[\text{deg}])\mathsf{DR}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})=j_{*}\left[-\frac{k^{2}}{2}\mathsf{DR}_{g-1}(b;\mathsf{a},k,-k)\right]_{k^{d}\mapsto B_{d}}.

On the left side,
[\text{codim}]
and
[\text{deg}]
are the derivations that act (respectively) by multiplication by codimension and by multiplication by polynomial degree (in
b,a_{i}
) on the appropriate graded pieces. On the right side, the subscript indicates that powers of the formal variable
k
are replaced by the corresponding Bernoulli numbers.

Proof.

We follow the same proof strategy as for Theorem
6.1
. Things are mostly directly analogous, so we will only write down how the details change in a few key points.

Suppose we are checking that the coefficient of the pure boundary stratum
(j_{\Gamma})_{*}1
is equal on the two sides, where
\Gamma
is a stable graph with
c
legs. On the left side, we have to apply the derivation
(2[\text{codim}]-[\text{deg}])
to
C(\Gamma)
. Using Zagier’s formula for
C(\Gamma)
as in (
69
), we note that we can move
2[\text{codim}]
inside the brackets and replace it there with
[\text{deg}_{z}]
, the polynomial degree in the
z_{e}
variables. Then
[\text{deg}_{z}]-[\text{deg}_{a}]
annihilates the
\exp(a_{e,T}z_{e})
factors, so we just need to let it act on one of the other types of factor. In other words, the left side becomes

(-1)^{|E(\Gamma)|}\left[\sum_{T\subseteq\Gamma}\sum_{f\notin T}\prod_{e\in T}\exp(a_{e,T}z_{e})\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\notin T\\
e\neq f\end{subarray}}\frac{z_{e}}{\exp(z_{e,T})-1}\right)\left([\text{deg}_{z}]\frac{z_{f}}{\exp(z_{f,T})-1}\right)\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{coeff of }\prod z_{e}^{2},\\
\{a_{v}\}\mapsto\{b,a_{i}\}\end{subarray}}

Meanwhile, the right side of the identity to be proved is similar to (
71
), but we make the following changes:

•

We only have
|S|=m=1
, so we will write
S={f}
and replace the sums over
m
and
S
with a sum over edges
f
, and write
k
in place of
k_{1}
;

•

We did not use the top degree version of
\mathsf{DR}
, so the
z_{e,T}
in the denominator should be
\exp(z_{e,T}-1)
;

•

At the end we apply
k^{d}\mapsto-B_{d+2}
instead of
k^{d}\mapsto\zeta(-d-1)
.

The result is

\sum_{T\subseteq\Gamma}\sum_{f\in E(\Gamma)\setminus E(T)}\left[\prod_{e\in T}\exp(a_{e,T}z_{e})\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\notin T\\
e\neq f\end{subarray}}\frac{z_{e}}{\exp(z_{e,T})-1}\right)\left(-z_{f}^{2}\right)\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{coeff of }\prod z_{e}^{2},\\
\{a_{v}\}\mapsto\{b,a_{i},\pm k\},\\
k^{d}\mapsto-B_{d+2}\end{subarray}}.

We then proceed as before and process the steps involving
k
. This involves the new computation

\displaystyle\left[\exp(kT)\right]_{k^{d}\mapsto-B_{d+2}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{d=0}^{\infty}\frac{B_{d+2}}{d!}T^{d}

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{d}{dT}\right)^{2}\frac{T}{e^{T}-1}.

After this the two sides of the identity are of the same shape but they just use different meromorphic functions of
z_{f}
and
z_{f,T}
, as before. If we let
X=z_{f}
and
Y=z_{f,T}-z_{f}
, then we end up just needing to check that the coefficient of
X^{2}Y^{i}
in

\left(X\frac{d}{dX}+Y\frac{d}{dY}\right)\frac{X}{e^{X+Y}-1}-X^{2}\left(\left(\frac{d}{dY}\right)^{2}\frac{Y}{e^{Y}-1}\right)

is
0
for all
i\geq 0
. This is easily done with a symbolic algebra package.
∎

6.5
Top degree part of the universal
\mathsf{DR}
formula

The universal double ramification formula is used to compute the closure of the Abel-Jacobi section on the universal Picard stack
[
8
]
.
Let
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0}
denote the universal Picard stack of total degree
0
line bundles for
\mathfrak{C}_{g,n}\to\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}
(see Section
2.2
). Let
b\in\mathbb{Z}
and let
\mathsf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be a vector of integers with
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=b(2g-2+n)
. For
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we denote by
\mathsf{uniDR}_{g,r}^{c}(b;\mathsf{a})
the codimension
c
component of the tautological class

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Gamma_{\delta}\in\mathsf{G}_{g,n,0}\\
w\in\mathsf{W}_{\Gamma_{\delta},r}\end{subarray}}\frac{r^{-h^{1}(\Gamma_{\delta})}}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma_{\delta})|}\;j_{\Gamma_{\delta*}}\Bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}a_{i}^{2}\psi_{i}+a_{i}\xi_{i}\right)\prod_{v\in V(\Gamma_{\delta})}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{-1,2}(v)-b\kappa_{0,1}-\frac{b^{2}}{2}\kappa_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\prod_{e=(h,h^{\prime})\in E(\Gamma_{\delta})}\frac{1-\exp\left(-\frac{w(h)w(h^{\prime})}{2}(\psi_{h}+\psi_{h^{\prime}})\right)}{\psi_{h}+\psi_{h^{\prime}}}\Bigg]

(72)

in the operational Chow ring
\mathsf{CH}_{\mathsf{op}}^{*}(\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0})
. Here
\Gamma_{\delta}
runs over all prestable graphs
\Gamma
equipped with arbitrary multidegrees
\delta:V(\Gamma)\to\mathbb{Z}
with sum
0
. The weighting set
\mathsf{W}_{\Gamma_{\delta},r}
is defined in the same way as the set
\mathsf{W}_{\Gamma,r}
used in the
\mathsf{DR}
formula (Section
6.1
), except that
\delta(v)
is added to the right side of the vertex balancing condition.

For sufficiently large
r
, this expression is polynomial in
r
. Let
\mathsf{uniDR}^{c}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
be the constant term of this polynomial. We formally sum over
c
to define the total
\mathsf{uniDR}
cycle

\mathsf{uniDR}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a}):=\sum_{c\geq 0}\mathsf{uniDR}^{c}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})

As in (
62
), we can factor this as
\mathsf{uniDR}_{g}(b,\mathsf{a})=\exp(\mathsf{uniDRD}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a}))\mathsf{uniDRP}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
, where
\mathsf{uniDRD}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
is a divisor given as a linear combination of the
\psi_{i},\xi_{i}
, and
\kappa_{i,j}
with
i+j=1
.

For a detailed discussion of the invariant properties of the universal double ramification cycle formula, see
[
8
, Section 7]
.

We consider the “multiplication by
N
” map

[N]:\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0}\to\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0}\,.

(73)

A class
\alpha\in\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0})
has
weight
w
if
[N]^{*}\alpha=N^{w}\alpha
for all
N\in\mathbb{Z}
.
We define the subring

\mathsf{CH}^{*}_{\mathrm{pure}}(\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0}):=\bigoplus_{w\geq 0}\mathsf{CH}^{*}_{(w)}(\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0})\subset\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0})_{\mathbb{Q}}

generated by weight
w
classes. Compared to finite-type commutative group schemes (see Theorem
9.1
), this inclusion is strict because
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0}
is not finite type over
\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}
. Nevertheless, the universal double ramification formula lies in this subring.

Proposition 6.4
.

For any
b\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathsf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=(2g-2+n)b
, the class
\mathsf{uniDR}^{c}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
lies in
\mathsf{CH}^{c}_{\mathrm{pure}}(\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0})
.

Proof.

This is easily checked for the divisor class
\mathsf{uniDRD}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
- we have that
\psi_{i}
has weight
0
,
\xi_{i}
has weight
1
, and
\kappa_{i,j}
has weight
j
. For the ”piecewise polynomial part”
\mathsf{uniDRP}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
, we just need to check that the coefficient on the boundary stratum corresponding to a graph
\Gamma
with multidegree
\delta
(possibly with
-\psi-\psi^{\prime}
decorations) depends polynomially on
\delta
. But this coefficient is just one of the
\mathsf{DR}
graph invariants
C(G)
of (
64
), evaluated at
a_{v}
equal to
-\delta(v)
plus a linear combination of
b
and the
a_{i}
. Since
C(G)
is polynomial in these inputs
[
60
,
59
]
, we are done.
∎

A simple case of Proposition
6.4
first appears in
[
9
, Proposition 4.2]
. By Proposition
6.4
, we can consider the following definition:

Definition 6.5
.

We define the top degree part
\widetilde{\mathsf{uniDR}}^{c}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
as the sum over
m
of the weight
2c-m
part of the coefficient of monomials in
b,a_{i}
of degree
m
.

In other words, we use the sum of the weight grading and the polynomial codimension grading in
b,a_{i}
to define the top degree part. It is easily checked that since
C(G)
has degree at most twice the number of edges of
G
, the highest ”degree” we can get in this way in codimension
c
is
2c
(just as it was for the top degree part of regular
\mathsf{DR}
). We define the total top degree part
\widetilde{\mathsf{uniDR}}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
by summing over
c
as usual.

For
1\leq m\leq g
, we consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Pic}^{\mathbb{G}_{m}^{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Pic}_{g-m,n+2m,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{M}_{g-m,n+2m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

by gluing the pairs of markings
(n+1,n+2),\ldots,(n+2m-1,n+2m)
. The right square is Cartesian.

Theorem 6.6
.

Let
b\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathsf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=(2g-2+n)b
. Then we have

\mathsf{uniDR}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})=\sum_{m=0}^{g}\sum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{h}\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{2^{m}m!}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{m}k_{i}\Big)(j_{m})_{*}q^{*}\widetilde{\mathsf{uniDR}}_{g-m}(b;\mathsf{a},k_{1},-k_{1},\ldots,k_{m},-k_{m})\,.

Here we use the negative zeta value regularization (
66
) on the right hand side, as in Theorem
6.1
.

Proof.

The proof is nearly identical to that of Theorem
6.1
in the previous section. As before, the exponential factor
\exp(\mathsf{uniDRD}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a}))
is of pure top degree (relative to its codimension) so we can write

\widetilde{\mathsf{uniDR}}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})=\exp(\mathsf{uniDRD}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a}))\widetilde{\mathsf{uniDRP}}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a}).

We can again use the projection formula to cancel most of the exponential factors on both sides, leaving only
\psi_{n+1},\ldots,\psi_{n+2m}
(as before) and now also
\xi_{n+1},\ldots,\xi_{n+2m}
. But the
\xi
classes appear as factors of the form

\exp(k_{i}(\xi_{n+2i-1}-\xi_{n+2i})).

Since these two
\xi
classes become equal on applying
q^{*}
, these factors vanish. The only change in the remainder of the proof is that we subtract
\delta(v)
from the right side of the variable specialization (
65
).
∎

We can lift Lemma
6.3
to
\mathsf{uniDR}
in the same way. The only change required is that we must replace
[\text{deg}]
with
[\text{deg}]+[\text{weight}]
.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
g,n\geq 0
. Then

(2[\text{codim}]-[\text{deg}]-[\text{weight}])\mathsf{uniDR}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})=(j_{1})_{*}q^{*}\left[-\frac{k^{2}}{2}\mathsf{uniDR}_{g-1}(b;\mathsf{a},k,-k)\right]_{k^{d}\mapsto B_{d}}.

7
Forgetful pushforward of the
\mathsf{DR}
formula

7.1
Statement of result

In this section we prove an identity satisfied by the pushforward of the
\mathsf{DR}
formula along the forgetful map
\pi:\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n+1}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
forgetting the last marking.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
g,c,n\geq 0
. Let

F=\pi_{*}\mathsf{DR}_{g}^{c}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})\in\mathsf{CH}^{c-1}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{Q}[b,a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1}]/(a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n+1}-(2g-1+n)b)\,.

(a)

F
is a multiple of
(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}
.

(b)

We have the identity

\left[\frac{F}{(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}}\right]_{a_{n+1}:=b}=(g+1-c)\mathsf{DR}_{g}^{c-1}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}).

To see that the statement of part (b) makes sense, note that setting
a_{n+1}:=b
(i.e. quotienting by
a_{n+1}-b
) naturally induces a ring homomorphism

\mathbb{Q}[b,a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1}]/(a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n+1}-(2g-1+n)b)\to\mathbb{Q}[b,a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}]/(a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n}-(2g-2+n)b).

When
c>g
, Theorem
7.1
is an easy consequence of the
\mathsf{DR}
relations proved in
[
19
]
, since then both
\mathsf{DR}_{g}^{c}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})
and
(g+1-c)\mathsf{DR}_{g}^{c-1}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
vanish. But for
c\leq g
, it gives a new interpretation of the lower codimension parts of the
\mathsf{DR}
formula, and this is the case we need in the proof of Theorem
1.1
. Of course, on the level of the strata algebra Theorem
7.1
is new for all
c
.

The proof of Theorem
7.1
is essentially just a lengthy computation. We break it up into subsections as follows. In Section
7.2
we begin the proof and describe how to break the computation of
F
into three parts. In the following three subsections we handle each of these parts in turn. Finally, in Section
7.6
we state and prove a generalization of Theorem
7.1
to
\mathsf{uniDR}
.

7.2
Setting up the computation

Although Theorem
7.1
is stated in pure codimension, it will be convenient to sum over
c
and prove that version instead. In other words, we want to prove that the total
\mathsf{DR}
pushforward

\pi_{*}\mathsf{DR}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})

is a multiple of
(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}
and that we have the identity

\left[\frac{\pi_{*}\mathsf{DR}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})}{(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}}\right]_{a_{n+1}:=b}=(g-[\text{codim}])\mathsf{DR}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}),

(74)

where the linear operator
[\text{codim}]
acts by multiplying pure-dimensional cycles by their codimension.

We can merge these two goals into the single congruence

\pi_{*}\mathsf{DR}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})\equiv(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}(g-[\text{codim}])\mathsf{DR}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\pmod{(a_{n+1}-b)^{3}}.

(75)

Here we are abusing notation in a subtle way which we will now explain. The left side of the congruence naturally belongs to the ring (let us call it
R
) of polynomials in
b,a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1}
modulo the single relation
(2g-2+n+1)b=a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n+1}
. But it does not make sense to write
\mathsf{DR}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
in
R
, since the inputs to
\mathsf{DR}_{g}
then do not satisfy the relation
(2g-2+n)b=a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n}
in
R
. Note that this was not an issue with (
74
) because there we have set
a_{n+1}:=b
. However, the right side of (
75
) still makes sense if we use the facts that we are working mod
(a_{n+1}-b)^{3}
and that the problematic
\mathsf{DR}_{g}
factor is being multiplied by
(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}
. In other words, we think of
\mathsf{DR}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
as belonging to
R/(a_{n+1}-b)
and treat multiplication by
(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}
as an operator
R/(a_{n+1}-b)\to R/(a_{n+1}-b)^{3}
.

Whenever we write
\mathsf{DR}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
inside a congruence mod
(a_{n+1}-b)^{3}
for the remainder of this section, it will occur along with a factor of
(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}
and should be interpreted via the above procedure. Note that we have

(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}\mathsf{DR}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\equiv(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}\mathsf{DR}_{g}(b;a_{1}+a_{n+1}-b,a_{2},\ldots,a_{n})\pmod{(a_{n+1}-b)^{3}}.

This is another way to interpret the notation; the choice of which
a_{i}
to increase by
a_{n+1}-b
does not affect the value of the expression mod
(a_{n+1}-b)^{3}
.

We begin by rewriting both sides of (
75
) using the factorization
\mathsf{DR}=\exp(\mathsf{DRD})\mathsf{DRP}
and manipulating the exponential factors. Recall that

\mathsf{DRD}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})=-\frac{b^{2}}{2}\kappa_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\frac{a_{i}^{2}}{2}\psi_{i}.

Although the terms here are not equal to the pullbacks under
\pi
of the corresponding divisor classes on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
, we can write down the error terms:

\mathsf{DRD}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})=\pi^{*}\left(-\frac{b^{2}}{2}\kappa_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}^{2}}{2}\psi_{i}\right)+\frac{a_{n+1}^{2}-b^{2}}{2}\psi_{n+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}^{2}}{2}\delta_{i,n+1},

where
\delta_{i,n+1}
is the class of the boundary divisor where markings
i
and
n+1
come together and bubble off.

The projection formula then tells us that

\displaystyle\pi_{*}\mathsf{DR}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{b^{2}}{2}\kappa_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}^{2}}{2}\psi_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\quad\pi_{*}\left[\exp\left(\frac{a_{n+1}^{2}-b^{2}}{2}\psi_{n+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}^{2}}{2}\delta_{i,n+1}\right)\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})\right].

Meanwhile, on the right side of (
75
) we can use the fact that
[\text{codim}]
is a derivation to compute that

\displaystyle(g-[\text{codim}])\mathsf{DR}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})=

\displaystyle\quad\exp(\mathsf{DRD}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}))(g-[\text{codim}]-\mathsf{DRD}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}))\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})

Using both of the last two equations, we can cancel a factor of
\exp(-\frac{b^{2}}{2}\kappa_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}^{2}}{2}\psi_{i})
from both sides of (
75
) to get the equivalent congruence

\displaystyle\pi_{*}\left[\exp\left(\frac{a_{n+1}^{2}-b^{2}}{2}\psi_{n+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}^{2}}{2}\delta_{i,n+1}\right)\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})\right]\equiv

(76)

\displaystyle\quad(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}\left(g-[\text{codim}]-\mathsf{DRD}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\right)\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\pmod{(a_{n+1}-b)^{3}},

where again we should note that the
\mathsf{DRD}
and
\mathsf{DRP}
components of the expression on the right side only make sense because we are multiplying them by
(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}
.

The proof now is a matter of verifying (
76
) by carefully computing this pushforward modulo
(a_{n+1}-b)^{3}
. This can be expressed as a sum of three different types of terms via the identity

\exp\left(\frac{a_{n+1}^{2}-b^{2}}{2}\psi_{n+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}^{2}}{2}\delta_{i,n+1}\right)=\left(\exp\left(\frac{a_{n+1}^{2}-b^{2}}{2}\psi_{n+1}\right)-1\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\exp\left(\frac{a_{i}^{2}}{2}\delta_{i,n+1}\right)-1\right)+1,

which follows from the observation that the divisors appearing in the formula all have trivial intersection with each other. In other words, we can either have a power of
\psi_{n+1}
, a power of
\delta_{i,n+1}
for some
1\leq i\leq n
, or neither.

It turns out that it is natural to group part of the pushforward
\pi_{*}\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})
along with the terms with a power of
\delta_{i,n+1}
. For each
1\leq i\leq n
, let
\mathsf{DRP}_{g}^{(i,n+1)}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})
be the sum of those terms in the
\mathsf{DR}
formula sum (
63
) in which the graph
\Gamma
has a genus
0
vertex
v
with both legs
i,n+1
, no other legs, and a single incident edge.

We now state the contributions of these three types of terms:

Lemma 7.2
.

We have the congruence

\displaystyle\pi_{*}\left[\left(\exp\left(\frac{a_{n+1}^{2}-b^{2}}{2}\psi_{n+1}\right)-1\right)\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})\right]\equiv

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}\left(g+\frac{n}{2}-[\text{deg}]+\frac{b^{2}}{2}\kappa_{1}\right)\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+\quad(a_{n+1}-b)\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{i}+a_{n+1}-b,\ldots,a_{n})\pmod{(a_{n+1}-b)^{3}}.

Lemma 7.3
.

For each
1\leq i\leq n
, we have the congruence

\displaystyle\pi_{*}\left[\left(\exp\left(\frac{a_{i}^{2}}{2}\delta_{i,n+1}\right)-1\right)\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})+\mathsf{DRP}_{g}^{(i,n+1)}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})\right]\equiv

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}\left(-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{a_{i}^{2}}{2}\psi_{i}\right)\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+\quad(a_{n+1}-b)(-a_{i})\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{i}+a_{n+1}-b,\ldots,a_{n})\pmod{(a_{n+1}-b)^{3}}.

Lemma 7.4
.

We have the congruence

\displaystyle\pi_{*}\left[\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{DRP}_{g}^{(i,n+1)}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})\right]\equiv

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}\left([\text{deg}]-[\text{codim}]\right)\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\pmod{(a_{n+1}-b)^{3}}.

In the next three subsections we will prove these three lemmas. Adding Lemma
7.2
, Lemma
7.3
(summing over
i
), and Lemma
7.4
then yields (
76
) and completes the proof of Theorem
7.1
.

7.3
Proof of Lemma
7.2

We begin with the terms with a power of
\psi_{n+1}
. Since
a_{n+1}^{2}-b^{2}
is a multiple of
a_{n+1}-b
and we are working modulo
(a_{n+1}-b)^{3}
, we only need to consider terms with an exponent of
1
or
2
. The pushforward then turns this power of
\psi_{n+1}
into
\kappa_{0}=2g_{v}-2+n_{v}
or
\kappa_{1}
(on the vertex where the
(n+1)
st leg was attached).

It is tempting to conclude that this kappa class factors out (since the leg could be placed on any vertex) and that we are left with a kappa class times
\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
. This will turn out to be true when we started with
\psi_{n+1}^{2}
, but with
\psi_{n+1}
it doesn’t even make sense because we do not have the factor of
(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}
needed to use
\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
(see the discussion after equation (
75
)).

So we need to be more careful with this type of computation. Let
\Gamma
be a stable graph for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
. For simplicity we will assume that
\Gamma
has no automorphisms and we do not worry about internal
\psi
insertions - those details can be handled as in Section
6.4.1
(though things are even simpler here since we do not have gluing maps to worry about). Recall that the coefficient of the boundary stratum
(j_{\Gamma})_{*}1
in
\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
is given by the graph invariant polynomial
C(\Gamma)(\{a_{v}\})
after applying the specialization of variables (
65
).

For each vertex
w\in V(\Gamma)
, let
\Gamma_{w}
be the stable graph for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n+1}
given by attaching leg
n+1
at
w
. Then we have that the coefficient of
(j_{\Gamma_{w}})_{*}1
in
\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})
is again given by a specialization of the same
C(\Gamma)(\{a_{v}\})
. One way to describe the altered specialization in
\Gamma_{w}
as opposed to
\Gamma
is that first we replace
a_{w}
with
a_{w}+a_{n+1}-b
and then we perform the previous specialization. This motivates the following notation (to be used only in this section): let
T
be a formal variable (later to be set to
a_{n+1}-b
) and then given a vertex
w\in V(\Gamma)
, let

\mathsf{P}_{w}=\mathsf{P}_{w}(T,\{a_{v}\}):=\left[C(\Gamma)\right]_{a_{w}\mapsto a_{w}+T}\in\mathbb{Q}[T,\{a_{v}\}]\Big/\left(T+\sum_{v\in V(G)}a_{v}\right).

(77)

Also, for
1\leq i\leq n
, let
\mathsf{P}_{i}:=\mathsf{P}_{w_{i}}
where
w_{i}
is the vertex where leg
i
is located. All the different
\mathsf{P}_{w}
become equal to
C(\Gamma)
on setting
T:=0
, so in particular they are all congruent mod
T
.

We now return to the question of computing the pushforward of the
\psi_{n+1}^{i}
terms. For
\psi_{n+1}^{2}
, we have that the coefficient of the class corresponding to
\Gamma
with
\kappa_{1}
at vertex
w
is

\text{coeff}_{(j_{\Gamma})_{*}\kappa_{1}[w]}\left(\pi_{*}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{a_{n+1}^{2}-b^{2}}{2}\psi_{n+1}\right)^{2}\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})\right]\right)=\frac{(a_{n+1}^{2}-b^{2})^{2}}{8}[\mathsf{P}_{w}]_{T,\{a_{v}\}\mapsto b,\{a_{i}\}},

where the subscript indicates that we apply
T:=a_{n+1}-b
as well as the standard specialization (
65
).

Because this term is divisible by
(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}
, the dependence on
w
disappears modulo
(a_{n+1}-b)^{3}
. We can collect these terms for different
\Gamma
and
w
and factor out the
\kappa_{1}
to get (mod
(a_{n+1}-b)^{3}
) a contribution of

(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}\frac{b^{2}}{2}\kappa_{1}\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}).

(78)

This is part of the right side of Lemma
7.2
.

For
\psi_{n+1}^{1}
, things are very similar. The differences are that we do not have divisibility by
(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}
, but we do have that
\kappa_{0}[w]=2g_{w}-2+n_{w}
is a scalar. We get

\displaystyle\text{coeff}_{(j_{\Gamma})_{*}1}\left(\pi_{*}\left[\left(\frac{a_{n+1}^{2}-b^{2}}{2}\psi_{n+1}\right)\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})\right]\right)=

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\frac{a_{n+1}^{2}-b^{2}}{2}\sum_{w\in V(\Gamma)}(2g_{w}-2+n_{w})[\mathsf{P}_{w}]_{T,\{a_{v}\}\mapsto b,\{a_{i}\}}.

(79)

It turns out that we want to break this into two terms using

\frac{a_{n+1}^{2}-b^{2}}{2}=\frac{(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}}{2}+(a_{n+1}-b)b.

The first term created in this way is then divisible by
(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}
, so again we can combine terms with different
w
. Since

\sum_{w\in V(\Gamma)}(2g_{w}-2+n_{w})=2g-2+n

is independent of
w
, we end up with a contribution of

\frac{(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}}{2}(2g-2+n)\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})

(80)

towards the right side of Lemma
7.2
.

The second term is more complicated because we only have a factor of
a_{n+1}-b
, not
(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}
. We use the following lemma:

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
\mathsf{P}_{w},\mathsf{P}_{i}
be defined in terms of
C(\Gamma)
by (
77
). Then

\displaystyle(a_{n+1}-b)\left[\sum_{w\in V(\Gamma)}(2g_{w}-2+n_{w})b\cdot\mathsf{P}_{w}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\mathsf{P}_{i}\right]_{T,\{a_{v}\}\mapsto b,\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}}\equiv

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}(1-[\text{deg}])[C(\Gamma)]_{\{a_{v}\}\mapsto b,\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}}\quad\pmod{(a_{n+1}-b)^{3}}.

Proof.

Recall the formula for
a_{w}
in the specialization (
65
). If we combine the sum over
w
with the sum over
i
and pull out a negative sign, we can rewrite the left side as

-(a_{n+1}-b)\left[\sum_{w\in V(\Gamma)}a_{w}\mathsf{P}_{w}\right]_{T,\{a_{v}\}\mapsto b,\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}}.

(81)

We need to do a little algebra with Taylor polynomials now. Note that the formal identity

f(x,y+t)\equiv f(x+t,y)+t\left(\left(\frac{d}{dy}-\frac{d}{dx}\right)f\right)(x+t,y)\pmod{t^{2}}

holds for any polynomial
f(x,y)
by expanding both sides as Taylor series in
t
. Applying this to the polynomial
f=C(\Gamma)
with
x=a_{w_{1}},y=a_{w},t=T
gives the identity

\mathsf{P}_{w}\equiv\mathsf{P}_{1}+T\left[\left(\frac{d}{da_{w}}-\frac{d}{da_{w_{1}}}\right)C(\Gamma)\right]_{a_{w_{1}}\mapsto a_{w_{1}}+T}\pmod{T^{2}}.

Applying this identity to replace all the
\mathsf{P}_{w}
with
\mathsf{P}_{1}
in (
81
), we get (mod
(a_{n+1}-b)^{3})
):

\displaystyle-(a_{n+1}-b)\left[\left(\sum_{w\in V(\Gamma)}a_{w}\right)\mathsf{P}_{1}\right]_{T,\{a_{v}\}\mapsto b,\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}}

\displaystyle-(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}\left[\sum_{w\in V(\Gamma)}a_{w}\left(\frac{d}{da_{w}}-\frac{d}{da_{w_{1}}}\right)C(\Gamma)\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{w_{1}}\mapsto a_{w_{1}}+T,\\
T,\{a_{v}\}\mapsto b,\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}\end{subarray}}.

On the first line, the sum of
a_{w}
is equal to
-T
so this line simplifies to

(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}\left[\mathsf{P}_{1}\right]_{T,\{a_{v}\}\mapsto b,\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}}.

(82)

On the second line, the sum of
a_{w}
inside the brackets is equal to
0
(since it is before the change of variables introducing
T
) so the terms with
(d/da_{w_{1}})
cancel. Also, we can write

\sum_{w\in V(\Gamma)}a_{w}\frac{dC(\Gamma)}{da_{w}}=[\text{deg}]C(\Gamma),

where
[\text{deg}]
is the operator that multiplies a homogeneous polynomial by its degree, so the second line becomes simply

-(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}[\text{deg}]\left[\mathsf{P}_{1}\right]_{T,\{a_{v}\}\mapsto b,\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}}.

(83)

Since both (
82
) and (
83
) have a factor of
(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}
, we can replace
\mathsf{P}_{1}
with
C(\Gamma)
and add them to get the right side of the lemma.
∎

We now apply Lemma
7.5
to complete the proof of Lemma
7.2
. The remaining contribution we had to analyze had coefficient

(a_{n+1}-b)b\sum_{w\in V(\Gamma)}(2g_{w}-2+n_{w})[\mathsf{P}_{w}]_{T,\{a_{v}\}\mapsto b,\{a_{i}\}}

(on the class of the boundary stratum corresponding to
\Gamma
). By Lemma
7.5
, we can replace this with

(a_{n+1}-b)\sum_{w\in V(\Gamma)}a_{i}[\mathsf{P}_{i}]_{T,\{a_{v}\}\mapsto b,\{a_{i}\}}+(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}(1-[\text{deg}])[C(\Gamma)]_{\{a_{v}\}\mapsto b,\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}}.

But these terms can easily be summed over
\Gamma
and rewritten in terms of
\mathsf{DRP}_{g}
. The result is

(a_{n+1}-b)\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{i}+a_{n+1}-b,\ldots,a_{n})+(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}(1-[\text{deg}])\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}).

Adding this to (
78
) and (
80
) gives the right side of Lemma
7.2
.

7.4
Proof of Lemma
7.3

We now move on to the term with a power of
\delta_{i,n+1}
(for a single fixed
1\leq i\leq n
): we wish to compute

\pi_{*}\left[\left(\exp\left(\frac{a_{i}^{2}}{2}\delta_{i,n+1}\right)-1\right)\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})\right].

Let
\iota:\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n+1}
be the map corresponding to the boundary divisor
\delta_{i,n+1}
, i.e. we glue a rational bubble to the
i
th marking and place markings
i
and
n+1
on the bubble. The projection formula (along with
\pi\circ\iota=\mathrm{id}
and the standard formula for the normal bundle of a boundary divisor) then gives that

\pi_{*}(\delta_{i,n+1}^{k+1}\alpha)=(-\psi_{i})^{k}\iota^{*}\alpha

for any class
\alpha\in\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n+1})
.

Since
\mathsf{DR}
cycles pull back to
\mathsf{DR}
cycles along gluing maps at separating nodes, we can compute

\displaystyle\iota^{*}\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})

\displaystyle=\exp(\mathsf{DRD}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{i}+a_{n+1}-b,\ldots,a_{n})-\iota^{*}\mathsf{DRD}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1}))

\displaystyle\quad\quad\cdot\,\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{i}+a_{n+1}-b,\ldots,a_{n})

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{(a_{i}+a_{n+1}-b)^{2}}{2}\psi_{i}\right)\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{i}+a_{n+1}-b,\ldots,a_{n}).

Combining these equations, we have that

\displaystyle\pi_{*}\left[\left(\exp\left(\frac{a_{i}^{2}}{2}\delta_{i,n+1}\right)-1\right)\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{a_{i}^{2}}{2}\psi_{i}\right)-1}{-\psi_{i}}\exp\left(\frac{(a_{i}+a_{n+1}-b)^{2}}{2}\psi_{i}\right)\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{i}+a_{n+1}-b,\ldots,a_{n}).

This looks a little worrying - we have high powers of
\psi_{i}
, but the right side of Lemma
7.3
only allows a single
\psi_{i}
. However, this is why we include in Lemma
7.3
the additional terms with no
\delta_{i,n+1}
but where the
\mathsf{DRP}_{g}
factor uses a graph with a similar shape, with legs
i
and
n+1
bubbled off.

Such stable graphs for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n+1}
are in bijection with all stable graphs for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
. Let
\Gamma
be the stable graph on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
, i.e. the one obtained after forgetting leg
n+1
and contracting the resulting unstable component. Then the
\mathsf{DR}
formula gives that the pushforward of these terms is

\frac{1-\exp\left(\frac{(a_{i}+a_{n+1}-b)^{2}}{2}\psi_{i}\right)}{\psi_{i}}\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{i}+a_{n+1}-b,\ldots,a_{n}).

Adding this to the previous expression, we get

\frac{1-\exp\left(\frac{(a_{i}+a_{n+1}-b)^{2}-a_{i}^{2}}{2}\psi_{i}\right)}{\psi_{i}}\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{i}+a_{n+1}-b,\ldots,a_{n}).

Since
(a_{i}+a_{n+1}-b)^{2}-a_{i}^{2}
is divisible by
a_{n+1}-b
, terms with a high power of
\psi_{i}
will vanish modulo
(a_{n+1}-b)^{3}
. We are left with

\displaystyle\frac{a_{i}^{2}-(a_{i}+a_{n+1}-b)^{2}}{2}\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{i}+a_{n+1}-b,\ldots,a_{n})

\displaystyle+\frac{-((a_{i}+a_{n+1}-b)^{2}-a_{i}^{2})^{2}}{8}\psi_{i}\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{i}+a_{n+1}-b,\ldots,a_{n}).

The second line is a multiple of
(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}
. After reducing modulo
(a_{n+1}-b)^{3}
it becomes

(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}\left(\frac{-a_{i}^{2}}{2}\psi_{i}\right)\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}),

which is part of the right side of Lemma
7.3
.

We split the first line into a sum of two terms using

\frac{a_{i}^{2}-(a_{i}+a_{n+1}-b)^{2}}{2}=-\frac{(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}}{2}-(a_{n+1}-b)a_{i}.

This gives the remaining components of the right side of Lemma
7.3
(since in the first term we have a factor of
(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}
so can change
a_{i}+a_{n+1}-b
back to
a_{i}
).

7.5
Proof of Lemma
7.4

What remains is to compute the part of

\pi_{*}\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})

that does not involve popping a rational bubble containing leg
n+1
and one other leg.

There are two different things that can happen with this pushforward. The first is that leg
n+1
is on a vertex that becomes unstable without that leg. Since we’ve already considered the case in which this happens with another leg present, this means it has two incident edges. In other words, we are popping a rational bubble formed by leg
n+1
moving into a node. In the other case, the graph remains stable without leg
n+1
. The pushforward is then computed by decreasing the exponent of
\psi
along one incident half-edge.

We group terms together based on the stable graph
\Gamma
for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
(given after possibly stabilizing by popping a rational bubble) along with a chosen edge
f\in E(\Gamma)
(either the node where the rational bubble was located or the edge along which the exponent of a
\psi
class was decremented. Thus after picking
\Gamma
and
f
there are three possibilities for the starting stable graph
\Gamma^{\prime}
for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n+1}
that we are grouping together - either the edge is subdivided with a genus
0
vertex and leg
n+1
is placed there, or leg
n+1
is placed on one of the two endpoints of the edge.

We now consider the
\mathsf{DR}
formula for these terms. Let
r>0
be a positive integer. Then we want to sum over half-edge weightings
w:H(\Gamma^{\prime})\to\{0,\ldots,r-1\}
satisfying certain congruence conditions mod
r
. Although the graph
\Gamma^{\prime}
and the precise congruence conditions vary in the three cases described above, there are bijections between the weighting sets
W
in the different cases that keep things unchanged away from the edge
f
(or its subdivision). We show the effects of these bijections on the weights near the edge
f
below. Here
T:=a_{n+1}-b
,
A
is an arbitrary integer, and the weights should all be interpreted mod
r
.

-A
A
a_{n+1}
T-A
A-T
-A
A
a_{n+1}
T-A
A-T
a_{n+1}

We now simply add up the pushforwards of these three types of term, with corresponding weights grouped together. The result is something that looks a lot like the formula for
\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
, but with the factor for edge
f
changed:

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Gamma\in\mathsf{G}_{g,n}\\
f\in E(\Gamma)\end{subarray}}\left[\frac{r^{-h^{1}(\Gamma)}}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma,f)|}(j_{\Gamma})_{*}\Bigg[\sum_{w\in\mathsf{W}}\left(\prod_{e=(h,h^{\prime})\neq f\in E(\Gamma)}\frac{1-\exp\left(-\frac{w(h)w(h^{\prime})}{2}(\psi_{h}+\psi_{h^{\prime}})\right)}{\psi_{h}+\psi_{h^{\prime}}}\right)Q(A,T,r)\Bigg]\right]_{r=0},

where
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma,f)
is the stabilizer of
f
in
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)
,
A
is the value of
w
on one of the two half-edges in
f
(chosen arbitrarily),
\mathsf{W}
is a suitable set of weightings, and
Q(A,T,r)
is a somewhat messy function of
A,T,r
(with values that are power series in
\psi,\psi^{\prime}
on the two halves of
f
).

To write down
Q
, it will be convenient to let
[x]_{r}
for an integer
x
denote the unique integer between
0
and
r-1
congruent to
x
(mod
r
). Also, to save writing we will replace
w(h^{\prime})
with
-w(h)
in the
\mathsf{DR}
formula - this will not affect the result at the end after taking the
r^{0}
coefficient. We can then take

Q(A,T,r)=\frac{-\psi^{\prime}\exp\left(\frac{A^{2}}{2}(\psi+\psi^{\prime})\right)+(\psi+\psi^{\prime})\exp\left(\frac{[A-T]_{r}^{2}}{2}\psi+\frac{A^{2}}{2}\psi^{\prime}\right)-\psi\exp\left(\frac{[A-T]_{r}^{2}}{2}(\psi+\psi^{\prime})\right)}{\psi\psi^{\prime}(\psi+\psi^{\prime})}.

Here the
\psi
and
\psi^{\prime}
in the denominator come from the terms where the pushforward decrements the exponent of
\psi
or
\psi^{\prime}
.

Let
\widehat{Q}(A,T,r)
be the same expression with the instances of
[A-T]_{r}
replaced by
A-T
. Then expanding in
T
gives (after a straightforward calculus computation) that

\widehat{Q}(A,T,r)=T^{2}\left(\frac{-A^{2}}{2}\exp\left(\frac{A^{2}}{2}(\psi+\psi^{\prime})\right)\right)+O(T^{3}).

Since we will set
T:=a_{n+1}-b
and work modulo
(a_{n+1}-b)^{3}
, this means that if we were using
\widehat{Q}
instead of
Q
, we would get a contribution divisible by
(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}
. Moreover, note that

\frac{-A^{2}}{2}\exp\left(\frac{A^{2}}{2}(\psi+\psi^{\prime})\right)=(1+[\text{codim}])\frac{1-\exp\left(\frac{A^{2}}{2}(\psi+\psi^{\prime})\right)}{\psi+\psi^{\prime}}.

Since each edge also contributes
1
to the codim after applying
(j_{\Gamma})_{*}
, this means that we would get precisely

(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}\cdot[\text{codim}]\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}).

(84)

It remains to compute the above contribution using
Q(A,T,r)-\widehat{Q}(A,T,r)
instead of
Q(A,T,r)
. We may assume that
r>T>0
; then
Q(A,T,r)-\widehat{Q}(A,T,r)
vanishes for
T\leq A<r
and agrees with a formal power series in
\psi,\psi^{\prime}
with coefficients that are polynomials in
A,T,r
for
0\leq A<T
. Call this power series
\overline{Q}
. Explicitly, we have

\displaystyle\overline{Q}(A,T,r)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\psi^{\prime}(\psi+\psi^{\prime})}\left(\exp\left(\frac{(A-T+r)^{2}}{2}(\psi+\psi^{\prime})\right)-\exp\left(\frac{(A-T)^{2}}{2}(\psi+\psi^{\prime})\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\exp\left(\frac{A^{2}}{2}\psi^{\prime}\right)}{\psi\psi^{\prime}}\left(\exp\left(\frac{(A-T+r)^{2}}{2}\psi\right)-\exp\left(\frac{(A-T)^{2}}{2}\psi\right)\right).

We wish to compute

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Gamma\in\mathsf{G}_{g,n}\\
f\in E(\Gamma)\end{subarray}}\left[\frac{r^{-h^{1}(\Gamma)}}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma,f)|}(j_{\Gamma})_{*}\Bigg[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\mathsf{W}\\
0\leq A<T\end{subarray}}\left(\prod_{e=(h,h^{\prime})\neq f\in E(\Gamma)}\frac{1-\exp\left(-\frac{w(h)w(h^{\prime})}{2}(\psi_{h}+\psi_{h^{\prime}})\right)}{\psi_{h}+\psi_{h^{\prime}}}\right)\overline{Q}(A,T,r)\Bigg]\right]_{r=0}.

If
f
is a separating edge, then
A
is uniquely determined by the balancing conditions on
w
and the factor
\overline{Q}
comes out of the sum over
w
. The sum over
w
is then just the product of the sums appearing in the
\mathsf{DR}
formula for the two connected components formed by cutting the separating edge
f
. In particular, it is polynomial in
r
for
r
sufficiently large and divisible by
r^{h^{1}(\Gamma)}
. Since
\overline{Q}
is divisible by
r
, the
r=0
specialization then vanishes.

So we can replace the sum over
f
with a sum over non-separating edges
f
. In this case, we can factor the sum over
w
by first summing over
A
:

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Gamma\in\mathsf{G}_{g,n}\\
f\in E(\Gamma)_{\text{nonsep}}\end{subarray}}\left[\sum_{k=0}^{T-1}\frac{r^{-h^{1}(\Gamma)}}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma,f)|}(j_{\Gamma})_{*}\Bigg[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\mathsf{W}\\
A=k\end{subarray}}\left(\prod_{e=(h,h^{\prime})\neq f\in E(\Gamma)}\frac{1-\exp\left(-\frac{w(h)w(h^{\prime})}{2}(\psi_{h}+\psi_{h^{\prime}})\right)}{\psi_{h}+\psi_{h^{\prime}}}\right)\overline{Q}(k,T,r)\Bigg]\right]_{r=0}.

But the subset
\{w\in\mathsf{W}\mid A=k\}
is naturally in bijection with the set of balanced weightings for the graph
\Gamma^{\prime}
formed by deleting edge
f
, attaching a new leg at each endpoint of
f
, and appending weights
k,-k
to the vector
\mathsf{a}
. So we have the
\mathsf{DRP}
formula for
\Gamma^{\prime}
, but we have the extra factor
\overline{Q}(k,T,r)
. We also have an extra factor of
r^{-1}
since
h^{1}(\Gamma^{\prime})=h^{1}(\Gamma)-1
. Fortunately,
\overline{Q}(k,T,r)
is divisible by
r
, so we get that the expression inside the sum over
k
is at least a polynomial in
r
(for
r
sufficiently large).

We can modify
\overline{Q}/r
by any multiple of
r
without changing the resulting
r\geq 0
specialization (the taking of which commutes with summing over
k
). We can also modify it by any multiple of
T^{2}
without changing the final result (mod
(a_{n+1}-b)^{3}
), since the sum over
k
gives an extra factor of
T
. A simple calculus computation gives that

\frac{\overline{Q}(k,T,r)}{r}\equiv k^{2}\exp\left(\frac{k^{2}}{2}(\psi+\psi^{\prime})\right)T\pmod{(r,T^{2})}.

Note that

\frac{k^{2}}{2}(\psi_{n+1}+\psi_{n+2})=\mathsf{DRD}_{g-1}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},k,-k)-j^{*}\mathsf{DRD}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}),

where
j
is the map gluing the last two markings. We can then rewrite our expression (using the projection formula) as

T^{2}\exp(-\mathsf{DRD}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}))\frac{1}{T}\sum_{k=0}^{T-1}\frac{k^{2}}{2}j_{*}\mathsf{DR}_{g-1}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},k,-k).

Here the factor of
\frac{1}{2}
comes from the choice of labels of legs
n+1
and
n+2
after cutting
\Gamma
at
f
.

Since the coefficient of
T^{1}
in
\sum_{k=0}^{T-1}k^{d+2}
is precisely the Bernoulli number
B_{d+2}
, this gives the negative of the right side of Lemma
6.3
(times
T^{2}
, mod
T^{3}
, with the exponential factor removed). Therefore it is equal to

(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}([\text{deg}]-2[\text{codim}])\mathsf{DRP}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a}).

When combined with the earlier contribution (
84
) in this section, we get the right side of Lemma
7.4
. This completes the proof of Lemma
7.4
(and thus also Theorem
7.1
).

7.6
Extension to
\mathsf{uniDR}

As in Section
6.5
, we generalize the pushforward identity Theorem
7.1
to the context of
\mathsf{uniDR}
. One complication that arises here is that compared with
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
, the forgetful morphism
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n+1}\to\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n}
is not proper. There are actually two separate things that go wrong. The first is that the forgetful morphism for stacks of prestable curves
\mathfrak{M}_{g,n+1}\to\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}
is already not proper. We address this by using curves with valuation in a semigroup, following
[
11
, Section 2.2]
.

Consider the semi-group
\mathcal{A}=\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}
and consider the moduli stack of
\mathcal{A}
-valued prestable curves
\mathfrak{M}_{g,n,\mathbf{1}}
. These are prestable curves with an additional choice of element
a_{v}\in\mathcal{A}
for every vertex
v
, such that every vertex with
a_{v}=0
satisfies the usual stability condition. The universal curve is denoted
p:\mathfrak{C}_{g,n,\mathbf{1}}\to\mathfrak{M}_{g,n,\mathbf{1}}
. By
[
11
, Proposition 2.6]
, the morphism
\mathfrak{M}_{g,n+1,\mathbf{1}}\to\mathfrak{M}_{g,n,\mathbf{1}}
forgetting the last marking can be identified with the universal curve. Let
F:\mathfrak{M}_{g,n,\mathbf{1}}\to\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}
be the morphism forgetting the
\mathcal{A}
-valuation.

Let
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,\mathbf{1},0}\to\mathfrak{M}_{g,n,\mathbf{1}}
be the universal degree
0
Picard stack for the family
\mathfrak{C}_{g,n,\mathbf{1}}/\mathfrak{M}_{g,n,\mathbf{1}}
. Let
p:\mathfrak{C}_{g,n,\mathbf{1}}\to\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,\mathbf{1},0}
be the pullback of the universal curve and let
\mathcal{L}
be the universal line bundle on
\mathfrak{C}_{g,n,\mathbf{1}}
. Then tuple
(\mathfrak{C}_{g,n,\mathbf{1}}/\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,\mathbf{1},0},\mathcal{L})
defines a
\mathsf{uniDR}
formula
\mathsf{uniDR}_{g,\mathbf{1}}(b;\mathsf{a})\in\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,\mathbf{1},0})
. This
\mathsf{uniDR}
formula looks just like the standard
\mathsf{uniDR}
formula, i.e. it is blind to the
\mathcal{A}
-valuation in the following sense:

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
F:\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,\mathbf{1},0}\to\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0}
be the morphism forgetting the
\mathcal{A}
-valuation. Then
F^{*}\mathsf{uniDR}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})=\mathsf{uniDR}_{g,\mathbf{1}}(b;\mathsf{a})
.

Proof.

For the forgetful morphism
F:\mathfrak{M}_{g,n,\mathbf{1}}\to\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}
, the pullback of the universal curve
\mathfrak{C}_{g,n}\to\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}
along
F
is the universal curve
\mathfrak{C}_{g,n,\mathbf{1}}
. Therefore, the same holds for
F:\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,\mathbf{1},0}\to\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0}
. Moreover, the universal line bundle on
\mathfrak{C}_{g,n,\mathbf{1}}
is the pullback of the universal line bundle on
\mathfrak{C}_{g,n}
so the
\mathsf{uniDR}
cycle formula on
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0}
pulls back to the
\mathsf{uniDR}
cycle formula for
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,\mathbf{1},0}
.
∎

However, now we come to our second problem: there is no forgetful map from
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n+1,\mathbf{1},0}
to
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,\mathbf{1},0}
. The issue is that we might need to contract a component of the curve on which we have a nontrivial line bundle. We address this by using an open substack
\mathfrak{Pic}^{\prime}_{g,n+1,\mathbf{1},0}
that does admit a proper forgetful map to
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,\mathbf{1},0}
. This is simply the open substack determined by the condition that if the underlying curve is such that forgetting marking
n+1
would make a component unstable, then the line bundle must be trivial on that component (which is necessarily genus
0
). Equivalently, we can construct
\mathfrak{Pic}^{\prime}_{g,n+1,\mathbf{1},0}
by pulling back the forgetful map on
\mathcal{A}
-valued prestable curves, as follows.

We consider the fiber product

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Pic}^{\prime}_{g,n+1,\mathbf{1},0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,\mathbf{1},0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{M}_{g,n+1,\mathbf{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{M}_{g,n,\mathbf{1}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

By
[
11
, eq. (22)]
, there exists a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{C}_{g,n+1,\mathbf{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{g,n,\mathbf{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{C}_{g,n,\mathbf{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n+1,\mathbf{1},0}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,\mathbf{1},0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

where the square is the fiber product and
r
is the canonical map which is proper birational. The tuple
(\mathfrak{C}_{g,n+1,\mathbf{1}}/\mathfrak{Pic}^{\prime}_{g,n+1,\mathbf{1},0},r^{*}\mathcal{L})
defines a
\mathsf{uniDR}
formula

\mathsf{uniDR}^{\prime}_{g,\mathbf{1}}(b;\mathsf{a})\in\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\mathfrak{Pic}^{\prime}_{g,n+1,\mathbf{1},0}).

We can now push forward
\mathsf{uniDR}^{\prime}_{g,\mathbf{1}}
along
p
and compare the result with
\mathsf{uniDR}_{g,\mathbf{1}}
, lifting
Theorem
7.1
:

Theorem 7.7
.

Let
g,c,n\geq 0
. Let

F=p_{*}\mathsf{uniDR}^{\prime c}_{g,\mathbf{1}}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})\in\mathsf{CH}^{c-1}(\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,\mathbf{1},0})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{Q}[b,a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1}]/(a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n+1}-(2g-1+n)b)\,.

(a)

F
is a multiple of
(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}
.

(b)

We have the identity

\left[\frac{F}{(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}}\right]_{a_{n+1}:=b}=(g+1-c)\mathsf{uniDR}_{g,\mathbf{1}}^{c-1}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}).

Proof.

We use the same proof strategy as before, noting only the changes that happen with
\mathsf{uniDR}
. As before, we begin by canceling exponential factors as much as possible using the projection formula – this step requires pulling back
\mathsf{uniDRD}
along the forgetful map. The exponential factor in (
76
) now includes an additional term:

(a_{n+1}-b)\xi_{n+1}.

There are no changes to the right side other than having extra terms
\xi_{i},\kappa_{0,1},\kappa_{-1,2}
inside the divisor
\mathsf{uniDRD}
.

We again divide the computation into the same three pieces as before, with the only difference being that each now carries an additional factor of
\exp((a_{n+1}-b)\xi_{n+1})
.

7.6.1
Changes in Section
7.3

Here, where we have a power of
\psi_{n+1}
, there are two places where things change. The first is that we get an additional term with pushing forward
\psi_{n+1}\xi_{n+1}
(which produces a
\kappa_{0,1}[v]
on the vertex where leg
n+1
is attached). Since this term is divisible by
(a_{n+1}+b)^{2}
, computing its contribution is easy - we get precisely

(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}b\kappa_{0,1}\mathsf{uniDRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}),

which accounts for the new
\kappa_{0,1}
term in the
\mathsf{uniDRD}
factor.

Second, Lemma
7.5
requires changes because the specialization (
65
) used to write
a_{w}
in terms of
b,a_{i}
now also includes the term
-\delta(w)
. This means that the left side of Lemma
7.5
must include
+\sum_{w}\delta(w)\mathsf{P}_{w}
inside the brackets, while on the right side
[\text{deg}]
must be replaced by
[\text{deg}]+[\text{weight}]
(since the degree of
C(\Gamma)
in the
a_{v}
variables corresponds to the mixed degree of
\mathsf{uniDRP}
in the
b,a_{i},\delta(v)
variables and the weight in
\mathsf{uniDRP}
is the degree in the
\delta(v)
variables). Both of these changes produce additional contributions which will be exactly canceled out by changes described in Section
7.6.3
below.

7.6.2
Changes in Section
7.4

The first thing to note here is that the correct analogue of
\mathsf{DRP}_{g}^{(i,n+1)}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})
for
\mathsf{uniDR}^{\prime}
is to require that the rational bubble containing legs
i,n+1
must also have
\mathcal{A}
-valuation
0
(which then also implies that it has
\delta(v)=0
). The boundary strata of
\mathfrak{Pic}^{\prime}_{g,n+1,\mathbf{1},0}
with this property are in bijection with the boundary strata of
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,\mathbf{1},0}
, as desired.

Then in both types of terms in this section, the
\xi_{n+1}
exponential just factors out to become an exponential of
\xi_{i}
after the pushforward. Since positive powers of
\xi_{n+1}
come with factors of
a_{n+1}-b
, the only new thing that we get (mod
(a_{n+1}-b)^{3}
) comes from the part of the previous result that was not yet divisible by
(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}
(the final line of Lemma
7.3
). So the new contribution is

(a_{n+1}-b)(-a_{i})(\exp((a_{n+1}-b)\xi_{i})-1)\mathsf{uniDRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{i}+a_{n+1}-b,\ldots,a_{n}).

Reducing mod
(a_{n+1}-b)^{3}
gives us

-(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i}\mathsf{uniDRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}),

which accounts for the new
\xi_{i}
term in the
\mathsf{uniDRD}
factor.

7.6.3
Changes in Section
7.5

Here there are multiple new things that happen. One thing that happens is that we are no longer just decrementing an incident
\psi
exponent when the vertex remains stable - we also need to handle the
\xi
exponent. In other words, we need to know the formula for

\pi_{*}\big(\xi_{n+1}^{i}\psi_{1}^{e_{1}}\cdots\psi_{n}^{e_{n}}\big).

Using the relation
\psi_{i}=\pi^{*}\psi_{i}+\delta_{i,n+1}
and applying the projection formula, we find that two types of terms arise: either we replace the
\xi_{n+1}^{i}
by
\kappa_{-1,i}[v]
, or we decrement one of the
\psi
exponents and convert
\xi_{n+1}
into a corresponding
\xi_{i}
.

In the first case, we must have
i\leq 2
in order to obtain a nontrivial contribution, since for
i>2
the expression is divisible by
(a_{n+1}-b)^{3}
. When
i=1
, we use the identity
\kappa_{-1,1}[v]=\delta(v)
, which precisely cancels one of the changes involving
\delta(w)\mathsf{P}_{w}
from Section
7.6.1
. When
i=2
, the extra factor of
(a_{n+1}-b)
allows us to combine terms across different vertices
v
, and factor out a global
\kappa_{-1,2}
term. This yields

(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}\frac{\kappa_{-1,2}}{2}\mathsf{uniDRP}_{g}(b;a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}),

which accounts for the new
\kappa_{-1,2}
term in the
\mathsf{uniDRD}
factor.

In the second case, we just get the same thing as before except with an extra power of
\xi
along the edge
f
. We also just get an extra power of
\xi
when pushing forward a class on a graph that requires restabilization. Thus, the
\xi
factors out of all such terms, and any term involving a positive power of
\xi
vanishes due to the presence of an extra factor of
(a_{n+1}-b)
.

The final change that happens is that we replace Lemma
6.3
with Lemma
6.7
. This just means that we are replacing
[\text{deg}]
with
[\text{deg}]+[\text{weight}]
, which cancels out with the previous time when we did this (in Section
7.6.1
).
∎

8
Fourier transform and pushforward

8.1
Leading term of the Fourier transform

Let
\epsilon_{0}
be a small stability condition (i.e. such that the trivial line bundle is
\epsilon_{0}
-stable) and let
\epsilon
be any nondegenerate stability condition. By Theorem
5.1
, we have a Fourier transform

\mathfrak{F}_{g}:\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon_{0}})\,.

(85)

Since
\epsilon_{0}
is small, we have the unit section
e:B\to\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon_{0}}
.

Proposition 8.1
.

For a nondegenerate stability condition
\epsilon
and a small nondegenerate stability condition
\epsilon_{0}
, let
\mathfrak{F}
be the Fourier transform (
85
). For any
\alpha\in\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
, we have

\pi_{*}(\alpha)=e^{*}\mathfrak{F}_{g}(\alpha)

Proof.

Let
\pi_{1}:\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}\to\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon}
and
\pi_{2}:\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}\to\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon_{0}}
be the two projections.
Consider the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{C}^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon_{0}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}

(86)

where
\pi_{2},\pi
are flat by Proposition
3.9
, and
\tilde{e}
is the map induced by
\textup{id}\times e
. Since
e:B\to\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon_{0}}
factors through
J^{\epsilon_{0}}_{C}
, the fiber product is isomorphic to
\overline{J}_{C}^{\epsilon}
. Therefore, since
e
is a regular embedding, we have

\displaystyle e^{*}\mathfrak{F}_{g}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=e^{*}(\pi_{2})_{*}(\pi_{1}^{*}(\alpha)\mathrm{td}(-T_{\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}})\tau(\overline{\mathcal{P}}))

\displaystyle=\pi_{*}\widetilde{e}^{*}(\pi_{1}^{*}(\alpha)\mathrm{td}(-T_{\overline{J}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}})\tau(\overline{\mathcal{P}}))

\displaystyle=\pi_{*}(\alpha\cup\mathrm{ch}(\widetilde{e}^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}))=\pi_{*}(\alpha)

where the second follows from the base change formula applied to (
86
) and the last equality follows from the Todd class calculation in the proof of Proposition
5.2
and
\widetilde{e}^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\cong\mathcal{O}
.
∎

The same argument implies
\pi_{*}(\alpha)=e^{*}\mathfrak{F}_{g}^{\circ}(\alpha)
, which will be used later.

We state a consequence of
[
8
]
. This formula will serve as the first step in computing the Fourier transform using the universal
\mathsf{DR}
cycle formula. Let
\varphi:\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\to\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n}
be the morphism (
10
).

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
b\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathsf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=(2g-2+n)b
. For any nondegenerate stability condition
\epsilon
of degree
0
, we have

(\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}})_{*}[B_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}]=\mathsf{uniDR}^{g}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})|_{\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}}\in\mathsf{CH}^{g}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\,.

Proof.

We consider the Abel-Jacobi map for the universal Picard stack
[
8
, (3.1)]
:

\mathsf{AJ}_{b;\mathsf{a}}:\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}\to\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n},(C,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\mapsto\omega_{C,\log}^{\otimes-b}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}x_{i})\,.

Consider the schematic image
\overline{\mathsf{AJ}_{b;\mathsf{a}}}\subset\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n}
which is the smallest closed reduced substack through which
\mathsf{AJ}_{b;\mathsf{a}}
factors. Since
B_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}
is irreducible and proper, the image
\mathrm{im}(\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}})
is a closed irreducible subset of
\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
. Both
\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}
and
\mathsf{AJ}_{b;\mathsf{a}}
agree over the locus where the underlying curve is smooth, so we have
\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}=\overline{\varphi^{-1}(\mathsf{AJ}_{b;\mathsf{a}})}
. Since
\varphi
is smooth,
\overline{\varphi^{-1}(\mathsf{AJ}_{b;\mathsf{a}})}=\varphi^{-1}(\overline{\mathsf{AJ}_{b;\mathsf{a}}})
by
[
61
, 081I]
. Therefore we get

\mathrm{im}(\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}})=\varphi^{-1}(\overline{\mathsf{AJ}_{b;\mathsf{a}}})\,.

(87)

We compare (
87
) with the universal
\mathsf{DR}
cycle formula.
Since
\varphi
is smooth (Lemma
2.2
), by
[
8
, Theorem 0.7]
, we have

\mathsf{uniDR}^{g}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})|_{\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}}=[\varphi^{-1}(\overline{\mathsf{AJ}_{b;\mathsf{a}}})]\,.

Since morphism
\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}:B_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}\to\mathrm{im}(\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}})
is birational,
(\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}})_{*}[B_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}]=[\mathrm{im}(\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}})]
. Therefore, we have
(\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}})_{*}[B_{b;\mathsf{a}}^{\epsilon}]=[\mathrm{im}(\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}})]=[\varphi^{-1}(\overline{\mathsf{AJ}_{b;\mathsf{a}}})]=\mathsf{uniDR}^{g}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})|_{\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}}
.
∎

When
\epsilon
is small, Proposition
8.2
follows from
[
18
, Theorem b]
.

We compute the lowest codimension nontrivial term of the Fourier transform up to codimension
g
, which will be the main input to prove Theorem
1.1
.
For a class
x\in\mathsf{CH}^{*}(J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n})
,
[x]_{\mathrm{codim}=c}
denotes the codimension
c
part.

Theorem 8.3
.

For a nondegenerate stability condition
\epsilon
on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
, we consider the partial Fourier transform
\mathfrak{F}_{g}^{\circ}:=\mathrm{ch}(\mathcal{P}^{\vee}):\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\to\mathsf{CH}^{*}(J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n})
. Let
b\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathsf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=(2g-2+n)b
.

(a)

If
c<g
, then
\big[\mathfrak{F}_{g}^{\circ}\big(\exp(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i})\big)\big]_{\mathrm{codim}=c}=0
.

(b)

If
c=g
, then
\big[\mathfrak{F}_{g}^{\circ}\big(\exp(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i})\big)\big]_{\mathrm{codim}=g}=(-1)^{g}\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{uniDR}}^{g}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})|_{J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n}}
.

Proof.

First, we collect the relevant results from previous sections. Let
\epsilon_{0}
be a small stability condition and let
\mathfrak{F}_{g}:\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon_{0}})\to\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
be the Fourier transform. By Proposition
5.8
, the Fourier transform of the resolved Abel-Jacobi section has the form

\mathfrak{F}_{g}\big([\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}]\big)=\exp(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i})(1+\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}})\,,

where
\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}}\in\mathsf{PP}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})
is a class supported away from the integral locus. By taking the inverse Fourier transform
\mathfrak{F}_{g}^{-1}
and restricting to
J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n}
, we obtain

[\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}]=\mathfrak{F}^{-1}_{g}\Big(\exp(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i})(1+\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}})\Big)\,.

(88)

Using Propositions
4.6
,
5.9
and
5.12
, the right-hand side of (
88
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle(-1)^{g}\cdot\mathfrak{F}_{g}^{\circ}\Big(\exp(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i}a_{i}\xi_{i})(1+\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}})\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+(-1)^{g}\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{g}\frac{(-1)^{h}}{h!2^{h}}j_{h*}q_{h}^{*}\mathfrak{F}_{g-h}^{\circ}\Big(\exp(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i})(1+\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}})\prod_{i=1}^{h}\sum_{k_{i}=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k_{i}}B_{2k_{i}}}{(2k_{i})!}\frac{\alpha_{i}^{2k_{i}-1}+\beta_{i}^{2k_{i}-1}}{\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i}}\Big)\,.

(89)

Below, we use (
89
) to match the recursive structure of
\mathsf{uniDR}^{g}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
(Theorem
6.6
) and the recursive structure of Fourier transform (Theorem
5.11
).

(a) We prove the statement by double induction on the genus
g
and the codimension
c
. For the base case
g=0
, there is nothing to prove. Now assume the statement holds for all genera less than
g
, and proceed by (finite) induction on the codimension
c
. Let
c<g
, and assume that the statement holds for all codimensions less than
c
.

Taking the codimension
c
part of (
88
), the left hand side is zero. On the right hand side, we apply Theorem
5.11
to simplify the term
\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}}
as follows. Write
\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}}
as a linear combination of tautological classes of the form
[\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\widetilde{\delta}},\gamma_{0}]
. As in the proof of Theorem
5.11
, we express
\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}}
as the pushforward of tautological classes from compactified Jacobians of the form
\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}
(see (
60
)). By Proposition
5.8
, the class
\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}}
is supported away from the integral locus. Therefore, the stratum
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g-h,n+2h}
is nontrivial, and the Chern classes of the normal bundle can be written in terms of
\psi
- and
\xi
-classes at the additional markings on
\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}
. Hence, we have

\Big[\mathfrak{F}_{g}^{\circ}\Big(\exp(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i})\cdot[\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\widetilde{\delta}},\gamma_{0}]\Big)\Big]_{\mathrm{codim}=c}=j_{h*}q_{h}^{*}\Big[\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{g-h}\Big(\exp(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i})(j_{\Gamma_{\delta}})_{*}(\widetilde{\gamma}_{0})\Big)\Big]_{\mathrm{codim}=c-h}

(90)

for some
h\leq g
and some
\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}\in\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{\Gamma_{\delta}})
.

If
h^{1}(\Gamma)>0
, then (
90
) vanishes by Corollary
5.15
. When
h^{1}(\Gamma)=0
, the class
\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}
can be written as a polynomial in the
\psi
and
\xi
classes. Consider a class of the form
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{1}^{\prime}\cdot\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}
, where
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}
is a monomial in
\xi
-classes at markings, and
\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}
is a monomial in
\psi
-classes at markings. By Proposition
5.13
and the projection formula, we have:

\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{g-h}\Big(\exp(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i})(j_{\Gamma_{\delta}})_{*}(\gamma_{1})\Big)=(j_{\Gamma_{\delta}})_{*}\Big(\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}\cdot\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{g-h}\Big(\exp(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i})(\gamma_{1}^{\prime})\Big)\Big)\,.

The class inside
\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{g-h}
remains a polynomial in the
\xi
- and
\kappa_{0,1}
-classes, and the codimension
c-h-\deg(\gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime})-|E(\Gamma)|
part of
\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{g-h}(\cdots)
contributes to the codimension
c-h
part of (
90
). Since
\Gamma
is nontrivial, we have
|E(\Gamma)|\geq 1
, so the codimension
c-h
part vanishes by the induction hypothesis. Therefore, (
90
) vanishes.

By the above argument, the codimension
c
part of the right hand side of (
88
) reduces to the codimension
c
part of (
89
), with the following simplification:

\displaystyle\Big[(-1)^{g}\mathfrak{F}_{g}^{\circ}\Big(\exp(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i})\Big)\Big]_{\mathrm{codim}=c}

\displaystyle+\sum_{h=1}^{g}\frac{1}{h!2^{h}}j_{h*}q_{h}^{*}\Big[(-1)^{g-h}\mathfrak{F}_{g-h}^{\circ}\Big(\exp(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i})(1+\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}})\prod_{i=1}^{h}\sum_{k_{i}=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k_{i}}B_{2k_{i}}}{(2k_{i})!}\frac{\alpha_{i}^{2k_{i}-1}+\beta_{i}^{2k_{i}-1}}{\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i}}\Big)\Big]_{\mathrm{codim}={c-h}}\,.

By Proposition
4.6
and
5.8
,
\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}}
and
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}
can be expressed as polynomials in the
\psi_{i}
and
\xi_{i}
classes. Since
\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{g-h}
is linear on the base, we can factor out the
\psi
-classes. By the induction hypothesis, the second term vanishes. Thus, for
c<g
, we conclude:

[\mathfrak{F}_{g}^{\circ}(\exp(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i}))]_{\mathrm{codim}=c}=0\,,

which completes the proof of part (a).

(b) We prove part (b) by induction on the genus. Assume the statement holds for all genera less than
g
. Taking the codimension
g
part of (
88
), Proposition
8.2
implies:

[\mathsf{aj}_{b;\mathsf{a}}]|_{J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n}}=\mathsf{uniDR}^{g}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})|_{J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n}}\,.

(91)

By the argument in part (a), the contribution involving the class
\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}}
lies in codimension greater than
g
. Therefore, by the vanishing result of part (a), the codimension
g
part of the right hand side of equation (
89
) simplifies to:

\displaystyle\Big[(-1)^{g}\mathfrak{F}_{g}^{\circ}\Big(\exp(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i})\Big)\Big]_{\mathrm{codim}=g}

\displaystyle+\sum_{h=1}^{g}\frac{1}{h!2^{h}}j_{h*}q_{h}^{*}\Big[(-1)^{g-h}\mathfrak{F}_{g-h}^{\circ}\Big(\exp(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i})\prod_{i=1}^{h}\sum_{k_{i}=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k_{i}}B_{2k_{i}}}{(2k_{i})!}(2k_{i}-1)(\xi_{i}-\xi_{i}^{\prime})^{2k_{i}-2}\Big)\Big]_{\mathrm{codim}={g-h}}\,.

By the induction hypothesis, each lower-genus term becomes:

\displaystyle\Big[(-1)^{g-h}\mathfrak{F}_{g-h}^{\circ}\Big(\exp(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i})\prod_{i=1}^{h}\sum_{k_{i}=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k_{i}}B_{2k_{i}}}{(2k_{i})!}(2k_{i}-1)(\xi_{i}-\xi_{i}^{\prime})^{2k_{i}-2}\Big)\Big]_{\mathrm{codim}={g-h}}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\sum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{h}\in\mathbb{Z}}(\prod_{i=1}^{h}k_{i})\widetilde{\mathsf{uniDR}}^{g-h}_{g-h,n+2h}(b;\mathsf{a},k_{1},-k_{1},\ldots,k_{h},-k_{h})\,.

Here, the infinite sum is interpreted using the
\zeta
-function renormalization defined in (
66
).
Finally, by Theorem
6.6
, the codimension
g
part of the leading term of (
89
) becomes:

\big[(-1)^{g}\cdot\mathfrak{F}_{g}^{\circ}(\exp(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i}))\big]_{\mathrm{codim}=g}=\widetilde{\mathsf{uniDR}}^{g}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})

which completes the proof of (b).
∎

8.2
Proof of Theorem
1.1

We finally finish the proof of our main theorem.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

We finally finish the proof of our main theorem. We will induct on
\ell
, the exponent of
\Theta
. We save the base case
\ell=0
for the end, so first assume
\ell>0
. We are given
\epsilon
, a nondegenerate stability condition for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
. Let
p:\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n+1}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
be the morphism forgetting the last marking. The pullback of
\epsilon
to
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n+1}
is degenerate. However, there exists a small perturbation
\epsilon^{\prime}
which is a nondegenerate stability condition for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n+1}
such that there is a morphism
q:\overline{J}_{g,n+1}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\to\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}
and the following commutative diagram holds:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{g,n+1}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}\,.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

Consider the class (on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n+1}
)

\mathcal{P}^{c}(b;\mathsf{a},a_{n+1}):=(\pi_{1})_{*}\left[\Theta^{l-1}\exp\left(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=2}^{n+1}a_{i}\xi_{i}\right)\right]_{\text{codim }g+c},

viewed as a polynomial in
b,a_{2},\ldots,a_{n+1}
.

By the inductive hypothesis,
\mathcal{P}^{c}(b;\mathsf{a},a_{n+1})=0
for
c<g-\ell+1
, is tautological for all
c
, and for
c=g-\ell+1
we have the formula

\mathcal{P}^{g-\ell+1}(b;\mathsf{a},a_{n+1})=(-1)^{g-\ell+1}(\ell-1)!\cdot\mathsf{DR}_{g}^{g-\ell+1}(b,\mathsf{a},a_{n+1}).

(92)

Now consider applying
p_{*}
. Applying
p_{*}\circ(\pi_{1})_{*}=\pi_{*}\circ q_{*}
, the projection formula for
q
, as well as the identity
p^{*}\kappa_{0,1}=\kappa_{0,1}-\xi_{n+1}
, we get

p_{*}\mathcal{P}^{c}(b;\mathsf{a},a_{n+1})=\pi_{*}\left[\Theta^{l-1}\exp\left(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=2}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i}\right)\cdot p_{*}\exp((a_{n+1}-b)\xi_{n+1})\right]_{\text{codim }g+c-1}.

Since
p_{*}(\xi_{n+1}^{i})=0
for
i<2
, this pushforward is divisible by
(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}
. Moreover, if we divide by
(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}
and then set
a_{n+1}:=b
, the formula
p_{*}(\xi_{n+1}^{2}/2)=-\Theta
yields

\left[\frac{p_{*}\mathcal{P}^{c}(b;\mathsf{a},a_{n+1})}{(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}}\right]_{a_{n+1}:=b}=-\pi_{*}\left[\Theta^{l}\exp\left(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=2}^{n}a_{i}\xi_{i}\right)\right]_{\text{codim }g+c-1}.

This immediately gives the induction step for parts (a) and (c) of the theorem. For part (b), we also apply Theorem
7.1
to see that the steps we have taken (apply
p_{*}
, divide by
(a_{n+1}-b)^{2}
, set
a_{n+1}:=b
) indeed act on the right side of (
92
) by replacing
l-1
with
l
and
n+1
with
n
.

For the remainder of the proof we assume
\ell=0
. We prove (a) and (b) by induction on the genus. When
g=0
, there is nothing to prove. Consider the map

\mathfrak{F}_{g}^{\circ}:\mathsf{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon})\to\mathsf{CH}^{*}(J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n})

as defined in (
50
). Let
\mathsf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with
\sum_{i}a_{i}=0
. By Proposition
8.1
, it is sufficient to prove that

e^{*}\Big[\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{g}(\exp\big(\sum_{i}a_{i}\xi_{i})\big)\Big]_{\mathrm{codim}=g}=(-1)^{g}e^{*}\widetilde{\mathsf{uniDR}}^{g}_{g}(\mathsf{a})=(-1)^{g}\widetilde{\mathsf{DR}}_{g}^{g}(\mathsf{a})\,,

which is proven in Theorem
8.3
. This proves (a) and (b).

We now prove (c). By Proposition
8.1
and the above argument, it suffices to show that

\Big[\mathfrak{F}^{\circ}_{g}(\exp\big(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i}a_{i}\xi_{i})\big)\Big]_{\mathrm{codim}=c}\in\mathsf{R}^{c}(J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n})

(93)

for all codimension
c
, where
\mathsf{R}^{*}(J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n})
is the restriction of tautological ring (
11
) to
J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n}
.

We proceed by induction, first on the genus and then on the codimension. For the base case
g=0
, there is nothing to prove. Assume the statement holds for all genera less than
g
. For codimenions less than or equal to
g
, (
93
) was already established in (a) and (b). Now assume (
93
) holds in codimension less than
c
, where taking the codimension
c
part of (
88
), the left-hand side is zero. The right-hand side reduces to

\displaystyle\Big[(-1)^{g}\mathfrak{F}_{g}^{\circ}\Big(\exp(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i}a_{i}\xi_{i})\Big)\Big]_{\mathrm{codim}=c}+\Big[(-1)^{g}\mathfrak{F}_{g}^{\circ}\Big(\exp(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i}a_{i}\xi_{i})\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}}\Big)\Big]_{\mathrm{codim}=c}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\sum_{h=1}^{g}\frac{1}{h!2^{h}}j_{h*}q_{h}^{*}\Big[(-1)^{g-h}\mathfrak{F}_{g-h}^{\circ}\Big(\exp(-b\kappa_{0,1}+\sum_{i=1}a_{i}\xi_{i})(1+\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}})

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{h}\sum_{k_{i}=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k_{i}}B_{2k_{i}}}{(2k_{i})!}\frac{\alpha_{i}^{2k_{i}-1}+\beta_{i}^{2k_{i}-1}}{\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i}}\Big)\Big]_{\mathrm{codim}={c-h}}\,.

By the induction hypothesis, the second term and the third term lie in the tautological subring. Therefore (
93
) holds in codimension
c
, completing the proof of part (c).
∎

For the pushforward of monomials of divisors with
2\ell+m+\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}\leq 2g
, explicit formula of the class
\gamma_{b;\mathsf{a}}
does not play a role. When
2\ell+m+\sum_{i=2}^{n}k_{i}>2g
, the pushforward depends on the stability condition–which can be easily checked over the locus
\mathcal{M}^{\,\mathrm{ct}}_{g,n}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
of compact type curves.

Remark 8.4
.

Let
q:\overline{J}_{g,n+1}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\to\overline{J}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}
be the projection used in the proof of Theorem
1.1
. By Lemma
7.6
the same pushforward formula as in Theorem
7.7
holds for
q_{*}
. Therefore, a similar argument to that used in the proof of Theorem
1.1
(c) generalizes Theorem
8.3
for monomials with
\Theta
divisors.

Remark 8.5
.

A natural question arises as to whether the intersection numbers on compactified Jacobians possess a distinct structure. The generating series of the intersection numbers from double ramification formulae with
\psi
-monomials satisfy a noncommutative Korteweg-de Vries (KdV) hierarchy, as shown by Buryak and Rossi
[
14
]
. Therefore, Theorem
1.1
provides a partial answer to this question, though further investigation is required.

8.3
Connection to the perverse filtration

Let
\pi:\overline{J}_{C}\to B
be the projection. By the Decomposition Theorem
[
13
]
, the perverse truncation functor associated to
\pi
induces the canonical increasing
perverse filtation

P_{0}H^{*}(\overline{J}_{C},\mathbb{Q})\subset P_{1}H^{*}(\overline{J}_{C},\mathbb{Q})\subset\cdots\subset P_{2g}H^{*}(\overline{J}_{C},\mathbb{Q})=H^{*}(\overline{J}_{C},\mathbb{Q})\,.

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
\pi:M\to B
be a projective, flat morphism of relative dimension
g
, where
M
and
B
are proper and smooth. For a class
\alpha\in P_{2g-1}H^{*}(M,\mathbb{Q})
, we have
\pi_{*}(\alpha)=0
.

Proof.

Let
d=\dim B
and
\alpha\in H^{c}(M,\mathbb{Q})
. By the Poincaré duality on
B
, it suffices to show that
\pi_{*}(\alpha)\cup\beta=0
for all
\beta\in H^{2(g+d)-c}(B,\mathbb{Q})
. Since multiplying a cohomology class pulled back from the base does not alter the perverse degree, we have
\alpha\cup\pi^{*}(\beta)\in P_{2g-1}H^{2(g+d)}(M,\mathbb{Q})
. Let
\omega\in H^{2}(M,\mathbb{Q})
be a relative ample divisor. By the relative Hard Lefschetz
[
13
, Théorème 5.4.10]
, cupping with
\omega^{g}
induces an isomorphism of graded pieces:

-\cup\omega^{g}:\mathrm{gr}^{P}_{0}H^{2d}(M,\mathbb{Q})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{gr}^{P}_{2g}H^{2g+2d}(M,\mathbb{Q})\,.

(94)

Since
\pi^{*}:H^{2d}(B,\mathbb{Q})\cong\mathbb{Q}\to P_{0}H^{2d}(M,\mathbb{Q})
is nonzero, (
94
) implies that
P_{2g-1}H^{2(g+d)}(M,\mathbb{Q})=0
.
Thus
\alpha\cup\pi^{*}(\beta)=0
. By the projection formula, this implies
\pi_{*}(\alpha)\cup\beta=\pi_{*}(\alpha\cup\pi^{*}(\beta))=0
.
∎

We give a proof of Theorem
1.1
(a) in cohomology following the idea of Maulik-Shen
[
40
]
.

Proposition 8.7
.

Theorem
1.1
(a) holds in
H^{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n},\mathbb{Q})
.

Proof.

Let
\pi:\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
denote the projection. We use the notion of
strong perversity
as defined in
[
40
]
and show that
\xi_{i}
and
\kappa_{0,1}
have strong perversity 1. By
[
44
]
, the complex
R\pi_{*}\mathbb{Q}
has the full support property, meaning that any non-trivial simple perverse summand has support equal to
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
. Since
\xi_{i},\kappa_{0,1}\in H^{2}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{\epsilon},\mathbb{Q})
, it suffices to verify strong perversity being
1
over the open locus
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
. Over
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}
, the morphism
\pi
is a torsor under the abelian variety
J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n}
. Since
n\geq 1
, we can identify it with
J^{\underline{0}}_{g,n}
via twisting by a section. Under the twisting both
\xi_{i}
(resp.
\kappa_{0,1}
) maps to
\xi_{i}
(resp.
\kappa_{0,1}
) modulo
\psi
-class. Since
\psi
-class has perversity zero, pulled back from
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}
, it does not change the strong perversity. By
[
22
, Section 3]
,
\xi_{k}
and
\kappa_{0,1}
are weight
1
classes so they have strong perversity
1
over
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}
. Therefore
\xi_{k}
and
\kappa_{0,1}
satisfies strong perversity 1 over
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
.

The
\Theta
divisor, by codimension considerations, has strong perversity
2
. By
[
40
, Lemma 1.3]
, the strong perversity is multiplicative and hence the class
\Theta^{\ell}(\kappa_{0,1})^{m}\prod\xi_{i}^{k_{i}}
has strong perversity
2\ell+m+\sum k_{i}
. Applying
[
40
, Lemma 1.2]
, we conclude that the monomial lies in
(2\ell+m+\sum k_{i})
-th perverse filtation of
H^{*}(\overline{J}_{g,n},\mathbb{Q})
.
Since
2\ell+m+\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}<2g
, the pushforward to
H^{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n},\mathbb{Q})
vanishes by Lemma
8.6
.
∎

The independence of stability condition of Theorem
1.1
(b) in rational cohomology is reminiscent of the “
\chi
-independence” phenomenon (
[
39
]
). Following
[
8
, Section 0.3.3]
, we consider the strata algebra
\mathrm{PicS}_{g,n}
associated with
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n}
. This is a
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra freely generated by tautological classes. Given a choice of universal line bundle, there exists a realization map
\rho:\mathrm{PicS}_{g,n}\to H^{*}(\overline{J}^{\epsilon}_{g,n},\mathbb{Q})
. Let
\Xi=\Theta^{\ell}(\kappa_{0,1})^{m}\xi_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots\xi_{n}^{k_{n}}\in\mathrm{PicS}_{g,n}
with
2\ell+m+\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}\leq 2g
. For two different choices of realizations
